I
PROLOGUE
N the early spring of 1850 a little girl sat on a
sun-warmed stone beside a clump of pussy wil-
lows, gazing far off beyond the hillside road that led
to town. The air was warm and fragrant. White
clouds were drifting lazily above. To the child who
sat there, dreamy eyes on a blue horizon, the whole
earth seemed, for a time, to have ceased breathing.
It was very still so still that she heard the tinkle of
a cow-bell; and from below in the valley (which
looked like a green bowl set deep among those
Massachusetts hills) the chug, chug, chug of a freight
train was faintly audible, distance lending enchant-
ment to the sound, and to its smoke, which rose, a
feathery veil against the sky.
The little girl stretched out a hand as if to feel the
sunshine. How warm it was! The dandelions would
soon be out! A bluebird, lighting for a moment on
the very tip of a young savin tree, surveyed the world
with evident approval, lifted his wings as one would
lift a gay, blue parasol, and was away again, bent on
that glorious adventure which is Life.
Watching him, the child drew in a quick breath of
happiness-extraordinary happiness. It was as if, al-
most, she shared the ecstasy of that joyous flight. The
Ibird was gone, but he had left something beautiful
and lasting in her heart-something she would
remember.
...
"Some day," she murmured, as a sudden west wind
stirred the treetops, "I'll fly away like the bluebird.
I'll see all the things in the story books: Kings and
Queens, maybe; and prairies, and rivers, and shining
mountains....”
Little did this small Mary Alden dream that ten
years later, seated in an ox-drawn prairie schooner,
she was to gaze out over the endless Kansas plains
and behold, strangely, a valley cupped in New Eng-
land hills a bluebird perched on the tip of a young
savin-and feel again the sense of joyous ecstasy
which filled the heart of that little girl who had
been herself. For this was Adventure! This was Life!
Was she not really seeing those prairies, and rivers,
and shining mountains?CHAPTER I
T had been a long, hard year. As yet the country
had not recovered from the panic of 1857; nor
had Luther Alden regained even a modicum of cour-
age since the bank at Worcester was swept away, tak-
ing with it all that he had to show for twenty years
of toil on his hillside farm. In those winter evenings
after the chores were done and a simple supper eaten
in the old-fashioned kitchen, the discouraged man
would slump into his mother's rush-bottom arm-
chair and sit until bedtime, brooding in silence.
The habit of watching her husband covertly as she
busied herself about the never-ended household tasks
grew upon Persis Alden during that dreary time. He
did not have to tell her that he was worrying about
what the future held in store for them-wondering,
too, if when another autumn came their daughter
Mary (the very apple of her father's eye) would re-
ceive the coveted appointment as teacher of the dis-
trict school in an adjoining village, or be obliged to
"work out" for some farmer's wife more fortunate
than themselves.
Either alternative seemed a calamity to Luther
Alden, because Mary would leave home-and what,
indeed, would the old house be without her? Perhaps,
he mused, a bitter line tightening his lips, perhaps itwould have been better for all concerned had he
joined his brother Daniel in a search for gold in the
Rocky Mountains; yet at the time this had seemed
merely another of Dan's wild adventures.
For Daniel Alden was the D'Artagnan of the
family. At twelve years old he started out for school
one morning, and not until five years had passed did
his people lay eyes on him again. To be sure, a letter
postmarked Boston arriving some two weeks after the
lad's disappearance, allayed the anxiety of his widowed
mother; but it adroitly omitted any address, and
months went by before she learned that her "wander-
ing boy" had stowed away on a clipper ship bound
for the East Indies out of Salem harbor.
During those years occasional letters had drifted
back to the isolated farmhouse in Massachusetts, for
Daniel Alden possessed "the pen of a ready writer."
And then one bitter night when a New England bliz-
zard was doing its raging best to snow them in, the
door opened quietly and their soldier of fortune made
his dramatic entrance.
Luther Alden had only to shut his eyes in order
to see his scapegrace brother standing as if blown
there by the storm, his back against that hastily closed
door. He was no longer the little lad who had run
away one bright spring morning, but a tall, slim,
handsome stripling, his blue eyes dancing with merri-
ment as he said: "Hello, folks!" and caught his
mother in a bear-like hug.
It was, Luther observed shrewdly, the old, old tale
of the returning prodigal. In those brief weeks ofhis brother's stay the "fatted calf' was indeed slaugh-
tered, and cherished preserves brought up from the
cellar with ruthless disregard of the lean months
ahead. Even Persis, Luther's bride of less than a
year, would sit spellbound at Daniel Alden's feet,
listening to romantic stories of foreign ports-sniffing
imaginary spices-visioning those "far-off faëry isles
of the sea" with dreamy eyes. And never, mused
Luther in new-born bitterness, had his mother's face
lighted with such transparent joy at sight of her
elder son who had stayed at home to shoulder the
burden laid down by his dead father.
Possibly Daniel Alden, who was an understanding
soul, sensed something of his brother's attitude on
this first visit. More than once he surprised a wistful
expression in Luther's eyes which set him pondering;
and one night when he attempted to try his hand at
milking, and old Molly, indignant at his unskilful
efforts, switched an angry tail across his face, the
boy exploded: "Gosh, Lute! I don't see how you
stand it!"
Luther arose to lay a soothing hand on the cow's
flank.
"Stand what?" he questioned.
"Everything here," responded Daniel, his arm
flung out in a wide, expressive gesture. "These eternal
chores that are never finished. The stink of this hor-
rible old cow barn. The deadly sameness. Don't you
ever want to kick over the traces, Lute? See some-
thing besides these everlasting hills? Be something be-
sides a plodding New England farmer?"Quick anger sprang into the elder brother's eyes
as he retorted: "Without the plodders, who would
provide for our mothers when they grow old, Dan?"
There was silence for a moment in the dusky barn;
then: "I see," said Daniel, a flush deepening the color
of his sun-burned face. "You think me a slacker-a
ne'er-do-well. I suppose it's true, in a way; though
the idea never occurred to me before. But I can't
stay on and help you here, brother. Not after the life
I've lived these last five years. In six months I'd
probably blow up. In a year I'd murder the whole
family! Call me a tramp, if you must, but no rolling
stone. I've got two hundred dollars stowed in my dun-
nage. I meant but that doesn't matter now. I'll
leave it with you, Lute; and there'll be more like it
from time to time. I promise"
"I don't want your money," broke in Luther
gruffly. "Give it to Mother."
Dan's hand went to his brother's shoulder with the
self-same gesture Luther had used to pacify the cow.
He smiled, and no one had ever resisted Dan Alden's
smile.
"If I give it to Mother," he alleged with sagacious
truth, "she'll stow it away in a cracked teapot and
try to return it next time I blow in. Better not let
her know you've got it, Lute; but spend it to make
life easier for all of you. And don't get the impression
that I'm being a good boy now," he added, grinning.
"Don't you see that I'm merely buying my peace of
mind?"
But Luther Alden saw more than that. He said ashe gripped his brother's hand: "All right, Danny. I
understand. A little help now and then will take a
load off my shoulders; and and I can do more for
Persis. Clear out now, you clumsy farm hand! I've
got to milk this cow."
Be it said for the adventurer that he was as good
as his word. Sometimes the remittances which drifted
in from far places were long in coming; but while his
mother lived Daniel Alden did not forget. After her
death his occasional visits grew even less frequent.
Indeed, Luther had heard from him only at Christ-
mas time for three years, when in the spring of 1858
Dan put in an appearance, thrilled, it transpired, by
tales of gold in the Rocky Mountains.
"Why, man, you've only to take a pick and dig it
out!" he cried, when the elder brother urged caution
in such an undertaking. And a week later he was
away again, taking with him two venturous youths
from the village in the valley below them-promis-
ing to send Mary his first nugget-pausing at a turn
in the road to wave his hat in a last gay, character-
istic gesture and shout a challenge to those silent
hills: "Pike's Peak or bust!"M
CHAPTER II
ARY ALDEN never forgot that parting. She
stood in the doorway long after the nomad
uncle was lost to view, her heart stirred by a singular
unrest.
"Uncle Daniel is like a March wind, Mother," she
said dreamily. "A clean March wind blowing through
a house that's been closed all winter. It must be won-
derful to spend your life riding from one adventure
to another. How still everything seems now that he's
gone."
Her mother drew nearer to the door, and with sur-
prise Mary saw that there were tears trembling on
her lashes.
"I know," she answered, dashing away those tell-
tale signs of weakness with an impatient hand. "It's
always so when your uncle leaves us. There have
been times when his visits seemed like a cup of cold
water in a desert-to me, Mary. I recall so clearly
his first home-coming. It was in winter, and I'd been
a bit lonely here on the farm, being used to village
life before I married. Dan was only a lad, but what
marvelous stories he had to tell in those long, cold
evenings! When he left I felt that part of me went
with him. I remember weeping, and how my tears
distressed your father. I think he was jealous of Dan-iel because your grandmother made so much of him
in those days."
"Did she love Uncle Danny best?" asked Mary,
eyes still on the winding road below.
"They were both her boys, and she loved each in
his own fashion," responded Persis. "If she showed
more affection for your Uncle Dan, it was only be-
cause he showed more for her. Your father loves
deeply, Mary, but can't express it; while Daniel is
not ashamed to tell people that he cares for them.
Many's the time I've wished your father had some of
his brother's happy-go-lucky spirit; but how could
he-poor darling!-shouldering a man's burdens
when he was still a boy?"
Although Daniel Alden had promised to write
often of his wanderings, not until some time in June
did a letter straggle back, written at Leavenworth,
Kansas, on the last day of the preceding month. It
was addressed to Mary, and she read it aloud that
evening, glancing up surreptitiously from time to
time in hopes of catching her father unaware, and,
possibly, reading the thoughts he would not speak.
"Dear Niece Mary," wrote the gold seeker. "No
doubt you are wondering if your scatter-brained
uncle has met the last of his adventures at the hands
of unfriendly savages, but such is far from the truth.
Since my departure from Massachusetts with my two
young companions, the days have been both full and
exciting-so much so that when evening fell I found
myself too weary to do justice to the scene by letter.Yet not knowing what lies ahead of us on these vast
prairies, I dare not set forth to-morrow without send-
ing word of our welfare to my long-suffering family.
"There is not sufficient time to dwell on the trip
from Massachusetts here; but I wish I could make
you live with me these days in Leavenworth which
are fast drawing to a close. The greatest excitement
has prevailed since one James Keith rode into town
from the West on a footsore pony, bearing a quill
filled with gold dust. Far into the night street cor-
ners have been crowded with groups of men eagerly
discussing plans, routes, equipment. Bloody tales of
the Indians make many hesitate to start. Possible
spring blizzards along the trails-uncertainty about
firewood etc., all tend to deter the chicken-hearted.
Yet despite these dangers a goodly company is
equipped and we start at day-break, bound for the
Rockies, or Shining Mountains, as the traders and
trappers named them long ago.
"We intend to follow the valley of the Arkansas
River and the Fountaine-qui-Bouille, making per-
haps twenty miles a day-not resting on the Sabbath
if one member of the party has aught to say about
it! We are indeed a curious assembly, not all in
search of gold by any means. There are four business
men: land speculators, hoping to make a fortune by
promoting town sites and selling groceries. There
are two courageous young women, one with a babe
less than a year old, who are leaving the comforts of
civilization and bravely accompanying their men into
the wilderness. But the gold seekers predominate, ofcourse. We number nineteen, all told; and will no
doubt join other parties whose routes cross ours.
"One word more before I cease, and this for my
brother. Why not consider joining this great migra-
tion, Luther? What has life on those rocky New Eng-
land hillsides given you, except a backache? And who
knows what glorious adventure may lie beyond this
boundless, wind-swept prairie? Think of it, won't
you? Dream of it when the blizzards of winter shut
you in. It is never too late to make another start in
life, brother; and I swear I saw the light of the pio-
neers in Mary's eyes when I came away. Let the girl
decide it. Let Persis. I know their decisions without
asking. Build you a covered wagon during the com-
ing winter, and set forth like hundreds of others in
the spring. After all, you have only one life to live,
brother and have you ever lived it? . . ."
If Mary Alden, eager to see those rivers and moun-
tains of her childhood's dream, hoped that this plea
would stir her father to the point of exchanging his
ancestral acres, unyielding though they were, for an
uncertainty, she was to be disappointed. In truth,
Luther had never regarded his runaway brother as a
fount of wisdom; nor was it in his nature to act im-
pulsively. Thus another uneventful year was to drift
by, a year so lean that the man grew even more appre-
hensive of the future, before two happenings caused
him to wonder if, after all, the family vagabond might
not be right.
One was Mary's long-promised nugget: a small,irregular lump of yellow metal from Cherry Creek,
which reached the hillside farm in the spring of
1859, almost a year after Daniel Alden had started
West. This nugget held a curious fascination for the
elder brother. Often of an evening he would take it
from the box behind the clock (where Persis had
hidden it for safe keeping), and turn the gold idly
in his gnarled fingers, his eyes centered on something
far away.
The other fateful happening which occurred that
summer was the arrival of a copy of the New York
Tribune, sent to Persis by a cousin in Connecticut
who knew that Daniel was among the gold seekers.
A cloud lifted from Luther's troubled spirit as he
read the story. This gold then, was no myth-no hal-
lucination of his brother's credulous brain. The nug-
get (which he had vaguely distrusted) must actually
have been dug from some river bed-perhaps after
drifting down from a mountain gulley rich with
gold. It was all true. The famous Horace Greeley
himself had been there had washed a pan of the
precious dust with his own hands! Incredible figures
danced before Luther's eyes: "First day $66.
second day $80.
fourth $305. Have sold
their claim for $2,500....”
...
To the hard-worked farmer who had not saved
any such sum in twenty years, that copy of the New
York Tribune read like a fairy tale, and stirred some
long dormant urge within his blood. Even the words
that followed these lurid figures could not stifle it.
For Greeley showed his common sense by adding:"There are said to be five thousand people already
in this ravine, and hundreds are pouring into it
daily. For all these nearly every pound of pro-
visions and supplies of every kind must be hauled
by teams from the Missouri River, some seven hun-
dred miles distant, over roads which are mere trails.
...
...
Part of this distance is desert. To attempt to
cross it on foot is madness-suicide-murder. To
cross it in teams in midsummer when the water-
courses are dry and the grass is eaten up, is possible
only for those who know where to look for wood
and water. A few months hence, probably by
the middle of October, this whole region will be
snowed under-frozen up. There then for a
period of six months, will be neither employment,
food nor shelter within five hundred miles for the
thousands pressing hither under the delusion that
gold may be picked up like pebbles on the
seashore...."
...
Strange, perhaps, that this warning did not stran-
gle the rising urge in a man as conservative as Luther
Alden. Not that he did not try to fight it. Going
about his accustomed tasks, mowing the scanty crop
of grass in his west lot; gathering wind-falls beneath
dying trees in the old orchard; storing away squashes,
carrots, parsnips against winter, Luther marveled at
the desire which now possessed him. What business
had he, a man of forty-six, to harbor dreams of high
adventure? That was for striplings like those who
had followed his brother into the wilderness. Yet the
longing to join that cavalcade of covered wagonsoften swept over the harassed farmer with a force
that was well nigh irresistible.
Not once did he speak of what seemed his "weak-
ness," but on a late September morning Mary met
him coming from "the high pasture" bearing an arm-
ful of tough young hickories.
"For what?" she asked, as her father threw his bur-
den onto the barn floor.
"I thought maybe I'd fuss over 'em come winter,"
evaded Luther, and turned away before she could
question more.
But not for nothing had Mary Alden listened with
rapt attention to the enthralling words of her Uncle
Dan. She recalled an evening when he described
the making of a covered wagon. Young hickories, it
seemed, must be dressed down and bent into the
great bows which, covered with canvas, transformed
a plain farm wagon into a prairie schooner. Could
it be possible that her father was considering such
a step? Had that small gold nugget from Cherry
Creek spoken more eloquently than Daniel? The
girl's heart quickened with sudden, joyous anticipa-
tion as she went to help her mother with the noon-
day meal.B
CHAPTER III
EFORE the oaks in the high pasture showed
Indian red, Persis Alden knew that her daugh-
ter's surmise on that September morning had been
true. Perhaps, she pondered, the time was ripe for
a change, and the very fingering of that nugget from
Cherry Creek had stirred something within her hus-
band so strong that despite the valiant struggle he
was making, it would sweep him out of a rut grown
deep with years.
"But you're not to question him," the understand-
ing woman insisted, when Mary grew impatient at
her father's silence. "Let him dwell on the idea-
fight his own fight-face the knowledge of how little
he can accomplish on this old farm. Just now I doubt
not the poor dear is calling himself a fool; but he'll
see things differently as time passes. And if we urged
him to take any such step and things went wrong,
daughter, could we ever forgive ourselves? Be patient.
Some day your father will tell us what is on his
mind."
This came about almost as Persis prophesied. It
was a rainy November night; and while Mary fash-
ioned a bonnet out of old material, her mother sat
close by, busy with a darning needle and Luther's
socks. Each seemed occupied with her own thoughts,and a sense of quiet pervaded the square east room
where they spent their evenings until bitter cold
drove them nearer the kitchen range. Huge logs of
apple wood blazed on the hearth; but above their
crackling one could hear the branches of a tall white
lilac bush, slapping against a windowpane as the wind
rose.
"I wonder where Uncle Daniel is to-night," said
Mary, after a long silence.
Her father, seated beside the hearth, cracking hick-
ory nuts on an old flatiron and dropping them into
a yellow mixing bowl, raised eyes that had been
seeing pictures in the fire.
"What brought that thought into your head,
daughter?" he questioned, subtly startled.
"The rain," she answered. "I wondered if it were
storming near Cherry Creek, and if Uncle Dan was
safe in the little cabin he planned to build when we
last heard."
"More likely he's lying in a tent, with an empty
stomach and snow drifting down his neck for com-
fort," opined Luther, almost as if trying to persuade
himself that this was true.
The girl laughed; and even Persis smiled as she
responded: "What is a little snow to your brother
Daniel, who has climbed the slippery masts of the
Flying Cloud, during a storm at sea? And you know
yourself, Luther, that the cabin built by that land
promoter, which Dan described in his last letter, was
snug as possible. He even mentioned a muslin table-
cloth, and a volume of Dickens on the window sill!He said also that they had venison, beans, and cakes
for dinner. Does that sound like an empty stomach?
It's a rough life, of course; but I've no doubt it is
comfort personified to an adventurous soul like
Daniel Alden."
This was a long speech from Persis, and Luther
glanced at her in some surprise before observing:
"That sounds almost as if you'd like to try it for
yourself, Mother."
"And why should I not?" countered his wife
briskly. "Am I an old woman to hug the fireside? I
love these hills, my dear, because they're home to
me; but in all my forty years I have seen nothing
else. Did you never thrill at your brother's stories
of ships, and seas, and foreign ports, Luther? Come
now, own up! Aren't there times when you feel like
a galley slave?"
"Do you?” he questioned, and set his flatiron upon
the hearth.
Persis Alden threw back her head and laughed.
She had even teeth and, unlike many women of her
day, had taken care of them. They gleamed now in
the soft lamplight, while Luther secretly admired
them, though it never would have occurred to him
to tell her so.
"Sometimes I do," she answered with sturdy hon-
esty, her laugh taking all sting from the admission.
"Many a morning when cowslips were budding by
the brook and violets opening their eyes, Luther,
I've yearned to tear off my apron and run away from
the kitchen range and all those blackened pots andpans that have stared me in the face for twenty years;
and I've no doubt you've felt the same come warm
spring weather."
...
An understanding smile brightened the man's som-
ber face as he responded: "For lo! the winter is
past the flowers appear upon the earth; the
time for the singing of birds has come, and the voice
of the turtle is heard in the land.' Yes, I'll admit
that spring stirs something within us, Persis, but we
must stifle it if"
"And why,” broke in Persis Alden with a rare
touch of exasperation, "should we stifle a desire to
gaze upon God's miracles? Must life be nothing but
hard work, Luther? Why, even the Good Book tells
us that 'man shall not live by bread alone.'!"
"And is that all I have given you these twenty
years, my dear?"
The woman laughed again; but it was a laugh with
tears behind it, and rising, she went closer to her
husband, laying her hand upon his shoulder in a
light caress.
"Come! come!" There was a gentle gaiety in her
voice. "I know what's been troubling you these many
weeks, dear. You have been thinking that Daniel has
played all the years you've been working, and has
almost as much to show for it in the end. You're
fighting an urge to join him even now-to try your
luck with all those gold seekers. You're wondering
if the hardships of such a journey would be too much
for me, or for Mary"
"And all the time," broke in their daughter, toss-ing aside the unfinished bonnet as she arose, “I'm
fairly wild to start!"
Looking up into the girl's shining eyes her father
winced a little.
"Have you no feeling then, for the home where
you were born, and your father and grandfather
before you?" he reproved her.
"Don't be a goose, darling! I love every stick and
stone of the old farm. But we needn't sell it, need
we, Father? And if things aren't what we hope, we
can come back. Just think how wonderful it would
be to see new places that great, wide, wind-swept
prairie-those mountains glittering in the clear sun-
light that Uncle Daniel writes about. The very
thought of such a journey thrills me through and
through! Why shouldn't we go?"
"There are merciless savages roving those 'wind-
swept plains' that sound so romantic from a dis-
tance," Luther reminded her.
"True,” put in Persis, who had resumed her seat
and was plying the darning needle again, "but per-
haps if we treat them kindly they will respond in a
like spirit."
"Are you, too, against me, Mother?" questioned
the man, turning to her.
There was silence while Persis rolled the last pair
of socks into a tidy ball. Then she replied: "Against
you? No. I am for you heart and soul, Luther, as I
always have been. But when we were young I saw
you bear burdens too heavy for your shoulders-
watched care sweep away your youth with ruthlesshands, taking part of my own youth with it. And
now a chance has arisen to give it back to you-to
us, I should say. Don't tell me that the spirit of
adventure is dead within you, for I know better."
"But-but if something should happen if you or
Mary"
"That is a chance we must take," she told him,
with all the bravery of a pioneer. "And don't forget,
Luther, that things happen here as well. Did not your
father fall dead from a bolt of lightning, right in his
own pasture, and my Uncle Thomas drown during
a spring freshet in the valley he had never left in
forty years? It's not always the adventurous souls who
meet with an untimely end, my dear. But there! I
won't urge you to make a change against your will;
and whatever you finally decide, Luther, I shall be
content."
"But I shall not!"
At the girl's voice her parents looked up, startled,
for not often did Mary assert herself with so much
vigor. "What does life here hold in store for me?"
she went on passionately. "I'll tell you, if you are too
blind to see. Year after year of teaching in District
School-boarding around, an unwelcome guest in
the homes of my pupils dressing on winter morn-
ings in an unheated room-hurrying to school with
ears and fingers freezing, and then like as not, having
to build a fire in the stove myself. Is it an alluring
prospect at nineteen, Father? Can't you see why I
long to do more thrilling things? I am young and
strong; and after all" (she glanced up, meeting theirtroubled eyes with laughter), "I am the niece of
your brother Daniel, am I not?"
"But you are my daughter!"
Luther Alden had risen, and stood looking down
at her, his face brightening as he repeated proudly:
"You are my daughter, and I shall not fail you.
Come out to the barn, Mary, and help me bring in
a load of hickories."L
CHAPTER IV
ONG as she lived, that winter stood out in Mary
Alden's memory as the shortest and most excit-
ing of her life-exciting because the great adventure
lay just ahead and the thrill of anticipation stirred
her blood-short because there seemed scant time in
which to prepare for this amazing journey.
Before Thanksgiving Luther had made a trip to
Worcester, where many yards of stout white canvas
and a brand new "dead-axle" wagon were purchased,
the unwelcome financial outlay being deemed essen-
tial to the success of their undertaking. None of them
ever forgot the day when this important arrival was
wheeled into the big barn to be admired not only by
themselves, but by a score of "neighbors” who had
climbed the hill in order to view the plain farm
wagon which was to be transformed into that vehicle
of romance, a prairie schooner.
It was then that the real work began, and the New
England farmer proved himself a clever carpenter.
Along both sides above the wagon box he built wide
shelves extending over the wheels, to make beds for
the travelers. At either end were contrived cupboards
suitable for dishes or clothing; the door of one doing
double duty, since it could be let down to provide
a table where they might eat if overtaken by badweather. And while her father labored at remodel-
ing the new wagon, Mary spent hours heating and
pouring water over the dressed hickories until those
tough, pliant slats were slowly bent over carefully
driven stakes, resembling an enormous letter U.
Thus there came a day when these long hickory
bows were set in place, and Persis brought forth her
own handiwork: the canvas cover which was to con-
vert their wagon into a snug house on wheels. Not
until then did the woman quite believe she was to
be a part of that great western-bound migration, the
thought of which had thrilled her long, long before
the arrival of Mary's golden nugget from Cherry
Creek. Indeed, there were moments during those
weeks of preparation when Persis felt that she
scarcely knew her husband-had, perhaps, never pen-
etrated his inmost thoughts. For her chance proph-
ecy seemed to be coming true; and the youth of
which Luther had been robbed by cares and worry,
showed in his quickened step, his brightened eyes.
Even the man's voice held something which Persis
had never heard there something vaguely reminis-
cent of his brother Daniel's. And if at times, looking
off at the familiar hills, her courage waned a little,
she had only to go into the old barn and stand for
a while watching her husband's eager preparations
for the expedition, to be reassured.
For, strangely perhaps, once the momentous de-
cision had been made, Luther Alden did not look
back; and his pleasure in the task at hand would
have been contagious, had contagion been needed tolure his wife and daughter away from the unyielding
farm.
"That prairie schooner is to Father what my old
doll house was to me when I was seven," laughed
Mary; while Persis answered, a smile curving her
lips:
"You're right, daughter. It's a beloved toy to him
-almost the only toy he has ever owned. Why, as
a little lad your father never had a Christmas tree,
and once trimmed up his mother's broom as a sub-
stitute! It seems incredible when they were sur-
rounded by these wooded hills where Christmas trees
could be had just for the cutting; but Grandma
Alden was a hard-worked widow, Mary, and gave
little thought to the needs of her children so long
as they were fed and clothed."
"Poor Father!" sighed Mary. "I did not realize how
much more care-free my childhood was than his. It
seems curious that he cares so much for this old
farm."
"It's home to him, child; but for the time being
he has forgotten the wrench he'll feel at parting from
it. And I sha'n't remind him. It does me good to see
him so engrossed."
Yet in truth, Luther Alden had not forgotten that
parting; nor did Persis suspect the problem that
stared her husband in the face as he worked long
hours at his new, congenial task. For both women
took it for granted that Bess and Dolly, the stout
farm horses who seemed almost a part of the Alden
family, were to propel them westward; while Luthernot only realized that the journey would be exhaust-
ing for those gentle creatures, but that they would
require grain in quantities too heavy to carry on
such an expedition.
Oxen were needed, two yoke of oxen, for they
could subsist on pasturage along the way. Yet the
cost of such beasts would mount, perhaps, to as
much as one hundred and fifty dollars; and already
Luther's slender nest egg had sadly dwindled. Thus
as he fitted neat shelves into the new wagon, the man
wondered if he could find it in his heart to sell those
ancestral acres which seemed so much a part of him
-burning his bridges with a ruthless hand. He
played with the thought-winced at it-decided in
brave moments that it was the thing to do-repudi-
ated the idea a hundred times; and then, in answer
to this persistent question, came another letter from
Daniel Alden, addressed to Mary and containing not
only welcome instructions for the journey they were
contemplating, but a draft on a St. Louis bank for
two hundred dollars.
This missive was over-long on its way, being
dated late in the previous July; and Luther stopped
work upon the prairie schooner while his daughter,
perched on a tool bench, her full skirts billowing
about her slender ankles, read it aloud:
"Dear Niece Mary: Think not from the postmark
that I have lost faith in these 'diggings' and turned
my back on the Shining Mountains. Not I! This goes
by the hand of a trusted friend, who leaves DenverCity in a few days by the Leavenworth City and
Pike's Peak stage coach, the first lap of the journey
to St. Louis, where he aspires to form a company
with which he will return next spring, bringing his
young wife with him to found a home in this new
country.
"I am entrusting to him a quill of gold dust to be
exchanged for a draft on some good bank, which he
will enclose in this letter. For I am hoping that your
desire (as well as your dear mother's) to see this
wonderful portion of our country, has by this time
overcome my brother's circumspection, and that even
now you are making plans to join me here before
another year has drifted by.
"If this be the case, I beg that you use the enclosed
for the purchase of two yoke of oxen. Judging from
all reports, there is no safer way to cross the plains.
But if your father still prefers to end his days where
he began them, it is my earnest desire that you and
your mother spend this money for some 'extrava-
gance' a trip, perhaps, to the great city of New
York-or Washington-something you will remem-
ber and dream about when you are old and gray,
though that, I admit, is a difficult thing for even my
active imagination to picture, dear Mary!
"Many exciting happenings have taken place here
since I last wrote you, one of the most thrilling (to
me!) being the arrival of letters from Massachusetts.
The long, finely written pages with which you were
so thoughtful as to remember your absent uncle,
have been read till there is little left of them, despitehis active existence which starts at daybreak and lasts
until he is too fatigued to keep awake.
"Perhaps one of our most interesting events was a
visit in early June from the great Horace Greeley,
of whose adventures you have no doubt read in the
New York Tribune. I was busily at work in Gregory
Gulch (which yielded the gold dust I am sending)
when I glanced up, and, like many another fellow
miner, dropped my pick to stare at an approaching
figure mounted upon a mule, and holding tight to
the creature's neck as if he feared momentarily to
be unseated.
"This rider was wearing a battered, white felt hat,
and his visage was very nearly obscured by plasters
adhering in various directions across his face, which
gave him a sinister expression. I learned later that
a troop of friendly Indians, attracted, no doubt, by
the bright red paint of the new stage coach on which
Greeley had secured passage, had swooped down
upon them, thereby frightening the mules which
shied into a gulley, upsetting the coach and causing
much minor discomfort to the passengers, not to
mention their fright.
"The great man and his friends (Villard and Rich-
ardson, newspaper men from Cincinnati and Boston)
seemed much impressed as they picked their way
amid a confusion of tents, wagons, horses and oxen,
not to mention the miners themselves, who set to
work again when they discovered that the strange
arrival was a mere man!
"That evening, despite his evident weariness,Greeley consented to make an open-air address which
several thousand men attended the first mass meet-
ing in the Rocky Mountains. Some of us feared a
disturbance from the many Southerners in the
Gulch, since they despise the man because of his
views on anti-slavery; but I am glad to report that
no such demonstration took place, and that the ques-
tion of slavery was not mentioned, though some were
disgruntled when Greeley mounted his 'cold water
hobby' and rode it hard, urging the men to live as
their wives and children left at home would desire.
"It was a picturesque scene, lighted only by pine
torches after darkness fell; and I wish you could have
beheld the grandeur of the surrounding mountains,
showing with weird distinctness in the clear atmos-
phere, and have heard the distant songs of men
encamped in the upper part of this wild valley.
"But enough, or this long screed will tire you;
though I must say that the Gulch is already a dif-
ferent place than when Greeley visited us. Pine trees
have come down to make way for streets. There are
stores, hotels, saloons" (this, and another uglier word
had been cautiously scratched out, but Mary's sharp
eyes deciphered them!), "express office, and printing
office. We number lawyers and doctors among us;
and are now known as Mountain City, and can boast
over two hundred dwellings.
"Only a few words more, but the most important.
If, as I hope, you intend to venture into the 'wilder-
ness,' come well prepared if you sell the farm to do
so. Buy a new wagon-new, stout canvas for a cover.The prairie winds would make short work of old
material. Bring all the foodstuffs you can pack away.
Prices here are appalling, flour bringing as high as
forty dollars a hundred pounds-eggs a dollar a
dozen-fresh vegetables are worth their weight in
gold; and dried fruit is our greatest luxury.
"Lastly-Do not attempt to cross the plains before
the first of May, after which pasturage for your oxen
will be fresh, and blizzards unlikely. And when reach-
ing Leavenworth let no one persuade you to take
the shorter trails, one of which follows the treacher-
ous Smoky Hill fork of the Kansas River-the other
the Republican fork. I should advise going as we
did, by the Santa Fé, though large numbers are now
reported as taking the Overland Trail as far as the
South Platte. In either case you will undoubtedly
fall in with other gold seekers so that you will not
lack company along the way. Indians may bother
you by begging or stealing; but I have found them
a not unfriendly people when treated fairly; though
many, I am forced to admit, are cruel and treach-
erous almost beyond belief.
"And now (if you have reached thus far in this
tedious epistle), I once more entreat my brother
Luther to strike off the fetters he has worn so long
and so patiently, and, before it is too late, taste the
freedom of the adventurer-the pioneer. Possibly
long ere this letter reaches New England I will have
received word of your proposed departure. Thus,
until we meet again under these clear skies, I remain
your affectionate brother and uncle, Daniel."T
CHAPTER V
HIS letter from his brother lifted a load
from Luther Alden's shoulders. Things moved
swiftly thereafter. To the over-conscientious New
Englander it was an immeasurable relief to realize
that he need not part with the old homestead-that
if no fortune was to be dug out of those mountain
gulches Daniel had pictured, he could bring his dear
ones back to the home acres, no worse off-God
willing-than they had been before.
And while the man worked away with renewed
courage, his wife and daughter were no less occu-
pied. For Persis was a typical New England house-
wife, and, of course, her home should be left in
"apple-pie order"! Then, too, suitable clothing must
be contrived, every stitch of which was set by hand
with the painstaking care of those whose garments
must last for months, possibly years to come. It was a
never-to-be-forgotten day when the two women drove
to Worcester behind old Bess, and, after a lengthy
perusal of Godey's Lady's Book, purchased material
necessary for their wardrobes.
"Get something good," Luther admonished them.
"No knowing when there'll be an opportunity to re-
plenish wearing apparel in that wild country. Don't
stint yourselves."But despite this unprecedented advice, Persis was
frugal in her spending, the life-long habit of mak-
ing a penny do double work, being something not
easily relinquished when one is forty.
For herself she chose a gray, light-weight wool, as
well as a good stout gingham in black and white-
both suitable for one of middle age. At first she
frowned upon her daughter's choice of poplin in red
and green Scotch plaid; but when the salesman threw
a fold of the material across Mary's shoulder, and her
mother saw how charmingly it set off the girl's dark
hair and eyes, she could not find it in her heart to
resist.
"But you must have something serviceable in cot-
ton," she decreed. "That brown plaid gingham,
trimmed with the tatting collar Aunt Martha gave
you, would be becoming. Dear me! How fast the
money slips away! My old cloak will have to do, I
fear; though I'd hoped to manage that cape pictured
in Godey's called 'The Alsatian.' "
Her voice was wistful, and Mary said: "You shall
have it, Mother, if I go without the poplin. You
haven't a decent wrap to your name; and we must
not shame Uncle Daniel by our appearance."
In this manner was Persis persuaded against what
she called her "better judgment"; though when the
cape was finished (trimmed with the heavy gimp
Mary insisted on), and she saw her husband's eyes
light with pleasure as he regarded her in it, the good
woman felt a sense of gladness at her great extrava-
gance.No wonder the weeks passed quickly with so much
to accomplish before April, the time set for their
start westward. Even after these costumes were com-
pleted and hung away (to be brought out proudly
and displayed to interested callers), there was much
to do. Foodstuffs had to be packed and planned for:
flour, sugar, beans, rice, tea and coffee, salt, pepper,
soda and cream of tartar, vinegar, bacon, pickles,
and the dried fruits which Daniel Alden had called
a luxury.
There followed a fierce scouring of old cooking
utensils; while the teaspoons which had belonged to
Persis' grandmother, and the silver cream pitcher
said to have come from England with the first
Aldens, were, with other treasures, packed into a
small leather trunk with Persis' initials in brass tacks
adorning its cover. Luther had made it for her in
the days of their betrothal, "And I would no more
part with that trunk than I would part with you,"
she declared indignantly, when Mary suggested leav-
ing it behind.
It was fortunate for the family peace of mind, how-
ever, that news of all which had occurred in far-off
Gregory Gulch since the writing of Daniel Alden's
letter, had not drifted back to the isolated New Eng-
land farm. For by the end of that summer of '59,
gold from the blossom rock and gulch sands totaled
infinitely less than in May and June. Realizing that
only an occasional man had made expenses, and but
one in hundreds acquired even a modest fortune,
panic seized the discouraged gold hunters, and des-perate miners began to desert the gulches in great
numbers.
Soon a second stampede back to the States had
started, and the plains were once more alive with
prairie schooners driven by disheartened, ragged,
disappointed men. These wagons which had left
"home" bearing such gay inscriptions as: "Lightning
Express"-"Ho, For the Gold Fields of Kansas!"-
"I'm Off For the Peak-Are You?" were now in-
scribed: "Bound For America"-"Root, Hog, Or
Die," and other equally portentous mottoes. And to
cap it all, late in September came the first of the
snows-deep snow, driving out even the most
courageous.
All this was happily unsuspected by the New
England Aldens as they packed their belongings in
the covered wagon. Oxen were already purchased:
sturdy, muscular beasts that looked strong enough
for the most fatiguing of journeys. Persis was not
idle for a single minute during this busy time. Going
from room to room, directing her daughter as to
what must be packed in the cowhide trunk, and
what put into the cupboards Luther had concocted,
her knitting needles moved incessantly as she fash-
ioned sontags for herself and Mary "bosom
friends," they were called in Godey's Lady's Book,
a term which she forbore to use, however, since
her husband considered it a bit "indelicate."
It was on a warm March afternoon when the doors
of the old barn were standing open, that Luther
Alden, moving back for a better view of his enchant-ing toy, declared: "It's finished, daughter, all but
one thing which we've forgotten."
Mary, who sat nearby shelling dried corn into a
tin pail, glanced up in some surprise.
"Forgotten! What can we possibly have forgotten
at this late date?"
Her father smiled, touching the white canvas
cover as he responded: "Didn't your uncle say that
a wagon of this sort should bear a name something
to show its destination? What shall it be, child?
'Pike's Peak or Bust', like so many others, or
"
"Oh, no!" the girl protested, and sprang up. Her
eyes were starry, as if (Luther told Persis afterward)
she were seeing visions. "Not 'Pike's Peak or Bust',
Father, but something lovelier. Let's paint on our
snowy canvas, 'To the Shining Mountains!' Could
anything in the world be lovelier than that?"
So on an April morning not long after, an ox-
drawn prairie schooner bearing this inscription,
made its slow way down a rocky New England road.
Below lay a valley fair with the verdure of early
springtime-"Green pastures and still waters," mur-
mured Luther Alden, his throat tightening. He
glanced at his wife, but seeing her lips set with a
strange rigidity, looked suddenly away. And then
as if sensing her parents' somber mood and wishing
to dispel it, Mary spoke, her voice ringing out happily
on the still, sweet air.
"Look, dears!" she cried. "A bluebird! And-and
he's flying West!"CHAPTER VI
NE bright, warm morning during the month
of June, a solitary horseman might have been
seen riding toward Bull Creek in Kansas, near which
the Overland and Santa Fé trails divide. A week
before, he had ferried across the Missouri River some
miles north of St. Joseph in company with two cov-
ered wagons drawn by horses: a party from Illinois
bound for the gold fields of the Rockies as he was
himself. Meeting by chance, but led by a common
interest, they had journeyed together for several
days; but once safely deposited on Kansas soil they
parted company, the heavily laden wagons taking the
route usually followed by those who cross the Mis-
souri between St. Joseph and Council Bluffs.
This route, which ascends the south side of the
Platte River and converges with the Overland trail
not far from Fort Kearney, was, in truth, one which
the lone rider had planned to follow; but growing
weary of the garrulous conversation of one man in
the party, and the almost incessant boasting of an-
other, he had invented some "important business"
with an imaginary friend, who was even then await-
ing his arrival in the town of Leavenworth!
"But we'll meet at Fort Kearney or somewhere
along the trail," he asserted blithely as they reachedthe parting of the ways; yet even as he spoke the
young adventurer was promising himself to loiter
each day as much as possible in order to avoid this
very thing-vowing, also, to steer clear of joining
another company. Though a not unsociable youth,
he preferred solitude to uncongenial companions;
and, turning south, breathed a sigh of relief at hav-
ing escaped with so little trouble.
Thus a week later found him on his way with a
replenished pack, a rested mount, and the thrill of
adventure tingling in his blood. Yet despite those
ardent vows on leaving the wagons from Illinois, he
had been almost persuaded to join a company of gold
seekers going by the shorter and more dangerous
Smoky Hill route, which straggles through the barren
Ridge Country where grass is thin and water all too
scarce. Love of his horse, however, and fear of
possible suffering for the animal, prevailed against a
natural desire to reach the gold fields in quicker
time; and again the youth set forth on his solitary
journey, knowing full well that there would be no
lack of company should he require it, since the
Overland Trail was said to be alive with prairie
schooners after passing Fort Kearney.
Even at five in the morning the Kansas sun is hot
in June, and reaching a big cottonwood on the edge
of a strip of woodland, the traveler dismounted with
the intention of giving his horse a moment's rest,
since he feared there would be little shade for many
miles. Not until then did he hear the sound of rip-
pling water."This must be Indian Creek," he told himself; and
striking into the woods in search of a cool drink,
came face to face with a young woman holding her
long, full skirts in one hand, while she struggled
with the weight of a large wooden bucket filled with
water.
An exclamation of surprise escaped her lips on
catching sight of him; but the stranger merely
snatched off his wide sombrero, and saying: "Allow
me, please," took the bucket into his own hands.
"Where to?" he questioned, a smile disclosing
strong, white, even teeth; while the girl protested,
quick color mounting to her hair:
"But why should you carry my bucket? And-and
where did you come from? There was no one near
when we chose this camping place last evening."
The young man rested the pail of water on a
stump, while his approving glance took in every de-
tail of the girl's appearance: her dark hair parted in
the middle and coiled low in the slender neck; her
brown eyes with lashes curving upward; her wide,
sweet mouth. Even the neat plaid gingham with its
snowy collar did not escape notice as he responded
gaily: “ 'If a body meet a body going through' (don't
run, please!), not the rye, of course, but a grove of
cottonwoods, and carrying a pail too heavy for her
strength, what should he do, may I ask? As to your
second question-I camped last night not far from
Westport, and am headed for the Rocky Mountains
by way of”
He stopped abruptly, being on the point of saying,"by way of the Overland Trail," when Fate, or what
he termed later "a kindly Providence" stayed his
tongue.
"The Rocky Mountains!" echoed his companion,
her dark eyes brightening. "Are you a gold seeker
like ourselves? Our camp is just beyond that"
"Mary! Mary, where are you?"
This anxious question came from not far away;
and hastily responding: "Coming, Mother," the girl
left her interrupted sentence unfinished and hurried
toward a prairie schooner, its white top visible be-
yond the trees. The young man followed with the
wooden bucket, and as they came in sight, Persis
Alden, coffee pot in hand, stared at her daughter's
escort in unfeigned amazement.
"I met this this gentleman near the brook,
Mother," explained Mary, "and he was kind enough
to carry the water for me. His name . . .”
She turned, her eyes asking the question, while the
stranger set his burden upon the ground, swept off
his hat again, and answered with an engaging smile:
"I am Michael Warwick, Madam, late of London,
England; but now merely a wanderer in search of
gold."
Persis vouchsafed a smile of welcome while she
wondered inwardly if this good-looking young fellow
might be a suitable acquaintance for her daughter.
But his own smile disarmed her; and even as the cau-
tious doubt flashed through her mind, the woman
laughed at it. Were they not pioneers, all of them,
faring forth into the wilderness to seek their for-tunes? Surely there was no room for snobbery on
these wide Kansas plains.
"If you can delay until our fire is started," she said
cordially, "I'll be happy to offer you a cup of coffee.
Here comes Mr. Alden with the kindlings now.
Luther," (as her husband joined them closely fol-
lowed by a young collie) "this is Michael Warwick,
another gold seeker, who will eat breakfast with us.
That is," she added, turning to the Englishman, "if
my invitation is not too late?"
"I must confess to a sort of breakfast an hour
since," the guest admitted, "but it left much to be
desired, and I shall be only too thankful for a cup of
coffee."
Luther Alden, who had dropped his firewood upon
the ground, stretched out a work-worn hand in
greeting.
"It will be a pleasure to share our simple meal with
you," he said with natural courtesy; and added, turn-
ing to the puppy: "Come, Zeb, shake hands with this
new friend and bid him welcome."
The dog, whose tail had been wagging vigorously
every minute, sat down at once and extended a cor-
dial paw which the Englishman shook gravely.
"Delighted to make your acquaintance, Zeb," he
assured the friendly animal. Then turning to Luther:
"Let me build the fire for you, Mr. Alden. I'd like
nothing better."
He knelt to his task, breaking the dry twigs in
shorter lengths and building with the skill of an ac-
complished camper which fascinated both Mary andthe dog. The latter, indeed, refused to move more
than a step away, and sat watching with alert, brown
eyes, each stage in the construction of the camp fire.
"He's an intelligent pup, isn't he?" smiled the
stranger. "Has he come far with you?"
"From Massachusetts." Mary was touching the
dog's head with a caressing hand. "He was a parting
present from a boy in the village. I admit we were
dismayed when we first saw him, wondering how we
could find room for a puppy in our crowded wagon;
but we should be sad indeed to part with him now,
wouldn't we, Zeb?"
The dog snuggled a bit closer, and the young man
said: "Why Zeb, may I ask? Was he, perhaps, named
for the friend who gave him to you?"
The girl laughed lightly, and shook her head.
"He is the namesake of another brave explorer.
Can't you guess who?"
Her companion's brows gathered in a perplexed
frown, then cleared suddenly as he exclaimed: "Why,
Pike, of course! Zebulon Pike, first to discover the
Peak for which we're headed! Come here, Lieuten-
ant, and shake hands again."
By this time the fire was crackling cheerily, and as
Persis brought out a slab of bacon, Luther suggested
that, since they were entertaining company, it would
be an excellent time to indulge in flapjacks.
There followed a merry meal, for the stranger
proved himself an amusing guest; and as he re-
counted his troubles with the party from Illinois,
even Luther Alden was forced to laugh."Like many an unpleasant happening it has a
comical side when looking back upon it," observed
the Englishman. "But if I'd been compelled to en-
dure the boasting of that man Smith for another day,
I should have lost my mind-not to mention my tem-
per which was near lost already! To hear the fellow
talk one would think he had discovered America
himself!"
"And in order to escape him you were forced to
change your plans and travel by way of the Santa
Fé?" asked Persis.
"Is that your own route?" countered the youth
suavely, the significance of his question being lost on
all of them as Luther answered:
"My brother Daniel, who has been at the gold
fields for some time, advised our traveling this way,
thinking it safest; though now it seems that more
are going by the Overland. For a time we hesitated
about what to do; but at last decided to follow in my
brother's footsteps."
"Besides," spoke up Mary eagerly. "I love the very
name of this old trail. The Santa Fé! To me it sounds
far more romantic than the Overland."
"A sensible reason for choosing a path through the
wilderness, my dear!" commented her father in
amused sarcasm.
"But why not?" defended the young man as Mary
flushed a little. "In fact, it was that very name which
lured me also." (This was indeed brazen, since not
until the last five minutes had he once considered
taking the southern route!) "You are right, MissAlden, there is something which captures one's imag-
ination in the names the French and Spaniards gave
these trails and rivers. One finds poetry and romance
in the very sound of them. La Fountaine qui Bouille
-Cache à la Poudre El Río de las Ánimas, which
means, I've been told, the River of Lost Spirits, so
called in memory of some Spaniards who perished on
that river without benefit of clergy." He sprang up,
and with a sudden change of subject, said to Persis:
"If you will permit a mere man to wash your dishes,
Mrs. Alden, I shall be happy to carry them to the
creek. The sun grows hotter as it gets higher, and I
dare say we should not loiter too long."
When he returned the oxen were in their places,
and Persis seated beside her husband. Mary had
waited to stow away the dishes; and as their guest ap-
proached the prairie schooner he paused to read the
lettering upon its cover.
'To The Shining Mountains!'" Blue eyes met
brown in a quick glance of understanding. "I think
you must be something of a poet, Miss Alden. It was
you, I am sure, who was responsible for that in-
scription."
The girl smiled down upon him from the wagon
hood.
"Only so far as appropriating it for our own use,"
she confessed. "Years and years ago the traders and
trappers called the Rockies the Shining Mountains.
I chose the name because"
"Come on!" called Luther with impatience. "The
sun is already hot, daughter, and we must start."The Englishman turned at once, and going to
Persis, stretched out a firm, tanned hand.
"I must thank you for a pleasant hour and a de-
licious breakfast, Mrs. Alden," he said courteously,
"but for fear of boring you as that Christopher
Columbus from Illinois bored me, I promise to keep
at a respectful distance in future, even though we
follow the same route."
"There'll be no need of that!" Persis assured him
warmly, troubled for fear her husband's warning had
sounded brusque. "It will give us all a feeling of
security to know that another traveler is nearby; and
haven't I heard that a good horse is invaluable for
locating wood and water on these great plains?"
"In that case," returned their new acquaintance
as he sprang into the saddle, "I shall be within hailing
distance should you need me."
With a smile for Mary he lifted his wide sombrero
high a moment, touched his mare lightly with a spur,
and was lost to sight beyond the cottonwoods before
the oxen lifted their heavy feet and started on the
long day's trek.Ο
CHAPTER VII
N that April day when the Aldens had parted
from their native village, many old friends rose
early to bid them "Godspeed!" Most of these good
people bore what they called "parting presents": gifts
offered in a spirit of loving kindness, yet ranging,
thought Persis, as she accepted a fancy jig-saw wall
bracket, from "the sublime to the ridiculous." The
poor woman knew she would be hard put to stow
away these unexpected donations in their crowded
wagon; and when a small boy whom her daughter
had taught in Sabbath school, came forward bearing
a squirming puppy, she was tempted to rebel until a
glance at the lad's face told her that he was making
this sacrifice for love of Mary.
"He's a good watch dog," the child said eagerly.
"He won't let nothing hurt you, Miss Mary, not wild
animals, or Injuns, or or nothing at all. P'raps you'd
better pet him some at first. I-I sorter think maybe
he'll miss me."
Seeing that her adorer was fighting tears as a small
pink tongue caressed his freckled cheek for the last
time, Mary made haste to answer: “Of course I'll pet
him! He's a darling puppy, Lewis. Let's name him
after Pike-the gallant soldier who discovered Pike's
Peak, you know. That's surely a splendid name for a
dog who's going to the Rocky Mountains.""You bet it is!" The boy's eyes lighted with some-
thing happier than tears. "Say! write me about him
sometime, won't you how he enjoys the prairies and
-and everything?"
"I'll write," she promised, taking the furry bundle
into her arms. "And what," the girl asked her dis-
mayed parents when at last they started, "what else
could I do?"
"Nothing," agreed Luther, who knew full well how
a small boy worships his dog and a girl like Mary,
"but how we'll manage a pup on this long trip. . . ."
He forbore to finish the sentence, while Persis ob-
served with humor: “Let us be thankful at least, that
come dinner time this apple pie of Mattie Badger's
can be stowed inside us! Where we can put such an
accumulation is beyond my reckoning. Take this silk
quilt which Granny Phillips has worked on for five
years. She says we're to save it for your wedding,
Mary, but where we'll store it in the meantime. . . ."
Words failed her, and the girl laughed as she re-
sponded: "Cheer up, Mother. Perhaps the time won't
be so long as you expect! It's a beautiful quilt. How
dear of Granny to give it to me! These skillets from
Mrs. Gilder will come in handy, anyway; and this
long toasting fork is splendid. The cross-stitch pin
cushion was from Miss Annie Joyce, Mother; and
Cousin Lizzie made the bookmark with the honey-
suckle painted on it. Don't look so worried, Father.
You mustn't let a silk quilt and a puppy mar the first
day of our journey!"
"What good word can you say for that infernaljig-saw contraption of Charlie Emerson's?" ques-
tioned her father with a wry smile.
"Tut-tut!" Persis reproved him. "It was kind of
Charlie to remember us; and the gold paint cost him
a pretty penny. What was it that Parson Gilder gave
you, Mary? Didn't I see him hand you a wrapped
package?"
"You did; but in all the confusion I've had no
chance to open it. Oh, dear! Zebulon Pike is napping
on my wedding quilt! Never mind, Zebby" (as the
dog looked up reproachfully), "you meant no harm,
and soon as I look at Parson Gilder's present I'll
make a bed for you."
Lifting a flat package she untied the cords and held
aloft a large, square blankbook, bound in brown
leather, with exquisite gilt tooling along its edges.
An envelope addressed to herself dropped from the
open pages, and Mary said: "Let's stop for a few
moments in this shady spot, Father, while I read you
the dear old parson's letter. It made a lump rise in
my throat to part with him."
The oxen came to a not unwilling standstill, and
opening the finely written sheets of paper, the girl
began:
"My very dear young friend: Many years ago (on
my eighteenth birthday, to be exact) my grandfather
presented me with this blankbook, which he had
brought from England. At that time it was my firm
intention to set down therein all the stirring events
of my career. On the flyleaf [executed with much fine
shading and youthful flourishes!] you will perceivemy name and the date on which I wrote it: 'Jonathan
Gilder 3d. from his grandfather, Jonathan Gilder 1st.
July 2nd. 1795.' And although so long a time has
passed since I penned this ornate inscription, I recall
wondering if these pages, roomy though they are,
would be sufficient to hold a record of my adventures.
"Alas! my child, 'Pride goeth before destruction,
and an haughty spirit before a fall.' For there have
been no adventures to relate-nothing, in fact, which
I deemed worthy of these fair pages. Many a time
have I taken pen in hand, only to realize that my
small joys and sorrows-my petty triumphs, were
truly of no account in this vast universe. Thus the
years went by, and though I have essayed to do my
duty by God and man, it has seemed at times only a
humdrum duty, and that those great adventures of
my youthful dreams were not to be.
"So it is to you I pass the torch, dear Mary. It is
you, setting forth on this far migration which, even
now, is making history, who must fill these pages.
Think not the smallest detail too trivial to record.
Your children, your grandchildren, and the descen-
dants of other brave pioneers will thank you for the
chronicle in years to come.
"And remember that my blessing is following you
into the wilderness, as it has followed you always
since that day when your proud young father laid
you in my arms, dressed in the long white christen-
ing robe which was the work of your dear mother's
fingers. I fear not for your future, little Mary. Why
should I? For surely the waste places shall be madeglad, and 'the desert blossom as the rose,' since your
bright youth is to become a part of them."
As Mary folded the letter in silence, her father
cleared his throat, while Persis dashed a sudden mist
out of her eyes and said: "That is a sacred trust,
daughter, and you must keep it faithfully. God grant
that the dear old man will live to read your story.
Each night you must record the number of miles we
travel; and even on the most uneventful of our days
there will be happenings of interest, I am sure."
This proved true. From the first entry, which be-
gan: "Went 22 miles to Enfield, where we are passing
the night at Cousin Hezekiah White's. Attracted
much attention along the way. . . ." there was no
lack of colorful material to set down; and the girl
made her handwriting as cramped as possible, fear-
ing, as Parson Gilder had feared before her, that the
pages were not sufficient for the chronicle.
So on a night in June, an early supper over, the
oxen grazing peacefully nearby, Mary Alden opened
her book and wrote:
"Traveled a few miles beyond Bull Creek, where
the two great trails divide. Three other wagons were
in sight, all going by the Overland which follows the
Platte River; and as we watched them taking their
slow way across the prairie, a feeling of loneliness,
almost of fear, crept over me. However, we are not
entirely deserted. Only this morning we were joined
by a young Englishman, Michael Warwick by name,
who rides a beautiful bay mare named 'Shamrock,'
in honor of his mother, who, he tells us, came fromthe north of Ireland. He is a gold seeker-a gentle-
man (if one can judge from speech and manners),
and possesses a sense of humor that is surely an asset
on a trip of this sort! We saw little of him through
the day; but in the late afternoon found him waiting
for us at this camping spot where he advised us to
stop, fearing there might be no more water until
reaching Black Jack Creek, which was too far for
our weary oxen to travel.
"It has been so hot that even Mother appeared
wilted, but she has revived now that this pitiless sun
has set and the day's march is over. Passed three In-
dians at noontime, but except for begging a little
food they did not molest us, and seemed greatly taken
with the sunbonnets which Cousin Julia White gave
us as a parting present! Must get to bed, as Father
wishes to make an early start to-morrow in order to
go a greater distance during the cooler hours. Mr.
Warwick, whose camp is not far off, has just called
good-night. It is indeed cheering to have company
nearby."P
CHAPTER VIII
RAIRIE! Nothing else in the world!
During the days that followed, as she sat in
the shadow of the wagon cover and gazed off at a
slowly receding landscape, Mary Alden found it
difficult to believe her eyes. She had expected a sort
of desert, brown and arid, but not this flower-dappled
wilderness that stretched away into a far horizon.
Sunflowers were everywhere; while morning glories
lifted their pale cups shyly to the sky. Primroses
abounded, and blue petunias-white daisies, and
brown-eyed Susans. Milkweed bearing strange orange
or lilac-colored blossoms flourished not far from the
delicate, familiar beauty of snow-on-the-mountain
and Queen Anne's lace. A glory of crimson phlox
vied with the loveliness of pink and lavender ver-
benas, goldenrod, blue larkspur. And roses! Their
fragrance was wafted on the air, bringing to Mary a
picture of the New England farm where she was
born, and her grandmother's favorite white rose
bush growing beside a rain water barrel at the shed
door.
Nor was this all. There were other blossoms,
"stranger flowers," the girl called them until she
learned their names: Spanish needle, rosin weed,
false indigo, chicory, a rioting profusion of brightblue spiderwort; and that gorgeous splash of never-
to-be-forgotten scarlet that is Indian paint brush.
This wealth of flowers held Persis Alden spell-
bound too; but it was the whole scene—the wide ex-
panse of plains and sky and color that enchanted
Mary. Sometimes the shadows of late afternoon would
change the limitless space into an ocean.
"Look!" she exclaimed the first time this miracle
occurred. "Am I dreaming, or do I behold the sea?"
And the young Englishman to whom she spoke,
smiled as he quoted:
"The desert circle spreads
Like the round ocean, girdled with the sky."
For by this time Michael Warwick was an estab-
lished member of the party from Massachusetts. This
came about soon after leaving Big Bull Creek, when
they met a dilapidated wagon headed back to the
States. Its original "Pike's Peak or Bust" had been
carelessly erased, and "Busted, by God!" was painted
below it in rough letters.
This depressing spectacle was propelled by a pair
of footsore mules, and driven by two disgruntled
miners whose report of conditions at the gold fields
was more disturbing to the cautious Luther than he
cared to admit.
"You'll turn back along of us if you know what's
good for you," one gaunt, bearded, disillusioned man
advised. "Even if you get there without bein' pes-
tered to death by Indians along the way, or maybescalped, there ain't enough gold in them Rocky
Mountains to make a respectable weddin' ring; and
what there was has been dug out already. I never
come so near starvin' back at the old farm in Ohio
as I done last winter at Denver City. Why, folks,
flour brings forty dollars a hundred pounds! Would
you believe it? And time and again I see men pickin'
up swill out o' the streets and eatin' it with con
siderable relish, too! But if the gold fever's got you,"
he ended pessimistically, as, much to Mary's indig-
nation, he spat a mouthful of tobacco juice onto a
rose bush, "you'll never believe what you don't see
with your own eyes. Why, back yonder some twenty
miles or so, we come on a damn fool pushin' his grub
in a wheelbarrow and takin' a boarder on top o' that!
But would he listen to us? Not him. He'd got the
fever."
Alarming though this story sounded, it was im-
possible not to smile at the picture of so resourceful
a pioneer. Recalling it after the wagons had
parted company, Persis remarked that the enter-
prising gold seeker with the wheelbarrow deserved
success. "And if boarders were plentiful along this
trail," she added, "I'd be tempted to follow his thrifty
example and help defray the expenses of our trip."
Michael Warwick, who sat nearby mending a torn
saddle-girth, lifted laughing eyes as he responded:
"And I would be tempted to take you up on that
proposition, Mrs. Alden, if I didn't realize that it was
uttered in a dark moment."
"So you are already tired of your own cooking?"bantered Mary, folding the dish towels which had
been drying in the sun.
The young man nodded, setting a careful stitch.
"But I didn't know it until last evening when I
tasted the Johnny cake baked in your Dutch oven,"
he confessed. And though a week ago, Miss Mary,
I flattered myself on being the king of flapjack
makers, I have humbly abdicated the throne in favor
of your mother. Perhaps all this heat has made me
lazy, for, joking aside, I would pay well for the sort of
meals you people seem to conjure out of nothing here
on the prairie. They seem fully as miraculous to me
as the tricks of a prestidigitator appear to a small boy
at a circus!"
"But I shouldn't want you to pay more than the
meals cost us," returned Persis, as if seriously con-
sidering the proposal.
The Englishman laughed as he inquired: "How
then, would the added labor help out with your ex-
penses? It is plain that you're too hospitable for your
own good, Mrs. Alden. But my money belt is not
empty yet, by any means; and if you care to lighten
it by allowing me to share your meals, I shall still
be in your debt after my board is paid."
Thus it was settled, though Michael took pains not
to let his presence become a burden. Often he rode
ahead searching for good "nooning places," as they
were called; or hunting a spot to camp at night where
there would be sufficient water for the thirsty ani-
mals. And as the days passed the young man saved
her husband so much hard work and worry, thatPersis was reluctant to accept even the modest sum
they had decided on.
"I never heard of a boarder making the fires on
which his meals were cooked," she protested vigor-
ously one day; but the Englishman merely grinned
into her troubled face and countered: "Did you ever
hear of a landlady who, without recompense, mended
her boarder's clothes, Madam?" And for some subtle
reason Persis forbore to say that it was Mary's clever
fingers, not her own, which darned a tear in the coat
he had tossed into the wagon a few hours earlier.
They were nooning that day in the shade of an
old cottonwood; and as the girl set up a folding table
which was her father's pride, she stooped to recover
three large turquoise-colored beads, half buried in
the dusty, sandy soil.
"See what I've found!" she cried, holding them in
her open palm for all to view. "Do you suppose some
traveler or trader lost them here?”
"Some dead warrior, more likely," asserted
Michael, his eyes lifting to the high branches of the
cottonwood which supported a rude, weather-beaten
platform, hitherto unnoticed by them all. "That is
undoubtedly the last resting place of an Indian," the
young man explained, "put there many years ago, of
course, or these beads could not have escaped from
the skins in which the body must have been wrapped,
mummywise. Many a time I've read about the cus-
tom, but have never seen such a tomb before. Shall
I climb the tree for further exploration?"
Persis Alden shuddered, and shook her head."Let's forget it, since we must eat in this grue-
some spot. But you should treasure the beads, daugh-
ter. They are a beautiful blue; and years from now
you will be glad to have them as a memento of our
journey across the plains."
"Give them to me," said Michael, and going to the
stream, proceeded to wash away the dust and dirt
that marred their color. "Now they are indeed beau-
tiful," he agreed. "Genuine turquoise, or I'm much
mistaken. You should string them on a ribbon, Miss
Mary, and wear them for an ornament."
"We can purchase a length of ribbon at the first
dry goods store we pass!" asserted Luther with dry
humor, as he in turn, took the beads into his hand
for closer examination.
"And in the meantime," laughed his daughter,
"the cord which Parson Gilder tied about his parting
gift to me shall make my necklace. Zebulon Pike
chewed several inches of it when on an exploring
expedition the other day, but there's enough left, I'm
sure."
"Then fetch it at once so these treasures will not
be lost," advised Persis as she and her husband re-
turned to their interrupted duties.
So it was Michael Warwick who slipped the ancient
beads onto the cord as Mary held it, and his fingers
that fastened it about her throat, a proceeding which
took over long, it seemed to Luther Alden, who kept
a wary eye upon the two young people as he fed
the fire.
"I feel like an Indian Princess!" declared the girl,her dark eyes shining as she touched the new trinkets
lightly.
"Indian or not, you are my Princess!" Michael re-
sponded.
He bent low in a Chesterfieldian obeisance which
brought a ripple of delighted laughter from Mary's
lips-a sound which did not escape the ears of her
adoring father; and later that day when the journey
had been resumed, he said out of a thoughtful
silence:
"I'm not sure, my dear, that we were wise to add
that young fellow to our party."
Persis, who had taken a liking to her boarder, sur-
mised the thought behind this utterance, but chose
to ignore it as she replied: "Why not? Only a
moment since I was wondering how we could have
managed without his help."
There followed more silence, broken only by the
patient pad, pad, pad of oxen feet. Then Luther
said, casting a suspicious glance upon his better-half:
"Are you really so innocent as you would have me
think, Persis? I swear it took the young man all of
two minutes to fasten that bit of string 'round Mary's
neck!"
His wife laughed softly-merrily, it seemed to
Luther Alden.
"Have you forgotten, dear man, that the back of a
young girl's neck is, more often than not, a comely
sight? Seems to me I can recall a time when you your-
self were not oblivious of its charms!"
"You are a shameless creature to remind me of itthen!" He reached out, touching her hand a moment,
much, Persis remembered, as the shy young Luther
would have done another proof, she mused, that
all the cares and weariness of this long journey had
failed to quench the ardor with which her husband
had started out upon it.
"I believe you are blushing at the memory," she
teased. "But don't worry too much about the young
folks, Luther. Friendship ripens quickly in an en-
vironment like this; but once we reach Cherry Creek
there'll be a host of brave young pioneers to divert
Mary's attention; though I doubt if any of them
measure up to our boarder in breeding and man-
ners."
"Fine manners can cover a multitude of sins," re-
torted the man gloomily. "Not that I've reason to
think ill of the lad," he added with characteristic
fairness. "But Mary's acquaintances have been limited
to the young men of our own village; and there's a
polish to this Englishman that we farmers lack."
"Is there indeed?" Persis sat straighter, a habit
she had when (as Daniel Alden said) her "feathers
were unduly ruffled." "Do you imagine for one mo-
ment, Luther Alden, that I would have chosen a man
who was not every inch a gentleman?"
Her indignation in his behalf stirred Luther's sense
of humor. It was hard work to suppress a smile as
he said soberly: "My brother Daniel once likened me
to a plodding clodhopper. He said"
"I care not a pin what Daniel said," began the
woman, when Mary called from her place in the rearof the big wagon, "What wild discussion is raging be-
tween you two? First I hear my own name uttered
in cautious undertones; and now you are speaking
angrily of my darling uncle. Zebulon Pike has kept
his ears cocked for at least five minutes, trying to hear
this family argument. Don't growl so, Zeb. Lie down!
Be a good doggy. They-" Her voice ceased suddenly;
then she cried out in great excitement: "Look,
Father! A baby antelope too young to be afraid of
us! Zeb must have heard it! And-and-why, see
those Indians!"T
CHAPTER IX
HEY camped that night near the little town of
Council Grove, stopping first for a supply of
corn, and a chat with the inhabitants of the village,
who gathered quickly, eager for news, as the white-
topped wagon drew up before the store. Not far be-
yond this settlement was the camping place a cluster
of trees extending for some miles, with a small creek
(Elm Creek, they had been told) running through it.
Oaks, hickories and walnuts predominated, and out-
going companies often waited there to repair prairie
schooners and get timbers for the remainder of the
journey, which they lashed under the wagons. One
company was just leaving as the New Englanders
arrived, a party of Mexican traders from Santa Fé,
who were planning to travel during the cool of night.
Both Persis and Mary were enchanted with this
strip of woodland; and when their evening meal was
over the latter expressed a desire to watch the sunset
from a small hill not far away, a suggestion which was
declined by her parents, both being weary, but sec-
onded with enthusiasm by the youthful boarder.
"Perhaps I should have gone along to keep an eye
upon them," observed Luther, as the young people
started off with only Zeb as chaperon, "but I feel
more tired than usual to-night, perhaps because thatband of Indians, keeping pace with us so persistently
on that distant ridge this afternoon, made me uneasy
for our safety; though I dare say we shall grow ac-
customed to such sights as the days pass. This grove
seems like an oasis in a desert, doesn't it, Mother?"
Persis nodded in reply; then said, after a moment:
"I believe it rests both tired nerves and muscles to
lie on one's back and look up at the sky through green
branches, be they in Massachusetts or Kansas."
"And after all," declared her husband, as if en-
deavoring to soothe an uneasy conscience, "our girl
knows how to behave herself better than some young
misses."
A smile of amusement curved Persis Alden's lips.
