"I'm relieved to see that your fears of early after-
noon appear to be diminishing! No doubt poor Mary
would have been disgusted if you'd tagged along.
Young folks can talk more naturally to each other
when alone, Luther. Have you forgotten how irri-
tated you were those evenings when you came court-
ing me, and Aunt Sarah would settle down with her
knitting and join our conversation?"
"Lead it, you mean," corrected Luther. "Her
tongue was hung in the middle and wagged both
ends. And I was a bashful lad, apt to lose my own
tongue when out in company. Still, I seem to re-
member a demure young lady who not infrequently
suggested a moonlight stroll on those occasions, know-
ing her aunt was too fleshy to enjoy such exercise!"
"I had to," retorted Persis calmly, "for I knew you
wouldn't! You were too afraid of offending AuntSarah and setting her against you. She never approved
of evening strolls, you know, thinking all young
people were depraved. I have sometimes wondered
if it were not some early indiscretion of her own
which made her so uncharitable to others."
A rare chuckle escaped from Luther Alden.
"I, too, have been guilty of that base suspicion,"
he confessed, "though it is difficult to picture Aunt
Sarah as an alluring creature, even in her youth. She
couldn't have been over forty when we were keeping
company, my dear, and she weighed two hundred or
I'm no judge."
"Two hundred and three, Luther; and she would
turn in her grave if she could hear this conversation.
But it's a corroding thing to suspect evil in the young
and innocent. I've no doubt she would consider our
Mary a lost soul for climbing that hill in company
with a young fellow whose father and grandfather we
know nothing of."
But even great-aunt Sarah would have had trouble
in finding anything to criticise in the deportment of
the two young people who stood together, gazing off
at the impressive scene. Westward rippled an unend-
ing sea of prairie grasses; while to the north-east lay
stretched what looked like acre upon acre of hills
and trees.
"How beautiful it is!" breathed Mary softly.
Her companion nodded.
"After the heat and dust of days it seems almost
too beautiful to be true. But let's sit down and watch
those gorgeous sunset clouds. Council Grove. Perhapsthis is near the very spot where the American Gov-
ernment made a treaty with the Osage chiefs. Am I
mistaken, Miss Schoolma'am?"
"I am no schoolma'am, though Father was deter-
mined to make me one," the girl responded with a
laugh. "But Uncle Daniel's party stopped here two
days, and he wrote about it in one of his few letters.
That historic treaty was made in 1825, and those
agents of the United States (sent out, I think, to
measure a road running from Missouri to Santa Fé)
drove a shrewd bargain."
"You mean," asked Michael, "that they got the
better of the savages?"
"It looks that way to me. For the perpetual
privilege of crossing their lands, and protection from
hostile tribes farther west, we paid the Indians a
paltry five hundred dollars in cash, and three hun-
dred in goods. There, Mr. Warwick! Could a real
schoolma'am have recited the lesson better?"
Michael smiled and told her that to him it sounded
letter perfect. "Do you know all the details of your
country's history as well?"
"I fear not; but I am living my country's history
now," she answered with a shy smile. "That's why
dear Parson Gilder gave me his cherished book, and
why I'm so conscientious about writing in it. Some-
times I close my eyes and see fine roads built across
these endless prairies even railroads; though per-
haps I would be called a foolish dreamer if I spoke
the thought."
The young man said, his eyes on the curve ofMary's cheek: "Why foolish? Already stage coaches
are going by the Republican River route. Isn't it
logical to think that railroads will follow when the
country west of us is more thickly settled? Tell me,
Miss Mary, what made you leave your life of safe
security in the East, and set out on so hard and
dangerous a journey?"
For a space there was silence while the girl's eyes
rested on a gold-tipped cloud. Then she said quietly:
"Sometimes I ask myself that very question, Mr.
Warwick. Was it a touch of the wanderlust inherited
from my Uncle Daniel-or a cowardly dread of see-
ing myself become the old maid teacher of a village
school? One thing I know, it was not the lure of
riches, though I'd surely enjoy a share of all that gold
said to be buried in the Shining Mountains! To be
quite honest, I think it was a longing for adventure,
and a desire to see with my own eyes some of the
wonders my uncle tells about."
"I take it this wandering uncle of yours is an old
bachelor," opined Michael, at which Mary laughed.
"Old! Why Uncle Dan is the youngest person I
ever knew! He says he has never stopped long enough
in any place to court a sweetheart; but" (her eyes
changed suddenly from gay to grave, a way they had
that fascinated her companion), "but I have often
wondered if there were not some girl he cared for-
someone, perhaps, who was not free. For all his jollity,
my uncle can look sad when he thinks no one is near.
Oh, you will love him! No one can help it. Dear me!"
she cried, springing up as a call sounded from some-where below them," we've stayed so long that it will
be too dark to set down a record of this day's journey.
I must rise early to-morrow and do it before we
start."
But after all, there was time a-plenty, for Persis
was so reluctant to leave the woodland shade, that
it was decided to tarry another day, giving the animals
a well-earned rest, doing the necessary laundry-
baking something ahead for the next few meals, as
well as exploring the grove more thoroughly.
"I had almost forgotten it was the Saboath day,"
said Luther, when in the cool of evening they rested
beneath a spreading oak. "You have a good voice,
Michael, and so have these ladies though they're too
modest to admit it. Let's have a hymn or two by way
of worship. A vesper service in this historic spot will
be something to recall in days to come."
Little did Luther Alden know that many years
later, when he himself was only a memory, his daugh-
ter was to look back upon that Sabbath evening and
hear again the sweet, pure baritone of the young
Englishman, and her father's deep-voiced "Amen"
which followed the closing hymn of that simple
service-a hymn so singularly fitting the time and
place:
"Through the day Thy love has spared us;
Hear us ere the hour of rest:
Through the silent watches guard us;
Let no foe our peace molest.
Jesus, Thou our guardian be;
Sweet it is to rest in Thee.""The stop at this grove will be one of the shining
lights of our adventure," said Michael Warwick as
he helped yoke up the oxen next morning. "A piping
hot day lies ahead of us, Mrs. Alden. I dread to leave
these trees behind."
"Don't weaken my courage with such remarks,
young man." This from Persis, who had drawn near
to watch proceedings. "Surely God never made any-
thing more beautiful and useful than a tree; a fact
I never fully appreciated till now. I could linger here
indefinitely if we hadn't so many miles to go."
"Oh, you weak-hearted pioneers!" jeered Mary
from the wagon, as she put the breakfast dishes in
place. "I've loved this quiet rest as much as any of
you, but for all that, I am eager to go on. Why, even
Shamrock is champing at the bit; and Zebulon Pike
can scarcely wait to start! Who knows what wonders
may cross our path to-day?"CHAPTER X
ND to think," cried Michael Warwick, spring-
ing up from the camp fire he was laying, "that
I missed the chance of hunting a wild buffalo!"
This was some ten days later-more strenuous
and anxious days than any which had preceded them,
for a band of none-too-friendly Indians had dogged
their steps, begging for food and pilfering when the
chance arose. In fact, so menacing did they appear
that during the nights Luther and Michael took turns
at sentry duty, burning a fire through the long dark-
ness to warn the savages that they were being
watched.
Even the courageous Persis had grown nervous at
the persistence of these unwelcome intruders; and it
was a relief to all when, early that morning, amid
hoarse shouts and cries, they rode away to the East.
"And may they never return!" observed Luther
solemnly.
Now it was noontime, and coming upon their
boarder who had ridden ahead for several hours,
Mary called out in great excitement that a herd of
buffaloes had crossed their path a few miles back.
"To think I missed it!" he repeated tragically;
while Persis, alighting from the wagon, retorted: "It's
quite as likely that you missed the chance of losingyour life, my boy. Time and again I thought our
precious collie was on the point of losing his. My
heart was in my throat."
"Mine too," Mary confessed, still thrilled by this
adventure. "Yet do you know, I think those mon-
strous beasts were terribly afraid of Zebby. Beside
their ugly bulk he looked a midget; but you should
have seen them paw the ground whenever he got
too close. The dog acted as if he knew his power and
enjoyed teasing them; though it looked like a dan-
gerous sport to me."
"But hump rib soup is said to be a delicacy sur-
passed by nothing on land or sea," persisted Michael.
"And I have never laid eyes upon a herd of buf-
faloes."
"Cheer up, lad!" admonished Luther, amused at
the young Englishman's chagrin. "You're likely to
see a-plenty before long. Haven't you noticed how
the grass grows shorter with every mile? This is a
buffalo region or I'm much mistaken."
"A rattlesnake region too," Michael responded. "I
put an end to a brute of a rattler an hour ago. At first
I thought I'd say nothing of it; but after all, it is
better to be on guard."
Instinctively Persis glanced down to where she
stood; while Mary shuddered and exclaimed: "Oh,
dear! I'd far rather meet a charging buffalo than a
rattlesnake! And we've only just got rid of those
dreadful Indians!"
Her voice was so distressed that the young man
sought to reassure her."Don't let the fear of encountering a rattler spoil
your pleasure in the journey to come, Miss Mary. I
promise to stay closer from now on, and my faithful
Shamrock shall be your bodyguard. It was she who
heard the rattle and gave me warning-a violent
warning, I'll admit, for she reared so suddenly that
I very nearly took a header, being half asleep at the
time. These nights on sentry duty have made me
drowsy."
"You should be careful not to nap in the saddle,
however," cautioned Persis. "A broken bone would
be no small matter here in the wilderness."
"No small matter for you, I fear!" Michael re-
torted. "I can imagine your kind hands turning that
prairie schooner into a hospital. See here, Mrs. Alden,
I think you're looking tired. Let me spread a blanket
in the shade of the wagon where you can rest and
close your eyes on this glaring sun. Miss Mary and I
can prepare the meal; and I have a surprise on hand
-a fat jack rabbit all ready for the fire!"
Although touched by his care of her, Persis ex-
claimed in horror: "Close my eyes, with rattlesnakes
in the vicinity! My worst enemy couldn't call me a
timid woman, Michael Warwick, but I prefer to
rest some other time."
That afternoon their way ran in the dark shadow
of Pawnee Rock, a sandstone cliff, long feared as the
most dangerous spot on the trail to Santa Fé. Though
Luther Alden was all for pressing on, both young
people declared they must stop and explore the place."Why, it was here," cried Mary, "that Kit Carson
when standing guard at night, shot his own mule,
mistaking the poor creature for a hostile Indian!"
"And have you forgotten," asked her father, "that
it was here the savages kept watch for wagon trains,
ready to scalp and plunder? Tales of this ancient
rock have chilled my blood, child. It seems almost
like tempting Providence to stop."
Nevertheless, interest in the frowning cliff where
thousands of trappers, emigrants and soldiers had
carved their names, surmounted Luther's dread of
what might be lurking in its shadows. He even got
out his jackknife to carve his wife's initials and his
own; while Michael Warwick did the same for him-
self and Mary, encircling the letters in a flowery deco-
ration which, Luther told Persis afterward, might
easily be mistaken for a heart.
"I noticed that it was an excellent imitation!" she
responded with a smile that showed a glimpse of her
fine teeth. "But if the young man thought to hide
the fact with all those flourishes, he deceived only
himself. Our Mary looked almost too demure and
innocent as she watched him work."
The man was silent; and, amused at his troubled
thoughts, Persis said quietly: "You must admit,
Luther, that the boy is something of an artist."
"I could have done as good a job myself," he
boasted, "if I hadn't expected an Indian to appear at
my elbow any moment. I do not like that bloody
spot, Persis, and am thankful it is behind us for good
and all."That evening Mary wrote in her diary:
"July 14th. Traveled not more than fifteen miles,
for this has been a day of interruptions, the first oc-
curring before dawn when the Indians who have
alarmed us for so long, and kept us at a high pitch of
excitement and uneasiness, took their noisy depar-
ture, a proceeding for which we all gave thanks.
After this rude awakening we made an early start, but
had not been on our way an hour when we overtook
three dilapidated and discouraged individuals who
had left Leavenworth City on foot many weeks ago,
with only twenty pounds of corn meal and less than
two dollars in money! It is difficult to imagine such
foolhardiness in men well past middle age; but in
sheer pity for their plight we stopped while Mother
and I cooked them a hearty breakfast. We also gave
them a slab of bacon and some stale biscuits, which
they accepted with such gratitude that one would
have thought the food was heavenly manna! Poor
souls! Though we left them much cheered, I cannot
help wondering if they will ever reach their goal.
"The next halt in our day's march was when we
stopped to watch our first real herd of buffaloes.
Father says there must have been more than a hun-
dred; and for a while our gay explorer, Zebulon Pike,
led them a merry chase. What huge, ugly, ill-shapen
beasts they are, with long, shaggy hair covering their
heads, and that awful hump on their poor backs!
And so clumsy! At times they seemed to tie them-
selves in knots, throwing all four feet off the ground
at once. It was a sight I shall not soon forget, and one-alas!-which Mr. Warwick did not witness, he hav-
ing ridden ahead to find a nooning place. The poor
fellow was greatly disappointed to have missed the
spectacle; and, indeed, even during the excitement
of watching the strange beasts, I was filled with re-
gret that he was not with us, remembering his inter-
est in wild life, and how we could scarcely tear him
away from a town of prairie dogs near Big Cow Creek.
"Our final interruption was in some ways the most
thrilling of our adventures so far, for we stood in
the shadow of historic Pawnee Rock, and left our
initials carved thereon among those countless others.
I discovered that Michael W. is clever at such work,
for he enclosed my initials and his own in a scroll
of very lovely flourishes.
"Later in the day several antelope crossed our path,
moving with regal, majestic grace. No other words
describe their haughty gait. Indeed, there is some-
thing almost disdainful in the way they carry their
proud heads; and they move so lightly that they seem
to walk on air.
"But I fear our troubles are not over with the de-
parture of those terrifying Indians, for Michael
Warwick has killed a rattlesnake. I believe this new
menace makes us all a trifle nervous, though the men
of our party would not admit it. Mother, I regret to
say, looks very tired, and I trust the continuous heat
is not too much for her. Oh, for a cooling rain such
as we have in old New England! The steady drip,
drip, drip would sound like music! Even a thunder
storm (although the last one here on the wide plainswas terrifying) would be a glad relief. From midday
on we long for the setting of the sun.
"It is almost bedtime, for we retire early before
real darkness comes upon us. Our boarder, who sings
a pleasant baritone, is treating Mother to an Irish
folk song as I write. This entertainment has added
much to the pleasure of our short evenings; and even
Father, who has little ear for music, delights to listen,
especially when Mr. W. starts some old, familiar
hymn in which we all join. Last night, just before
dusk deepened into dark, his voice came from his
camping place not far away:
'Softly now the light of day
Fades upon my sight away,
Free from care, from labor free-
Lord, I would commune with Thee.'
"It was very beautiful drifting across the silent
prairie, and made me want to weep a little or say my
prayers. I did not know just which, so I did both!
Surely despite its many hardships and the constant
dread of encountering hostile savages, this journey
across the plains has been a wonderful experience;
and each night I look forward in happy anticipation
to what the next day will bring forth."L
CHAPTER XI
ITTLE thought Mary Alden as she penned those
words in the peaceful afterglow, that before
another sunset she was to witness one of those pitiful
tragedies which were-alas!-a part of that migra-
tion. Nooning was over and the slow march resumed
again, when they sighted another prairie schooner in
the distance. After a while it became apparent that
this wagon was at a standstill, and the Englishman
rode on ahead to discover whether its occupants were
in need of help. When he returned, after what
seemed an endless time to those in the slowly ap-
proaching wagon, one glance at the young man's face
told them that something was amiss.
"It is a young couple from Georgia, Mrs. Alden,
preparing to bury their baby daughter, who died a
few hours since from an attack of dysentery. Judging
from appearances they are very poor, and having
nothing more suitable the mother was about to wrap
the little body in one of their scant supply of blankets.
I begged her to wait, thinking you would spare her
a sheet or tablecloth. The poor girl (she looks no
older than Miss Mary), seems stunned by the
calamity. She is dry-eyed; though her husband had
hard work to suppress his tears as he dug the grave.
It is the saddest sight that I have ever seen.""Poor souls!" cried Persis with quick sympathy.
"Of course I would spare them a sheet were it my
very last. Drive on at once, Luther. If ever our help
was needed it is now."
And help they did! For it was Persis herself who
lifted that small, emaciated body from the mother's
arms, wrapping it in its snowy shroud with tender
hands; while Mary, appalled at this, her first sight of
real tragedy, begged Michael's help in lining the
little one's last resting place with cool green vines
found growing near the river bed. And it was Luther
Alden who, standing bareheaded in the sun beside
that shallow grave, repeated those words so fraught
with heavenly comfort:
...
"And God shall wipe away all tears from their eyes
And there shall be no more death, neither sorrow nor
crying...."
"His deep voice trembled with emotion," wrote
Mary when she recorded this sad happening, "and as
he ceased, Michael Warwick motioned the young man
to take his wife away while he and Father filled in
that pitiful, small grave. Nor was that all. The sacred
spot was denied even a rough board marker. They
must, it seemed, trample upon the place so that no
thieving Indians would desecrate it. Michael War-
wick even went so far as to lead Shamrock across
the spot, not once, but many times; and when we
rode away an hour later nothing remained to show
what had happened save a branch of pink rosebuds
which I laid there after the grim task was done."Though Mother implored the grieving parents
to join our company, their mules were weary, and the
young mother so exhausted from sleepless nights
that it seemed wiser for them to rest a day before
going on. I hoped the poor girl would shed some heal-
ing tears when Mother kissed her at parting; but her
voice did not even break as she cried out passion-
ately: 'Oh, if only I could do something for you!"
It was very pitiful, and the whole thing has cast a
gloom over our day. I could never, never bear to lose
a child, and have decided firmly not to marry. I hope
Michael Warwick will not sing to-night. It would
be more, I think, than I could possibly endure."
It was difficult for Mary to be her own light-
hearted self during the next few days. She could not
forget the sad young couple they had parted from;
and often found herself gazing back over the prairie
-a dry, uninteresting stretch at this part of the
journey-searching in vain for a glimpse of the
shabby, mule-drawn wagon they had left behind.
"Do you suppose they are safe, Father?" she would
question out of a long silence, her eyes troubled. And
an hour later: "I wonder if anything has happened.
Do you think it was right for us to leave them there?"
"Your daughter is taking this thing too much to
heart for her own good," said Michael Warwick one
noon when he was laying the fire as usual, while Mary
and her father went in search of water.
"Give her time," replied Persis sensibly. "Mary has
never come so close to death before, and it has set herpondering on many things. Only yesterday the girl
declared she had given up all thought of marriage,
fearing to face its sorrows. But she's young-thank
God!-and come the right man she will change her
views on that subject, or I'm no woman!"
If a glint of amusement lurked in her eyes, the
Englishman refused to meet it as he said quietly:
"Perhaps it is better that she should not view death
for the first time in one who is near and dear to her.
I was only a carefree boy on a holiday in Switzerland,
when word came that my mother had been thrown
from her horse and fatally injured. I started home
within the hour; but even then, I was too late. That
was my first encounter with tragedy, Mrs. Alden, and
the scar still hurts. For a while I tried to go on as
usual; but I could not endure the loneliness of the
old home without her, which accounts for my cross-
ing the ocean to seek forgetfulness and a fortune."
"Poor boy!" said Persis, laying a motherly hand
on his. "Are there no other dear ones belonging to
you?"
"No one who needs me. My father died when I was
very young; and my brother is married. Besides, I
have long wanted to see America-and here I am!"
"And a great help to us, if that is any comfort,"
she assured him. "This migration would have been
far harder without your aid."
"And harder for me," Michael retorted, "without
the good meals you serve. Ah! here come our water-
bearers, Mrs. Alden, and Mary looks happier than
she has for days.""Which is only because I let a good dinner-
several good dinners, in fact, escape us," explained
Luther, who had caught the Englishman's last words.
"Two fine young antelope came down to the stream
for water, so close that I could easily have potted one
of them had not this silly girl implored me to let
them go. You see, young man, how it weakens one's
character to have a daughter. Though I knew it was
wicked to let all that good fresh meat escape, I was
putty in her hands."
Mary laughed as she rubbed her cheek affection-
ately against Luther's coat sleeve.
"They were such pretty creatures I couldn't bear
to hurt them," she declared, emphatically. "And I'd
rather go hungry any time than see a wild thing
killed."
"And you, brought up on a farm!" teased Michael,
glad at hearing the girl laugh once more.
"But even as a child, Mary could never bear to
watch her father behead a chicken," declared Persis,
"though it's a proceeding which amuses most young-
sters. I'm thinking some fresh meat would have tasted
good, daughter; though like you, I deplore the un-
necessary killing of wild creatures. Dear me! Unless
I'm very much mistaken it is hotter than ever. This
Kansas sun seems never to go behind a cloud; and the
poor flowers are wilting on their stems."
She was to recall these words a few hours later when
the sun had set and a thunder shower descended in
such sudden fury that Michael Warwick was forced
to seek shelter within the prairie schooner, wherethey viewed the storm from the rear opening of their
canvas cover.
It was a sublime and awe-inspiring sight, there on
the wide plains. Vivid lightning played round them
on all sides; and for a time the crashing of thunder
was incessant. A pallid moon, appearing at intervals
from behind a cloud, but added to the weirdness of
the scene; and at times the wind blew with such
ferocity that the wagon rocked.
"It makes one ponder on the wrath of God," said
Luther solemnly, as a great forked lightning cleft the
sky.
"But why," asked Mary, "should we harp so much
upon God's wrath? Why not, instead, regard a sight
like this as one of His great miracles?"
Her eyes were dreamy, and the Englishman seated
at her feet, fought a desire to touch those capable
young hands, folded so tranquilly upon her knees.
"You're right, dear child," said Persis, her voice
gentle. "The fear of God will get one nowhere, while
love of Him will save a soul alive. Well" (with a
glance at the lifting clouds), "the worst is over, and
the rain ceasing; though where poor Michael will find
dry shelter for the night, I fail to see."
"Don't worry, Mrs. Alden," returned the boarder.
"My brain has been battling with that problem for
the last half hour. After the wind has dried things off
a bit I'll spread my rubber poncho beneath the
wagon, with your permission."
"Even so," Persis regretted, "it will be a damp
sleeping place after such a deluge. And I doubt if theshower has cooled the air. Even as soon as this the
wind feels hot and dry again. It may sound cowardly
in a pioneer, but truth is, I dread the morrow."
“
'Sufficient unto the day is the evil thereof,' " re-
proved her husband. Then added with a laugh as he
alighted from the wagon and peered beneath it: "It
looks as if you'd have a bedfellow to-night, Michael.
One of the oxen has got ahead of you!"F
CHAPTER XII
OR the first time since leaving New England,"
wrote Mary in her journal a few days after the
prairie storm, "I have neglected this charge of Parson
Gilder's-not from choice or laziness, but because I
have been too busy to set down a single word before
darkness made the task impossible; and we dare not
keep a fire burning for fear of attracting Indians
again. A half dozen or more unwelcome redskins
have continued to appear from time to time; and
though so far they have kept at a discreet distance,
and seem not unfriendly, we dread their pilfering
and begging, and must be constantly on guard against
worse depredations, which is very wearing.
"The dawn following our weird thunder storm
broke hot and muggy. Even at six o'clock the air was
stifling; and as we finished breakfast, Mother (who
had bravely kept her bad feelings to herself) gave
out completely, to our great alarm. There being no
cool shade nearby, Father and Mr. W. carried her to
the wagon and laid her on her bed. I then removed
her dress and loosened her stays, and when she had
revived a little, and the breakfast dishes (which our
boarder had kindly washed) were stacked away, we
decided it was better to continue our journey, since
even the slow moving of the wagon would seem lessbreathless to Mother than standing still under the
intolerable sun; but we made not more than eight or
ten miles the entire day.
"At noon, having come upon both water and some
welcome shade, it seemed wise to rest until the cool
of evening and then resume our way by moonlight.
Although M. W. had killed and cooked a fine, fat
rabbit, Mother was unable to eat at all; and I myself
so worried about her that I could scarcely swallow.
"Perhaps this was fortunate, as before we finished
our meal an Indian trader with a cargo of skins came
by. His wagon had been stuck for hours in a mud
hole a few miles west, and a sorry time he had ex-
perienced getting free of it, his only companion be-
ing a boy of eight or ten. The poor man begged for
food, and we gladly offered him the remains of our
repast, which they ate greedily.
"This Indian, who came from Santa Fé by way of
Cimarron Crossing, both understood and spoke Eng-
lish, and promised to give aid (should they require
it) to the poor young couple who lost their child.
Although he advised us not to go on that night for
fear of getting into the same mud that had mired
him, we decided that even a few miles' gain would be
better than nothing; so after an early supper Father
and Michael W. yoked up the oxen and we traveled
until we reached the fatal creek. The water was very
low, but a number of good trees grew nearby, and we
settled down gratefully to our night's rest.
"Alas! What a night's rest it was! As if learning
of our proximity in some mysterious manner, mosqui-toes arrived by thousands-nay, millions! Dear,
patient Father sat through the remaining hours of
night wielding a palm leaf fan in order that Mother
should get some needed sleep. Mr. W. did not even
pretend to go to bed; and I saw him pacing in the
moonlight as he fought the creatures until I fell into
a fitful slumber.
"Come morning Mother was better, but the rest of
us were a sorry sight indeed, having been almost
devoured by the wingéd pests. Michael Warwick and
I could scarcely eat breakfast for laughing at each
other, he having a swollen upper lip which (he de-
clared) made him resemble his Irish grandmother;
and my left eyelid being almost closed. Owing to his
night's vigil Father had fared somewhat better; but
Zebulon Pike was the only member of our party who
appeared unscathed.
"That day (yesterday) our boarder killed his first
buffalo! We had crossed the creek successfully (thank
heaven!), and gone on a mile or two, when four of
the great beasts appeared in the far distance. Michael
W. at once gave chase on Shamrock, but I was rather
glad we were not near enough to watch the hunt. As
it was, Mother and I were beginning to feel anxious
for the young man's safety, when we came upon him
standing in an attitude of victory beside his prey-
a ridiculous tableau arranged for our special benefit.
"Fortunately, the end of the chase occurred not far
from the trail; and as it was out of the question to
discard so much good meat, Father deemed it best to
camp for a day while our two men skinned the animaland prepared the meat for drying. This also gave an
opportunity for laundry work, which seems a luxury
on this migration.
"To-day has been cooler, and skies cloudy during
the afternoon. Mother is almost herself again, and
much ashamed (poor darling!) at having caused us
so much anxiety. She even begged to help with the
washing, but that I would not allow. We shall set
forth as early as possible to-morrow morning, and,
unless overtaken by rain, expect to reach Fort Wise
by sunset. This will be an interesting place to visit,
being much more commonly spoken of as 'Bent's
New Fort,' since it was built as a trading post by
William Bent, a trader who married the daughter of
a Cheyenne Chief. Only last year it was purchased by
the Federal Government and christened Fort Wise,
in honor of the Governor of Virginia. The original
Bent's Fort, which was on the Arkansas River some
forty miles west of this one, was destroyed in 1852 by
its owner during a fit of anger because the Govern-
ment was slow in meeting his terms! It is said that the
man loaded all his belongings on wagons and then
set fire to the fort in which he had left a quantity of
gun powder, so that the old trading post, famous for
many years, was completely demolished. I suppose we
shall pass its ruins later on.
"Have been half expecting the sad young couple
we left behind to catch up with us to-day, but no sign
of them yet; and we begin to wonder if they became
discouraged and turned back. It is too dark to see
what I am writing, and Michael (to save space I willin future mention our boarder by his first name) is
singing 'I Dreamt That I Dwelt in Marble Halls.'
I used to dream that very thing when I was young
and foolish; but it is far more probable that I shall
dwell hereafter in a mountain cabin!"
:R
CHAPTER XIII
AIN! Much as the gold seekers had yearned for
frequent showers during their dusty march,
they were unprepared for the drenching downpour
which greeted them next morning.
"We'll never reach Fort Wise to-day," regretted
Luther, when after a somewhat unsatisfactory break-
fast he peered from the rear of the big wagon. "The
sky is so dark I suppose we might as well move on;
though if there were any signs of the storm lifting,
Mother, I'd suggest tarrying here a while."
"But you and Michael will be wet to the skin if
you attempt to yoke up the oxen now," said Persis,
joining her husband in order to look out at the
dreary waste. "You must not risk taking more cold,
my dear, for with you laid low we would be at a
standstill. Let's be patient for another hour, Luther."
Mary, who was busily engaged in an endeavor to
bring order out of chaos in their crowded space,
turned her head to say: "Rain before seven, clear
before eleven, you know. I cast my vote with Mother;
for if we struck a mud hole in this deluge, Father,
what should we do?"
But the young Englishman, outside now in his
rubber poncho, was all for moving on.
"Though you're right in saying your husband mustnot risk a soaking, Mrs. Alden, and I can manage
without him. Surely it would be a satisfaction to be
moving forward instead of standing still; and as Miss
Mary says, the clouds may lift by noon."
Thus it was decided, though Luther made strenu-
ous objection to letting their boarder yoke up the
oxen without help. Only the fact that he had been
fighting a cough for several days, and the insistence
of his womenfolk that he keep dry, prevailed upon
the impatient farmer to give in; and at last they
were away, Shamrock (greatly to her disgust), tied to
the rear of the slowly moving wagon, while Michael
occupied the seat with Luther, his poncho spread
across their knees.
It was a long day, the longest and most dreary of
their whole migration. Plod-plod-plod. Slow mile
after slow mile. Nothing to hear save rain beating
against their shelter-an occasional gust of wind that
rocked the prairie schooner-the pad of oxen-feet
in the wet grass.
Crossing a small creek, the wagon stuck, but
(though the women exchanged despairing glances)
it was soon dislodged, and the monotonous hours
dragged on. By noon, true to the old adage, rain had
ceased falling; but the skies remained sullen, and
there was little conversation within the schooner.
Even Zebulon Pike seemed somewhat affected by the
prevailing atmosphere. He would doze for a time,
then rise to prowl about the narrow space, sniffing at
this and that-pausing at Mary's side for a light caress
-standing upon his hind legs to peer between theshoulders of the two men, sometimes uttering soft
whines as if puzzled that he was not allowed to run
outside.
It was mid-afternoon when, after passing the mouth
of some creek or river, the sight of two covered
wagons and several tents came slowly into view out
of the mist and fog. Apparently this caravan had
settled down for the night; and as they reached the
first tent (a large, commodious one), a woman came
to the open flap and hailed them cheerfully.
"Welcome, weary travelers!" she called. "Come in,
and let me give you a cup of tea."
Had Aladdin appeared with his wonderful lamp
and conjured refreshments out of the dark heavens,
the gold seekers from Massachusetts would not have
been more surprised; and as if he understood and
accepted the invitation, Zebulon Pike leapt joyously
between Michael and Luther Alden, and running
to their hostess, lifted his right paw in greeting.
"You beauty!" she exclaimed as she "shook hands"
with him; then turned to Michael, who now stood,
hat in hand, before her tent.
"Can you tell me, Madam, if we are far from Fort
Wise or Bent's Fort, whichever you may call it?" he
questioned.
"Too far for comfortable travel a day like this,
though in my opinion any distance is too far in bad
weather! I absolutely refused to break camp this
morning, and my Mexican drivers are ready to slay
me for the decision. Come! bring your family inside.
It is rare luck to have company this dreary after-noon; and when one is cold and tired what is more
cheering than a cup of tea?"
"When I tell you that I am English," laughed
Michael, "you will understand how heartily we
agree!" He turned to Luther, who had already
alighted, saying: "This is my good friend Luther
Alden, Madam, who with his wife and daughter have
traveled all the way from Massachusetts. My own
name is Warwick, if that matters. Come, Mrs. Alden"
(as Persis hesitated to descend), "a cup of tea will
warm you up, and as this lady says, it is really too bad
a day to travel farther."
A moment later they were all inside the tent, feel-
ing a bit bewildered as they regarded the interior. It
was most complete, conical in shape with an iron pole
in the center. A table was fitted to this pole, above
which on a small shelf lay toilet articles of finest
ivory. A portable bed at one side was furnished with
soft, thick Indian blankets, and many pillows. There
was even a covering of sail-duck as a floor; and fold-
ing stools with seats of bright red carpeting were set
about.
"Am I dreaming?" said Mary in an awed whisper,
as their hostess, going to the tent opening, called out:
"Manuel! Manuel! Bring tea-much tea and little
cakes. Be quick about it. Pronto! Our guests are
weary and need refreshment."
"How kind you are!" exclaimed Persis when the
woman turned to them again. "We are indeed weary,
and, I am ashamed to confess, a bit disheartened by
this long rainy day; so your hospitality is doublywelcome. And it's a pleasure to see how homelike you
have made your tent."
A light flashed into the younger woman's face at
these words of praise. She had thick, dark hair, parted
in the middle-blue eyes a bright, sweet smile.
"But, Mrs. Alden, I have lived in tents so much!
Truth is, this is a copy of one which was made in
Philadelphia by a tent-maker for the army. That was
in 1846 when, a young wife of twenty, I took my first
journey across the plains. The trip was even more
hazardous then than it is now; yet there was never
a day when I did not glory in the wild life on every
side, and the beauty of the prairies."
"But did you feel no fear?" asked Persis as she was
motioned to a seat upon the bed, "which does duty
as divan as well as sleeping place," the woman ex-
plained, smiling. "You spoke of fear?" She paused a
moment, as if trying to remember, and suddenly that
inner light brightened her face again. "No, with my
husband at my side, I can say truly that I felt no real
fear at any moment, though there were anxious days
when bands of roving savages appeared unruly. And
besides, we were part of a large caravan; Mr. Mac-
Tavish, my husband, being a trader to Santa Fé and
Mexico. Now I am alone, and taking the trip home
for the last time."
As she talked, Mrs. MacTavish was laying a white
cloth on the table, and lifting a vase of blossoms from
the shelf, she set it in the center of the snowy linen
as decoration.
"Not everyone can have such flowers for the pick-ing, can they? I recall how they enchanted me on my
first journey. You have a cough, Mr. Alden," she
went on, turning anxiously to Luther. "A bit later
I shall insist on dosing you with a syrup given me by
an aged squaw of Santa Fé. I don't know from what
herb it is concocted, but it works magic! Ah, here is
Manuel with our tea-piping hot, too, as he knows
I like it. Thanks, Manuel. Set the tray here. Mr.
Warwick, I'm sure, will be obliging enough to pass
the cups."
("I could think of nothing but a scene from
'Arabian Nights,'" wrote Mary in recording this
event. "To watch that lovely woman pouring tea out
of a tall brass teapot into cups of fragile china, and
to know that just beyond our canvas shelter was sod-
den prairie as far as eye could reach, and no real
civilization for miles and miles, was something never-
to-be forgotten.")
Perhaps they all felt a bit of this glamor, for
Michael, said gallantly, his eyes smiling as he lifted
a steaming cup of tea on high: "May we drink to
your health, fair conjurer of this desert trail?"
A rippling laugh escaped their hostess as she re-
sponded: "I could swear you are Irish, not English,
Mr. Warwick. Surely you have kissed the blarney
stone! But I'm no magician. That praise belongs to
Manuel and his young wife Maria, who have charge
of my cocina, or cook tent. To prove it, I hereby
ask the pleasure of your company at supper and
breakfast; for you must not go on to-night. No, don't
protest" (as Luther would have spoken). “Thinkwhat a delightful break this makes in my lonely
journey."
Her eyes pleaded; and it was so evident that the
invitation was sincere, that they could not refuse it
without rudeness.
"Do you think it would be foolish for me to put on
my new poplin?" asked Mary, when she and her
mother had retired to the wagon for a bit of fresh-
ening up before supper. "Somehow I feel untidy be-
side Mrs. MacTavish and her lovely furnishings.
What do you say?"
Her mother smiled, and being immeasurably
cheered at the prospect of a meal not prepared by her
own hands, responded: "I say that you're longing
for an excuse to wear your finery, my dear; and I
don't wonder. Besides, it would be a pretty compli-
ment to our hostess; and as the rain has ceased, and
there are even signs of a bright sunset, I can't see
how we could hurt our gowns by wearing them for a
few hours."
"Then you will dress up, too?" cried Mary, de-
lighted at the prospect; and in another moment was
on her knees, dragging the cowhide trunk from its
long hiding place.WHEN
CHAPTER XIV
HEN Michael Warwick called from without
that supper waited, and Mary, throwing
aside the canvas flap, appeared before him in her gay
poplin, its shoulder cape and billowing skirts edged
with black velvet bands, the young man uttered an
exclamation of delight which brought a blush to her
soft cheek.
"You should have prepared me for all this
grandeur, Mary Alden," he said in mock reproach.
"Such a shock does strange things even to a stolid
Britisher like myself. And your mother, too?" (as
Persis, attired in the gray wool, joined her daugh-
ter). "Surely 'Solomon in all his glory was not
arrayed like one of these.' Come, Mr. Alden. Here's
a refreshing sight for eyes grown tired of the prairie!"
A proud light brightened Luther's face as he drew
near, though he said with characteristic New England
caution: "Take care that you don't splash any mud
onto that gown, daughter. No knowing when you
can have another," at which Michael, with a glance at
the sodden earth, said quickly: "Allow me, please,"
and before Mary could reply had picked her up in
his strong arms, depositing her a moment later on the
sail-duck floor of the big tent.
Though he tactfully forbore to look at his sweetburden as he set it down, the girl's cheeks were still
crimson when the young man returned a moment
later with her protesting mother; while Mrs. Mac-
Tavish cried out in great amusement:
"But what a charming way to travel on a wet night!
I see that our Englishman surpasses in gallantry even
the famed Sir Walter! And I am proud indeed that
you should dress so beautifully for my little party.
Let us be seated without delay, for Manuel is nervous
fearing the sopa de vermicile will be chilled."
None there ever forgot that feast which began with
steaming cups of a vermicelli soup. It was a truly
Mexican repast: stewed chicken, carne de asado,
cibollas, chili, peppers, and tortillas over which Persis
exclaimed with so much pleasure that a smile bright-
ened the visage of dark-eyed Manuel.
"Your praise will repay Maria for the labor of
making them," declared their hostess, flashing a smile
of kindly understanding toward the serving man.
"And such labor it is! Never shall I forget the first
time I saw tortillas made, when the daughter of Don
Pedro, a neighbor of those old days in Santa Fé,
offered to teach me the art. The corn is soaked until
the husks are off, and placed, a handful at a time,
between mola stones till it is ground to a paste. It's
a fatiguing, back-breaking task, which I confess I
never had courage to attempt; yet no Mexican sup-
per seems complete to me without them."
This bountiful repast ended with a pudding con-
cocted of grapes and bread; cups of chocolate, casas,
and sweets which Mrs. MacTavish called "dulces."When the last crumbs had been cleared away, and
Manuel departed with a smile and a huge tray of
dishes, Michael Warwick asked: "Are all Mexicans
such perfect servants, Mrs. MacTavish? This man
seems unusually skilful about his work; and it's plain
to see that he's devoted to his mistress as well."
"You are right," replied the lady, tucking a pillow
behind Persis' back as she sat down beside her on the
divan. "Manuel is devotion itself, and perhaps with
cause. When a half-starved little lad not ten years
old, my husband spoke kindly to him one day in
passing, and that evening he appeared at our house
clothed only in rags, and begged us most piteously to
buy him! It transpired that some time before, the
Apache Indians had murdered his entire family,
and carried the child into captivity. The poor boy
was abused unmercifully, and at last escaped, taking
refuge in a wretched hut near the Rio Grande where
he stayed for a while under the protection of an old
Mexican whom he called 'compadre'-godfather. But
this life too was one of misery; and the Mexican, a
forlorn and ragged creature, was glad to part with
Manuel for a few dollars. Thus he became our serv-
ant and trusted friend."
"When one considers the life you saved him from,
it is small wonder that he's devoted to you, Madam,"
said Luther gravely. "May I ask how often you have
taken this long trip?"
"Five times in fourteen years I have gone back
for a visit to my native state, Missouri. While my
husband was with me I never tired of the journey;but now, though I am to make my home henceforth
with a beloved sister, the way seems long, and my
heart turns back to the lonely grave I have left be-
hind."
There was a silence for a moment until Michael
asked: "Did you find many people of your own sort
in Santa Fé, Mrs. MacTavish?"
A soft, light laugh came from their hostess.
"Sometimes," she answered, "I wonder just what
my own sort is! Reared by an aristocratic southern
grandmother-married at nineteen to a wealthy
trader-meeting the great and near-great wherever
we have lived, yet I am quite as happy in company
with my faithful Manuel and his young wife, and
felt as at home in our first humble dwelling of four
rooms (which stood in the very shadow of the chapel
with its ever chiming bells), as in the palace of the
Governor! And many of our humblest neighbors at
Santa Fé were dearer to me than all the others, Mr.
Warwick."
"I have long dreamed of visiting that ancient city,
which, I believe, was well established before the Pil-
grims landed on Plymouth Rock," said Luther Alden,
much to the astonishment of his wife and daughter.
"Tell me, was the conquest of New Mexico really as
bloodless as we were led to think?"
"It was indeed. And most wisely, the Americans
have not tampered with the religion of the natives.
For my part, I feel confident that the City of Holy
Faith will always retain its Spanish atmosphere to a
great degree; and that when you and I, my friend,are only a memory, the faithful will still be sum-
moned to Mass by those chiming bells in the twin
towers of la iglesia."
Night was descending on the prairie now; and as
the shadows deepened Manuel appeared once more,
placing a lighted lantern on the shelf above the little
table. Its dim glow fell on Mary's upturned face as
she leaned forward eagerly, lost to the present, and
as unmindful of the picture she was making as of the
fact that all eyes were resting on her, refreshing them-
selves at sight of such unconscious beauty.
"Can you not tell us about the famous trading
post that William Bent destroyed?" she questioned.
"Surely you must have stopped there in your travels,
Mrs. MacTavish."
"Indeed I have! We stayed there an entire week
during my second trip across the plains, I being
stricken with a severe cold at the time. Fortunately
there was a doctor on hand, a polite Frenchman,
Parmentier by name, who had emigrated to St. Louis
some years before and was on his way to Mexico."
"Did you find comfortable accommodations in
such surroundings?" asked Persis curiously.
"At least, they were reasonably safe accommoda-
tions, Mrs. Alden, which was something for which to
be thankful. The fort was a huge place, one hundred
by one hundred and fifty feet or more in dimensions,
with high adobe walls full six feet thick. It stood
on a level bluff which sloped gently to the river-
an impressive sight there on the lonely plains."
"Were there bastions for defense?" asked Michaelwith something of an effort, to be sure, for not once
had his eyes left Mary's face, and he found it difficult
to pay attention to the conversation.
Mrs. MacTavish nodded thoughtfully, as if trying
to recall the picture.
"There were circular towers at two corners; and
a square watch tower above the entrance, always
occupied, I believe. Inside was a large, square space,
in the center of which stood a press for packing furs;
while opening out of this square were rooms, all with
dirt floors which had to be sprinkled many times a
day. There was a large dining room, kitchen, parlor,
bedrooms, and even a blacksmith shop! On the south
side was a corral for horses and cattle, used when
there was fear of an Indian uprising; but it served
more commonly as a gambling place for leather-clad
mountaineers, and was also a convenient spot for the
employees (among whom were St. Louis Frenchmen
and Canadian voyageurs) to beat out and pack great
piles of buffalo skins. I recall also a well of delicious
drinking water within the enclosure; and being very
young, and very good at the time," she added, her
eyes lighting with amusement, "I was greatly shocked
to discover a billiard room where the gentlemen
spent far too many hours!"
"Do you really mean that those busy traders and
trappers had time for play?" asked Michael with a
laugh.
"Much time, apparently, if it is possible to judge
from the popularity of that billiard table! And their
wives (the wives of soldiers and employees), satabout in the large parlor, an apartment which had
cushions for seats, laid upon the floor close to the
walls; and a center table to hold a pail of water and
a cup. I remember that much to my girlish embar-
rassment, some of las señoritas would comb their
hair in this parlor, even anointing it with oil from
a crock in one corner, regardless of the fact that
gentlemen were present."
The two young people broke into irrepressible
laughter at this picture; while Luther Alden, smiling
himself, observed: "If your sleeping room opened
out of this court yard, Madam, it was hardly an ideal
spot in which to lodge a sick woman."
"True, Mr. Alden, for that blacksmith shop alone
was a noisy place where wagons were repaired daily.
But fortunately, our bedroom was one of the upper
chambers, built along the side of the top wall with
windows facing both the plains and the patio. There
were parapets of adobe all along the roof, and on
the coping cactus plants in many varieties were
growing. Our room had only a dirt floor, of course,
but it was not a bad place to stay by any means;
and the laughter of little children playing in the
square below was a happy sound."
"Children!" echoed Mary in much surprise. "At
that old trading post!"
"A goodly number, my dear, mostly half-breeds,
belonging to the employees and their squaw wives.
While we were there Colonel Kearney himself ar-
rived, and the fort was crowded. I also recall a morn-
ing when three Indian warriors appeared, Arapahoes,thought to be spies; but evidently they were awed
by the military encampment, for no trouble ensued.
And one pathetic sight, which lingers with me, was
watching from my small window the body of a
soldier, who had died in the night, carried to its
burial place in the lonely sand hills."
"It seems curious that the owner of such a trading
post should have destroyed it ruthlessly," observed
Michael. "I take it he was a gentleman of high tem-
per! Is the fort that he built later, which we expect
to pass tomorrow, as large a place?"
"Very nearly. It is made of stone and adobe; and,
like the original structure, there are two bastions
which contain small cannon. Yes, it was a foolish
waste when William Bent blew up such a successful
trading post. I've heard that trading parties with
pack mules used to start out from there and wend
their way among the different tribes, sometimes col-
lecting a fortune in furs during a single day. Those
were stirring times."
"They were indeed," said Persis, rising with reluc-
tance. "I would gladly listen to these fascinating
tales all night, Mrs. MacTavish; but as we must make
an early start to-morrow, I dare say it's wiser to get
some sleep."
"How I wish we were traveling in the same direc-
tion!" sighed their hostess as they moved together
toward the opening in the tent. "We breakfast at
seven a simple meal, for much of Maria's cooking
equipment will be packed to-night. I hope my Mexi-
cans will not rouse you too early. They are a noisylot when preparing to break camp. Ah!" she ex-
claimed, peering out into the night, "how beauti-
fully the skies have cleared!"
For an appreciable space the little group gazed up
at countless stars which spangled the dark above.
It was Luther Alden who said reverently: "Surely,
'the heavens declare the glory of God, and the firma-
ment showeth His handiwork.' " And after a moment
in which they all stood silent: "We must say good-
night, Madam. You may be sure we shall look back
upon this evening as one of the brightest of our long
march."T
CHAPTER XV
RUE to Mrs. MacTavish's prediction, the camp
was aroused next morning by a great commo-
tion: loud calls, and what sounded suspiciously like
curses from the men and drivers preparing to set out
on the long trail.
"Hola los animal, cómo estande bravos!” shouted
one swarthy Mexican as an impatient mule switched
a tail across his eyes. The words seemed not short
of brutal to Persis Alden; but Mrs. MacTavish ex-
plained laughingly that they meant only: "Ho, ani-
mal! How wild you are!"-and that despite the
gruffness of his voice José was as tender-hearted as
a child, and made great pets of the beasts he cared
for.
"I recall a trip when the streams were dry and we
were forced to travel many miles without water. One
of our oxen, weak from thirst, fell by the road and
my husband gave him up for good. But not José!
He filled a bucket with mud from a mud hole and
plastered it over the poor creature's body; then made
a sort of tent of ox yokes and a blanket, to protect it
from the burning sun. Yet even these tender minis-
trations did not satisfy the good fellow, for he in-
sisted that each man drink his fill from the water
kegs, and give what was left (pitifully little, I fear)
ΙΟΙto the suffering beast, which in the course of a few
hours was able to struggle on until we reached a
stream."
The "simple breakfast" which had been promised,
seemed most elaborate to the New Englanders, for
despite noise and confusion on every side, Manuel
served them with ceremony. And not long after
(finding their oxen yoked up and ready for the
journey), they bade their hostess a reluctant fare
well, moving slowly westward as her own caravan
turned to the east.
"It's not often one meets with such warm-hearted
friendliness," declared Persis a while later as she ex-
plored the depths of a huge basket which Manuel
had placed in their wagon at the last moment. “Look,
Mary! Here are all sorts and kinds of Mexican deli-
cacies; and a quantity of the tortillas I enjoyed so
much at supper." Then as the oxen came to an
unexpected halt, she called: "What's the matter,
Luther? Has something happened?" and before her
husband could reply, went forward to join him;
while Michael Warwick appeared at the rear opening
of the canvas where sat Mary, gazing back over the
plains in an endeavor to catch a last glimpse of the
departing caravan.
"Step down, Miss Mary, and turn your face to the
west."
There was suppressed excitement in his voice
which the girl was quick to catch; and hearing an
exclamation from her mother at that moment, she
cried, startled: "Not Indians, I hope!"By way of answer the Englishman reached up as
he had done the night before, swung her lightly to
the ground, and pointed to the far horizon where,
soft, blue, hazy as if a thin veil covered them, the
outline of distant peaks was plainly visible.
"Mountains?" breathed Mary, unbelieving. "The
Shining Mountains?"
She turned, looking up into the young man's face
as a child might look for corroboration of a great
hope; and sudden emotion taking away his power
of speech, Michael could only nod in the affirmative.
When after a moment he found his voice, it was to
say: "A short way back they appeared so like a small
blue cloud that I dared not mention my suspicions
for fear of disappointing you. Those heights must be
the outposts of the Rockies, Miss Mary. The Huer-
fano Mountains-possibly the Spanish Peaks, which
Mrs. MacTavish told me the Indians call Wa-ha-
toy-ha. It may be that Pike's Peak itself is in the
background. We'll soon find out. But if you're really
as thrilled now as your face tells me, how will you
feel when that historic mountain rises before us?"
They had not long to wait, for in less than an
hour (perhaps because the air was crystal-clear) the
majestic Peak was in full view across the plains.
"I don't know whether to cheer or cry," confessed
the girl as they halted once more, feeling, perhaps,
that when standing still they could more fully realize
the welcome sight. She had been riding beside her
mother on the wagon seat (Luther having expressed
a desire to "stretch his legs" for a few miles), butnow she sprang lightly down to join the English-
man, who had dismounted, and stood with an arm
across Shamrock's glossy neck.
"Why not laugh, instead?" he questioned, and
Mary answered:
"I-I think I could not laugh just now even for
joy." Her lifted eyes were as shining as the moun-
tains. Then she burst out impulsively: "Oh, it was
good of you not to ride on ahead, Michael Warwick!
I was afraid you would, for the temptation to see
our mountain first must have been overpowering;
but, though it sounds selfish, I should have been
green with envy if you had!"
"There was no danger!" Looking down into her
earnest face the Englishman was forced to clasp his
hands behind him to keep them out of mischief as
he continued, bending his head so that the elders
should not hear him: "Aren't you aware, my dear,
that the first sight of that gorgeous peak would have
meant nothing to me-nothing at all without you
to share it?"
"Come, come!" cried Luther, who, as Fate would
have it, glanced toward them at that very moment.
"Those mountains won't run away, daughter. They'll
grow larger and more impressive with every step.
Let us push on."
"That's right, sir," agreed Michael, as if to loiter
was the last thing he desired; but his eyes were bright
with veiled amusement as he helped the girl into her
seat, and she was obliged to draw her hand away
from his possessive clasp."At last! We have seen the great Peak in the dis-
tance!" ran the next page of Mary's diary. "It seems
almost a lifetime since we bade farewell to Mrs.
MacTavish, after partaking of breakfast (and such
a breakfast!) in her spacious tent. She is a wonder-
ful woman, over-flowing with human kindness; and
as we told her about the young couple who had lost
their baby, she promised to keep watch for them
along the trail, though I doubt not they have long
since turned back.
"We were, it transpired, not many miles below
Fort Wise, which looked almost familiar with the
minute description given us by Mrs. MacTavish fresh
in our minds. There was much fine grass in that
vicinity, but as we continued on it grew noticeably
scanty, and our way led over high and broken coun-
try, rimmed on the northwest by mountains.
"In the afternoon we were overtaken by a sudden
thunder storm which lasted so long that it delayed
our progress seriously. Thus despite an early start we
made poor mileage; and to-day was not much better,
a broken axle stopping us for two hours or longer,
while Father and Michael did the repairing. But
since sunrise the weather has been perfect, and I can
scarcely keep my eyes away from those distant peaks.
The sight thrills me as nothing else has even the
prairies, perhaps because I realize that we are near-
ing our goal. Surely the atmosphere is clearer than
any I have ever seen. The air is like wine.
"We were told at Fort Wise that our road leads
past a trading post where one can buy corn and vege-tables; but I imagine the prices would be exorbi-
tant, since (according to a traveler we talked with
near the Fort) they sometimes charge as much as a
dollar a dozen for apples! This seems incredible to
us New Englanders who have, often enough, fed
them to the pigs!
"On this last lap of our journey I look forward to
the meeting with my Uncle Daniel. Will he, I won-
der, be there to greet us if we reach Auraria or
Mountain City in safety? I am much excited at the
prospect, and feel sure he is looking for us even
now.
"Michael Warwick has made friends with a baby
prairie dog, which he has (rather audaciously, I
think) named Victoria, in honor of his Queen! He
swears to take her with him as a mascot; and despite
the fact that she is named for Royalty, the little
creature sleeps quite comfortably in his coat pocket!
"Our visit with Mrs. MacTavish, combined with
this beautiful, clear air, has had a reviving effect
upon us all. Even the oxen (two of which Father,
who seems to have caught our boarder's passion for
naming things, now calls Kit Carson and Daniel
Boone) seem to move less sluggishly; and Zebulon
Pike dashes madly across the plains chasing prairie
dogs or an occasional jack rabbit, though with small
success. As for Shamrock, she dances like a well-
trained circus horse-a pretty sight. If I had a side-
saddle I should be tempted to mount her and ride
ahead on a solitary exploration. Met a large band of
Indians this morning, but though they surroundedthe wagon in a rather terrifying manner, and begged
for food as usual, they did us no harm.
"Michael has just interrupted me, saying that I
have written enough for one while, and will I ex-
plore a nearby mound before it grows too dark? So
I will close this record for another day. In fact, our
young man complains that it takes all my spare time;
but I dare not neglect it, fearing that in my delight
over the wonders at hand I may forget those we
have passed by. A gorgeous sunset portends another
bright day to come.":

CHAPTER XVI
HE "nearby mound" proved to be farther off
than the young people had thought, both
being deceived by the clear atmosphere; yet a vivid
afterglow still lingered when at last they stood there,
thrilled by the sky, the wide, still scene, the distant
mountains.
"Those Spanish Peaks might be great, crouching
sentinels," said Michael. "There's something almost
mysterious about such mighty crags."
"Who would have dreamed that I would live
to see such things!" murmured the girl. “Oh,
Michael"
She stopped abruptly, confused at having used the
young man's Christian name, while he responded
with a laugh: "I have been wondering how long
even a demure New Englander like yourself could
continue calling her daily companion Mr. Warwick.
Confess now, hasn't it been some strain? As for my-
self, if I must go on forever saying Miss Mary, when
I think of you always as”
"As what?" she questioned, a bit surprised at her
own temerity.
"Ah, don't you wish you knew?" teased Michael,

"On second thoughts," countered the girl, tryingto suppress a smile, but wholly unable to suppress a
dimple, “I am not interested."
"In that case I'll not bore you with the informa-

we reach the gold fields, which at times looks doubt-
ful."
"And I took you for a strong character!" bantered
Mary, enjoying herself immensely at this new game.
"You will see how strong I am when we arrive at
those Shining Mountains!" boasted the young man.
"When I have torn a fortune from their rocky sides
by aid of pick axe, I'll deposit my golden nuggets at
the feet of a lady to be named later, and with"
"It would be far more sensible to deposit them
in a good savings bank," proffered his companion

tion."
The youthful adventurer met her glance with
reproachful eyes.
"You appear a heartless creature, Mary Alden; but
I know you better than you suspect, and the tell-

veals"
"Blush!" broke in the girl with spirit. "If I have
more color in my face than usual it's merely the

"Our?" questioned the girl, with lifted eyebrows.
"Since when, Mr. Warwick, have you held a mort-
gage on my trinkets?"
"There being no Mr. Warwick present, how can he
reply to you?" asked Michael, quite unperturbed. "I
dislike intensely to end this tête-à-tête; but it will
soon be dark, and we must return to camp or your
mother will worry."
"It's Father-poor dear!-who will do the worry-
ing," corrected Mary; while for a brief moment their
glances met in mutual laughter and understanding.
"But a blind man could see that you're the treas-
ure of his heart," said Michael, resuming the sub-
ject as they made their way, none too rapidly, toward
the prairie schooner. "If I'm not mistaken, that con-
scientious sire of yours is scanning the landscape for
you even now; yet as Shamrock is tethered to the
lone cottonwood, he can hardly suspect me of abduct-
ing you!"
Mary laughed softly.
"Dear Father! He can never quite believe that I'm
grown up. To him I am still the little girl who used
to dream of doing the very things we are doing now.I think if it had not been for me and my longing to
get beyond New England's hills and valleys, he might
never have gathered courage to break away from the
farm that's been home to the Aldens for genera-
tions."
"Yet I could swear he has enjoyed every mile of
the long way," said Michael.
"Of course he has! This is my Father's great adven-
ture the one adventure of his whole life. Uncle
Dan once told me that if he hadn't run away, and
had been forced to lead the life that Father has, he
would have landed in jail! I'm not sure but that he
regarded the old home as a sort of prison, much as
he loved it; and his occasional visits were the most
thrilling thing in life to us."
"Even to your mother?"
For a space Mary was silent; then answered
thoughtfully: "Yes, even to Mother. You see, my
uncle brought with him glimpses of far-off ports and
places which she never expected to see with her own
eyes. I have always thought of him as a sort of fairy
prince, and his life a fairy tale. Why, he sailed on
two of the most famous clippers, Flying Cloud, and
Sovereign of the Seas! You should hear him tell of
the voyage in '51, for in the South Pacific, off Val-
paraiso, the ship was dismantled in a gale and they
were one hundred and two days reaching San Fran-
cisco."
"I'd like nothing better than to hear those sto-
ries," observed Michael. "Do you think you could
persuade him to tell them again?"This question brought a laugh to the girl's lips.
"It won't take much persuading! Did you ever
know a traveler who wasn't eager to talk of his ad-
ventures? Even I am not entirely exempt, and can
hardly wait to tell our experiences to Uncle Daniel,
or anybody who will listen, for that matter! Here we
are, Father, safe and sound. Did you think that your
one wee lamb was lost on the great plains?" she
teased, smiling up at him.
They had reached the wagon, and Luther, who
had in truth been scanning the growing darkness
anxiously, replied: "What if I did? The night comes
quickly on these prairies, child, and you were gone
some time."
"Only because that mound is farther off than one
would think from here, sir," explained Michael;
while Mary added: "We expected to reach it in five
minutes, and it took all of twenty. I believe there is
something exhilarating in this air, Father, for I'm
wide awake when by rights I should be sleepy after
the day's march. Give us a song, Michael, before we
say good-night. I need a lullaby."
"I second that request," urged Persis, who was rest-
ing on a blanket spread out upon the short, brown
grass. "I, too, feel wakeful, and have been wondering
if the stars are nearer to us here than they are at
home. They look so bright they may keep you from
sleeping, Michael, if you camp in the open. Come,
sing for us, do something quiet and soothing."
As Persis spoke she moved to make room for them
upon the blanket, while her husband, who preferreda wagon seat to the ground, was regarding the stars
from that lofty place when the Englishman began
softly:
"Flow gently, sweet Afton, among thy green braes!
Flow gently, I'll sing thee a song of thy praise!
My Mary's asleep by thy murmuring stream-
Flow gently, sweet Afton, disturb not her dream."
Was it with foresight that Persis Alden had turned
her back upon the others? True it is that she did not
see young Michael Warwick reach for her daughter's
hand-and keep it; and when the song went on:
"How pleasant thy banks and green valleys below,
Where wild in the woodland the primroses blow....'"
the girl's father saw only a familiar brook that rip-
pled through a bit of woodland on the home farm,
and for a moment was filled with sharp nostalgia
for sight of those fertile meadows in the valley where
cowslips lavished a gold more precious than any to
be dug from mountains; and violets.

Long after the singer's voice had died away, the
four still sat there, each lost in dreams of his own
making until roused by the eerie wail of a coyote on
a distant sandhill.M
CHAPTER XVII
IDSUMMER, and early morning on the prai-
rie! Mary Alden slipped quietly from the
wagon while her parents still slumbered, and drew
a deep breath of the dry, clear air. How beautiful
was that wide expanse with its rim of mountains!
And how beautiful was life, she mused, stirred by
a quick sense of happiness, when one is young, and
strong, with years and years ahead!
Mary moved a space, enchanted by the loveliness
on every side. Surely those heights looked nearer in
the morning than they had looked at sundown. And
close at hand, lighting the brown grasses with a
splash of scarlet, a clump of painter's brush flaunted
its gay banner boldly, as if to make amends for a
belated flowering.
When a half hour later the girl returned from
bathing in the cold waters of the creek, she found
the camp astir, and Michael Warwick building a fire
not far away. Remembering how possessively the
young man had clasped her hand last evening, an
unaccustomed shyness swept over her and she would
have sought shelter within the wagon for a moment,
had he not heard her step and called over his shoul-
der: "A great morning! Did our early bird capture
a worm?"Mary laughed, all shyness vanishing as he spoke.
"I trust her chirps didn't disturb your beauty
sleep," she countered, drawing nearer.
"Far from it! I was awake at dawn. You should
have seen those peaks tipped with the sunrise glow,
Mary. They were pink as coral, and made me want
to write a poem! There, the fire starts! If you'll bring
a skillet I'll cook the bacon. Perhaps if we hurry we
can serve breakfast as soon as your parents return
from their morning dip."
A smile curved Mary's lips as she responded: "It
would require courage to take much of a dip in that
icy water! I think the streams grow colder as we ap-
proach the hills. Let's have only a light breakfast,
Michael, and be on our way as soon as possible. I
feel all the sensations of a pioneer this morning, and
can hardly wait to start."
As this same impatience seemed to pervade them
all, the wagon soon moved westward, making good
time, for they nooned near the mouth of Huerfano
Creek. Not far from here stood a Mexican pueblo,
a flour mill, and the promised trading post where
Michael Warwick purchased a dozen eggs, refusing
to say what they had cost him, though he observed
with humor that keeping hens in this new country
might be an easier way to accumulate a fortune than
by digging it from hills! Sunset found them at a
point where the road forked, the Cherokee Trail
leaving the river and bearing to the right. For a
while there was some discussion as to route; but at
last the left hand branch was decided on since theyhad been advised that it led to an old Pueblo (called
Fountain City) at the mouth of the Fountaine qui
Bouille, where they would find a number of Ameri-
cans.
"We continued on our way some miles farther be-
fore camping," wrote Mary the following afternoon,
"and made an early start this morning, reaching the
Pueblo (a dozen or more adobe houses) in good
time. Have decided to pass the night in this vicinity,
since it seems good to mingle with other people for
a few hours."
"I am sorry to discover that you've been so bored
by your traveling companions," observed Michael,
who had been reading over the girl's shoulder as she
wrote. "And if you found the hairy specimen who
was making eyes at you a while since, more to your
taste than present company, I will endeavor to grow
a pair of whiskers. You really appeared spellbound
by that venerable grizzly bear."
"I was," laughed Mary, closing her book, for it was
impossible to go on writing with the young man so
near. "He is an old trader, and was telling me about
an Indian uprising which occurred only a few years
back at this very spot. It was on Christmas day, and
a party of trappers were celebrating here at the
Pueblo when a band of Utes descended on them,
killed every man, and carried a woman named Maria
Sandoval (the only white woman at the post) into
captivity, where she died a short time later."
"A pretty story!" commented Michael."The old man said it was a ghastly fight; only the
two small sons of the white woman escaping. There
was pillaging, of course, and fire; and until the gold
rush started two years ago this place has been de-
serted. It is the site of an old Indian camping
ground."
"You make shivers run up my spine," said
Michael; "but I see now how wise you were to en-
courage the attentions of that chimpanzee. I think
I'll make his acquaintance myself, a trifle later. Put
down that everlasting diary, my dear, and rest your
eyes with this mountain panorama. It covers, they
tell me, more than a hundred miles."
"If I put down this book every time you asked me
to," complained the girl, "it would work havoc with
the solemn charge of Parson Gilder. I wish you knew
that dear old man, Michael. He is a saint on earth;
and you'd love him dearly."
The young man grinned.
"Would the aged parson reciprocate my passion,
do you think?"
"You are utterly impossible," replied Mary coldly,
though the tell-tale dimple came into view. "Are you
never serious?"
"Often, Madam; and would be more so if your
father had not recently developed an uncanny sense
which warns him when the mood is descending on
me. Here he comes now, sniffing danger, and ready
to throw out the life line. Why are you laughing,
Mary Alden? I am serious enough this minute for
even you.""What is the joke, daughter?" asked Luther cheer-
fully as he drew near; but as Mary merely continued
laughing, Michael Warwick said:
"I suspect the joke is either myself, or you, Mr.
Alden; and should be interested to know which. If
your daughter deigns to confide in you, I hope you
will pass along the information to a suffering fellow
man. Now I must go and introduce myself to that
grizzly trader in the sombrero who was so fortunate
as to hypnotize this young lady for a full hour. I
thought at first that the fascination was due to his
abundant whiskers, but find instead that it was the
ancient's gory reminiscences."
"Our boarder seems to be in uncommonly good
spirits," remarked Luther, as Michael moved toward
the Pueblo. Then, as the girl remained silent, eyes
following the Englishman, her father added: "Has he
reason to feel especially light-hearted, daughter?"
Though she enjoyed teasing him, Mary reached
over to give his hand a reassuring squeeze.
"How should I know? Perhaps this scenery and the
invigorating air have gone to Michael's head. He is
a dear, Father; and what should we have done with-
out him on the long trek? Do you realize that not
once has he been irritable or out of patience?"
"For that matter," Luther reminded her, "he's
been no more forbearing than the rest of us. Only
yesterday your mother was commenting on the fact
that despite many discomforts along the way, there
has been no friction among us. But I suppose you are
right, Mary. The boy has something very takingabout him, and a quick wit; but-go slowly, my dear.
There must be many fine young gold seekers in those
mining towns. Go slowly."
Having done his duty by divesting himself of
this fatherly advice, Luther was overtaken by embar-
rassment; for it is hard for one reared among the
silent New England hills to express his feelings.
Thus, before Mary could reply he arose and strode
hurriedly away, as if important matters awaited his
attention. His daughter could not restrain a smile as
she watched him go; and when Michael returned a
short time after, latent amusement still lingered in
her eyes.
"Did you miss my nice old trader?" she inquired,
as the young man dropped down beside her on the
grass.
Michael nodded, his gaze on the far hills.
"The ancient had gone into his hole, or his den,
or his pueblo, or wherever he resides. Anyway, he
was not in sight; and your mother was having a good
gossip with a lady from Kentucky, on her way to the
gold fields in search of a missing husband. She has
been ill, and is stopping here to rest a while. Well,
my dear" (turning his eyes suddenly on Mary), "did
your father deliver his timely warning?"
"You sha'n't make fun of my darling father," the
girl retorted, flushing. "There are not many like
him. He is the sort, you know, who could not sleep
if he neglected what seemed his duty."
"Forgive me," said Michael quickly, and touched
her hand. "Sometimes, Mary, my sense of humor runsaway with my sense of propriety; but down under-
neath I admire your father as much as you do, and
know he is quite right. Tell me, would he be hap-
pier if I were to linger here a day or two and let you
continue on alone?"
Something like consternation clutched Mary
Alden's heart at this proposition. In fact, she dared
not look at her companion as she replied: "Why not
ask Mother? Here she comes now."
They both watched Persis approaching across the
grass. She stepped lightly, like a girl; and Michael,
smiling at her, would have stripped off his coat for
her to sit on, had not the woman stopped him with
a gesture.
"This breeze is cool, Michael, and you need a coat
more than I need a cushion. My dears, you should
have heard that awful woman! I was obliged to break
away or scream. Such tales as she related, and would
have gone on forever, I believe."
"What sort of tales?"
"Things that have happened to travelers like our-
selves. I could feel the hair rise on my scalp."
Both young people burst into laughter at this con-
fession; and Michael said: "In that case, you'd better
relieve that unpleasant sensation, Mrs. Alden, by
telling us the gruesome details."
"Yes, do!" urged Mary. "Our curiosity is aroused;
and the stories can't be much worse than those that
nice old trader told to me. Go on, Mother. Did these
frightful things happen to the woman herself?"
"No; but her sister, who crossed the plains in Maylast year, sent back a letter. She went by way of the
Leavenworth and Pike's Peak Express Company, in
a Concord coach which at that time followed a
