route near the Republican River. It seems there are
stations along the way where they stop for meals.
One of these places which they approached after
dark, was lighted only by candles; and as this woman
entered the room she saw, on a rude couch in one
corner, something she took to be a spirit from the
unknown world."
"She must have been a credulous female," observed
Michael.
"This is no laughing matter," reproved Persis.
"The poor creature was a gold seeker and literally
a living skeleton, whom an Indian had discovered
along the route and brought in, since (in the
woman's words) there was still life in the starving
corpse."
"Had he tried to cross the plains alone?" asked
Mary as her mother paused for breath.
Persis shook her head.
"The worst is to come. He had, that very day,
found strength to gasp out his story. It seems he was
one of three brothers who started from Illinois driv-
ing a one-horse cart. They went, Mary, by the route
your uncle warned us about the Smoky Hill
Trail"
"The very one I nearly took myself," broke in
Michael suddenly.
"It's fortunate you changed your mind," said
Persis. "My brother-in-law wrote that there was littlewater along the way. These poor young men left
home with insufficient provisions. They had not gone
half way when their food gave out. What did they
do? Killed their good horse, Michael, and ate it!"
"How ghastly!" exclaimed the Englishman, with
a glance at his beloved Shamrock grazing nearby.
"Nor was that all. They loaded their cart with the
poor beast's carcass and took turns pulling it over
the rough trail. Naturally, their strength went all too
soon, and, the last of the slaughtered animal being
devoured, they lay down to die. Having lost the trail
in a search for water, how could they hope to be
overtaken and rescued by some more fortunate trav-
eler? One brother who was weaker than the others,
begged them with almost his last breath, to live upon
his flesh and endeavor to get through."
"Oh, no!" protested Mary. "They-they couldn't
have done that!"
"But they did, daughter, and struggled on. A sec-
ond brother died, and was eaten by the survivor,
who, unable to find his way, sank down in delirium
when a roving Indian stumbled upon him by chance,
and brought him to the station."
"Did the man live?"
"Yes; but only to go insane-poor fellow! I'm
ashamed to have repeated such a story; but as you
say, it is better to get it off my mind, though I'll
reproach myself if it keeps either of you awake
to-night."
"Don't worry," comforted Mary. "It should make
us thankful that our own journey has been sparedsuch horrors. Nevertheless, it strikes me that since
we are traveling alone, your new acquaintance was
hardly tactful to repeat a tale of that sort."
"Tactful!" echoed her mother. "I should say not!
She seemed to gloat upon the horrid details; and
would still be telling them, I think, if I had not
made an excuse to get away."
"Perhaps there is a good reason for her husband
being among the missing," observed Michael dryly.
A laugh came from the girl: but as Persis forbore
to smile, Mary exclaimed: "Cheer up, Mother! With
the mountains in sight our trip seems almost at an
end. It is but two days' journey at the most, that old
man tells me, till we reach Colorado City, a thriving
town with at least two hundred buildings."
"And there are splendid camps along the way,"
amended Michael. "Pine timber, as well as grass and
water. Which reminds me, Mrs. Alden, would you
people prefer to drop your boarder here, and go on
your way alone?"
Persis, who had been gazing at the Spanish Peaks,
turned quickly in surprise.
"Is our boarder tired of his fare?" she questioned.
"You know better! But-" The young man hesi-
tated a moment and then said: "Truth is, I have
thought at times that your husband might like to
have his family to himself again."
A light of understanding sprang into Persis Alden's
eyes, and a smile to her lips.
"You're mistaken, Michael; and unless you would
feel freer and happier by yourself, we are glad ofyour company. You must not think that Mr. Alden
is inhospitable. He ..."
She paused, her eyes on Mary's averted face; and
Michael said quite soberly: "I understand. But will
you ask him about it frankly, Mrs. Alden? If he pre-
fers, I'll willingly stay behind, or ride on ahead. And
with no hard feeling."
"Of course, I'll ask him if you wish," Persis agreed,
"but don't worry, my dear boy. If you're not with us
when we set out to-morrow, it won't be because we
do not want you. That I can promise."I
CHAPTER XVIII
T was with a full heart that Mary penned the next
entry in Parson Gilder's leather-bound book; for
true to the promise of her "grizzled conquest" (as
Michael designated the old trader at the Pueblo),
two days of easy travel brought them to Colorado
City, just as the sun was setting behind Pike's Peak.
She wrote: "The mountains are glorious from here
-so near it seems that one might reach out and
touch them! I never imagined such grandeur; and
though some seventy odd miles still lie before us,
we are filled with a sense of almost immeasurable
thankfulness at having come so far in safety. The trip
from Fountain City was uneventful, save that the
scenery grew more magnificent with every hour. I
was so thrilled I could easily have wept at times, had
not Michael, sensing my mood in some uncanny
manner, insisted on making absurd remarks that
turned my impending tears to laughter.
"To-morrow will be the first of August, and even
should we linger here a day (something our boarder
is urging, since he's set on visiting the famous soda
spring), we should reach Auraria within the week
unless bad luck befalls us on the remainder of our
journey, which God forbid!
"Five months, lacking only a few days, since weleft the farm, and what long, long miles we have
come since! How well I recall that early morning
start the oxen picking their cautious way down the
steep hillside road-my father's solemn face as he
walked beside them-the dew-wet grasses sparkling
in the sunlight-a bluebird that seemed to lead the
way. And how often while crossing the great prairies,
those memories have crowded out all else; and,
though love of adventure urged me westward, my
heart ached for the green loveliness of that fair coun-
try we have left behind.
"I must confess that after so many weeks of almost
isolated travel, the sight of a real town (rough and
unsettled though it is compared with our trim New
England villages and their clean white houses) seems
almost confusing. This place is laid out on the north
side of the Fountaine qui Bouille, and covers, they
tell us, twelve hundred acres, stretching over a grassy
plain with table mountains surrounding it, and
Pike's Peak towering above. Most of the homes are
built of unhewn logs, though the majority of busi-
ness places boast square fronts of lumber which ap-
pear to give much satisfaction to their owners,
though truth to tell, they look ugly enough to me!
"Many of the inhabitants of the City are from
Kansas, and were, of course, anxious for the latest
news from 'home. One poor man, who, they told us,
had never been right in his mind since his wagon
was attacked by Indians on the plains and his wife
and son scalped before his eyes, asking over and over
if the border war had subsided, and if old JohnBrown had really left for the East; nor could he
remember when told that John Brown was executed
at Harper's Ferry last December, but in ten minutes
would be back again with the same questions. His
distress was sad to behold, and made us all realize
afresh how fortunate we have been to encounter no
really hostile savages the entire way.
"Here is Mother, saying that Michael Warwick has
prevailed upon Father to rest the oxen for a day; and
eager though I am to see my darling uncle, I am glad
of this opportunity to visit the soda spring which is
less than two miles off, at what must be the very foot
of these towering heights. Mother declares she is so
weary that she prefers to spend the day in rest-like
the oxen!-and as Father has other affairs to occupy
him, it looks now as though Michael and I would
take the trip alone. . . ."
Needless to say, this was exactly what the English-
man had planned; and as the day dawned clear and
sparkling, with a delightful coolness in the air, both
young people voted to make the excursion on foot,
carrying a lunch so they might linger along the way
and miss none of the strange rock formations.
"A man from Lawrence, Kansas, who talked with
me a while last night," said Michael, when, after hav-
ing walked too rapidly, they paused for breath,
"spoke of a place called Red Rocks not far from here,
where sandstone cliffs rise hundreds of feet above
their base. We ought to catch a glimpse of them
before long.""I have already caught so many glimpses," laughed
Mary, "that I'll never be able to make a record of
them as it is!"
"Oh, that record!" groaned the young man as they
started on. "Good heavens, girl!-do you think of
nothing else? Not that I don't expect to revel in its
pages when I'm old and gray," he added, throwing a
quick glance of amusement toward Mary's averted
face.
"I didn't dream you were planning to make a
copy of my book for your own use," she observed
demurely.
"That, I trust, will not be necessary," Michael
retorted; and then, in great excitement: "Look,
Mary! Did you ever see such crags? They must be
the ones I was describing. How red they are! And
how clearly they stand out against this deep blue
sky!"
For a full minute the girl gazed at this spectacle
in wonder. "They look," she said, awe in her voice,
"as if they had been cut from paper and pasted
there! Can't we get nearer to them, Michael, or must
we keep to our road until we reach the spring?"
"Better do all extra sight-seeing when we return,
I think. A shower may overtake us later; and as your
father agreed to lose a day so that we might see the
soda spring, we mustn't miss it. Take care!" he
warned, as Mary moved on reluctantly. "If you try
to look back over your shoulder at those cliffs, my
dear, you may land on your head! This is no New
York pavement, remember.""And if it were I shouldn't recognize it," she told
him, laughing. "Have you forgotten that I was
brought up on a farm-a rocky farm, at that, and
am used to rough footing?"
Their way followed an old Indian trail which had
been widened to make a better road where it passed
through thickets of choke-cherry and currant bushes.
The scenery on every side impressed both young peo-
ple so profoundly that they stopped often to drink
it in, "for who knows," sighed the girl, "if we shall
be fortunate enough to pass this way again?"
"And who knows," countered her companion,
"that we may not live long years near these very
mountains? Ah! here is the spring at last, boiling out
of a rocky bowl just as they told us! See that great
volume of gas rising into the air!"
This was indeed a wondrous sight which fasci-
nated them to the exclusion of all else until, stoop-
ing, Mary picked up a large turquoise bead from the
basin's edge.
"Look, Michael! Another bead for my necklace!
But it was dropped from no dead warrior in such a
place."
The Englishman took this bauble into his hand
curiously.
"It must have been left here as an offering to the
Great Spirit whom the Indians believe dwells in this
spring, their idea being that its bubbling waters are
caused by His breathing. That fellow from Kansas
says that whenever a party of Indians ride over the
plains and up Ute Pass in search of game, young peo-ple from Colorado City visit this place looking for
beads and other trinkets left by the savages."
The girl's face was rapt as she listened to this
story.
"But one would think they'd drop their offerings
in the water itself," she murmured.
"They do." Michael was gazing into the bubbling
spring. "And if I'm not mistaken there are more
beads in this rocky bowl. Just look at that large one!
We must have that anyway, Mary, if I have to empty
the entire basin with my hat as a dipper!"
"But first we must try the water," she cautioned,
peering down to catch a glimpse of the coveted trin-
kets, "though I was in such haste to get away this
morning that I forgot my cup."
"I'll make a cup of my two hands then," smiled
the young man; and kneeling down, scooped up the
water which his companion drank, making a wry face
as she raised her head.
"As a beverage I would not recommend this soda
spring!" she said; but her escort, tasting for himself,
declared it delicious.
"Delicious? What can have happened to your
palate?-though a lady from Ohio was telling
Mother that they use this water for making bread!
How can you swallow the stuff, Michael-not to
mention enjoying it? I'm wondering will I ever get
the taste out of my mouth."
"We'll soon fix that," he told her, "with a bacon
sandwich; but we must secure these beads before
some other traveler passes by," and plunging hissombrero into the basin Michael proceeded to empty
it, while the girl stood near lamenting the fact that
his hat would be little better than a ruin.
"What is a hat compared with a necklace for a
Princess?" he returned, moving his head to glance
up with an engaging grin. "Here's a beauty-large
enough to make a central bead for that golden strand
I've promised you when my ship comes in, via the
Rocky Mountains! The others are smaller, but I
think their coloring is even finer."
"They are all lovely," agreed Mary, taking the
baubles into her hand and turning each one over for
closer scrutiny. "I am sure that no real Princess ever
had so valuable an ornament, for each bead recalls
a day or place I should be sad to forget."
"I'm sorry if you need beads to make you remem-
ber this wonderful journey we have had together,"
observed Michael Warwick, such reproach in his
voice that Mary laughed, and with an abrupt change
of subject, declared that she was hungry.
"To judge from the position of the sun it is not
yet noon, young lady; but after all, one's hunger
should decide meal times, and I feel as ravenous as
a mountain lion. We were up early, you know, and
the walk has whetted our appetites. Why not eat
right here? I cannot imagine a pleasanter place."
"Nor I," she answered, seating herself upon a rock;
but the lunch box was scarcely opened when they
were startled by the explosion of firearms not far
away, and the clatter of horses' hoofs approaching
down the adjacent road. Both rose, alarmed, whilethe girl reached out instinctively to clutch at her
companion's arm.
In another moment, frantically urging his ex-
hausted mount, a man rode past at a gallop, fol-
lowed by a half dozen others, shouting, cursing, and
firing at the fugitive in what seemed a haphazard
manner, for one bullet grazed a rock and, glancing
off, buried itself in the ground near Mary's feet.
"Damn them!" cried Michael. "Only a few inches
and you would have been hit! There! there!" (his
arm drawing her closer), "the danger's past, Mary;
but if I meet those desperadoes later I'll give them
a tongue lashing if not worse."
"But-why were they shooting? What had the poor
fellow done, I wonder?" asked Mary breathlessly,
moving away a space, her color mounting as she real-
ized that she still clung to the young man.
"Stolen a horse, perhaps; and justice follows
quickly in this new country."
"You mean- Surely you don't mean”
Her eyes had widened with horror; and Michael
broke in quickly: "Forgive me! I shouldn't have men-
tioned my surmises. Try to forget them, Mary, and
let us continue our interrupted luncheon. I hope
this fright hasn't taken away your appetite. My own,"
he added lightly in an effort to divert her, "is unim-
paired!"
Back in Colorado City Luther Alden had watched
his daughter's departure with mingled feelings.
"Do you think it wise and, perhaps, proper," heasked his wife, "to let those young folks spend an
entire day alone? I would have gone with them
gladly, for I'm told that the soda spring is a real
miracle; but there's a blacksmith here, and I wanted
him to look the wagon over and make sure it is
ship-shape before we leave."
"Don't worry," soothed Persis. "One doesn't
bother with the proprieties in this wild place; and
I'd trust Michael Warwick with a new-born baby."
"So would I, my dear; but a maid of twenty is a
different matter! I shouldn't want our Mary to get
foolish notions into her head about the first young
fellow from the outside world who passes by."
"If there's a rule for keeping what you call 'foolish
notions' out of a girl's head, I have yet to hear of it,"
laughed Persis Alden. Then seriously: "Michael War-
wick has come to seem like my very own, Luther;
and if Mary turns to him I shall be rejoiced."
"There is no reason for her turning to anybody at
present," declared the girl's father emphatically, "and
I would have forbidden this excursion if you hadn't
told me at Fountain City that I had hurt the lad's
feelings with my caution. I dare say he is all you
think him, Persis. Surely he has a pleasant disposi-
tion and decent speech. I have yet to hear more than
a few mild curses from him, and those in moments
of unbearable exasperation. But for all that, I've
warned Mary to go slowly, and think she will."
"Then be at peace," advised his wife, suppressing
a smile at her husband's innocence regarding the
heart of woman. "How many times in the last twentyyears, Luther, have you quoted to me from the Bible
that 'sufficient unto the day is the evil thereof? It
is time now to take that wise saying to yourself, dear
man, and cease your fretting. Run along to the black-
smith. I'm going to do a bit of laundry work and
then lie down for a nap in Michael's tent. I was so
excited at arriving in this metropolis last evening
that I hardly slept a wink the entire night; but if
I'm not up by noontime, come and wake me."W
CHAPTER XIX
HAT roused Persis Alden a few hours later
was not her husband's voice, but the sound
of shooting. Alarmed, she started up to behold what
looked like the entire population of that frontier
town, assembled not far from the street, below the
very spot where Michael had pitched his tent.
Smoothing her hair hastily, the astonished woman
hurried toward the gathering, curious as to what it
was all about. Luther had vanished (the prairie
schooner standing deserted not far away), and think-
ing her husband must be a part of that obviously
excited throng, she paused on its outskirts, hoping
to catch a glimpse of his tall figure; and at this
very moment another woman came running from a
nearby cabin.
"What is the trouble?" called Persis. "What's hap-
pening? I can see nothing."
"Only the trial of a Mexican who stole a horse
last night," the woman answered as she hastened on.
"He was captured somewhere beyond Ute Pass; and
will probably be hung within the hour."
"Hung!" echoed the New Englander, running a
little in order to keep up with her companion. "How
terrible! Can nothing be done? I understand that
such things happen quickly in these places, but"She stopped abruptly, partly because the woman
was not listening, and partly to hear the words of a
man who stood upon an upturned barrel, and had
(she was to learn later) been hastily appointed
Judge.
"There are no further witnesses to examine," he
told the crowd, "and since there seems no doubt that
the prisoner is guilty of the crime of horse stealing,
will all those who think he should be executed, sig-
nify that belief by walking across the street?"
At this request almost the entire throng pressed
back from where they had been crowding about the
captive, a wretched Mexican in a torn sombrero.
Truly horrified, Persis made an effort to edge for-
ward in protestation, but before she could do so the
Judge had already pronounced the dread death
sentence.
Hardly had the words dropped from his lips, how-
ever, when a man (somewhat incongruously attired
in a long frock coat), stepped up to ask if he might
be allowed the privilege of speaking. This was, Persis
surmised, a minister of the Gospel, who, in a few
impassioned words, reminded the crowd that it was
a serious matter to take the life of a fellow man, how-
ever guilty.
"Let us at least," he said, "kneel down and pray
for the soul of this poor youth who is about to pay
the penalty of what was, perhaps, merely a crime of
impulse, and already repented. Let us”
But at this point the mob grew impatient, and one
man shouted: "Justice first, Parson, and prayers after-ward. Damn it! That horse was well nigh ruined."
Whereupon the terrified Mexican was marched to
the nearest cottonwood and executed without further
delay.
Although Persis endeavored to move back out of
sight and hearing of this tragedy, it was impossible to
do so while those behind her pushed forward for a
better view of the ghastly proceeding. All she could
do was to close her eyes for what seemed an eternity;
and when at last she dared to open them again, the
sight of that limp form dangling from the old cotton-
wood was too much for her, and the poor woman was
overtaken by sudden and violent nausea.
"You'll get used to these things in time," observed
a stranger who helped her back to the shelter of
Michael's tent. "Not so long ago I felt the same my-
self; but frontier life hardens us unbelievably."
"God forbid that I should ever be hardened to a
sight like that," responded the Easterner; and was
relieved when her well-meaning friend departed to
get dinner for a family which had (to all appear-
ances) watched the hanging with satisfaction.
"I sha'n't move from this tent till Luther tells me
the body has been cut down," Persis promised her-
self, shuddering. "Where can he be? I've no doubt
the poor dear is every bit as sick as I."
She was not far wrong in this surmise, as Luther's
face testified when he joined her a moment later.
"I wouldn't have had you witness such a sight for
a thousand dollars," he declared regretfully. "I had
gone to the blacksmith shop to get a bolt, and whenI returned the trial was in full swing. If this is pio-
neer life, I can't say much for it. I've no stomach for
dinner, Persis, and I judge by your looks that you
have none either."
"Nor can I spend another night in sight of that
cottonwood, my dear. Is-is..."
Her husband nodded understandingly.
"I've no doubt the poor creature is already buried,
perhaps without a single prayer. But as you say, this
camping place has lost its charm. I have half a mind
to yoke up the oxen and move to the other end of
the City before our young folks return. It's Providen-
tial that Mary was away."
"It is indeed," sighed Persis, "though she's bound
to hear of the affair. I was ashamed to be violently
sick, my dear, in front of everybody; but not being
the center of attraction just then, not many noticed,
for which I was thankful."
"Poor girl!" comforted Luther, patting her shoul-
der. "You must lie still while I start the fire and
bring you a cup of tea. We need food, I suppose,
however much our stomachs rebel at the idea; and
after we have pulled ourselves together, Persis, why
not move on? We can leave a message for Mary at
the nearest cabin, where they'd be sure to inquire
on finding that we'd broken camp."
"Better still, why not wait for them here, ready
to start out upon our journey? Even if we make but
a few miles to-night, Luther, it would be a help, and
no more trouble than setting up our camp a half mile
off. I confess I've a longing to get away from peoplewho hold human life as lightly as these temporary
neighbors seem to do."
"You'll be forced to turn back to civilization then,
I fear," counseled her husband. "Values are different
in the wilderness; and we shouldn't pass judgment
on these pioneers until we have lived among them
and understand the problems of frontier life. I was
as horrified at the whole proceeding as you were,
Persis; yet the man who acted as Judge was one who
talked with me last evening, a gentleman of breed-
ing and education from Philadelphia. Now try to
rest for a while; and when we've strengthened our-
selves with bread and tea we can plan what to do."
Persis turned on her side, closing her eyes wearily,
and thankful for her husband's kindly care. The
nausea had subsided; but she could not get the
dreadful scene out of her mind, and at last arose to
help Luther with the preparations for a simple lunch.
Though neither mentioned the fact, they turned
their backs on the ancient cottonwood as they choked
down bread and tea; but both felt better after the
meal, and Luther said:
"While you clean up these dishes, Persis, I'll walk
down street and make some inquiries about camping
spots a few miles on."
He was absent the best part of an hour, and was,
his wife was glad to see, decidedly more cheerful
when he returned.
"I've been chatting with a Mr. Oldham, my dear,
a minister from Iowa, the very one who pleaded
with that mob this morning, and who, I am glad tosay, saw to it that the victim received proper burial.
Like ourselves, he was shocked and sickened at the
affair. He came from Denver City a short time since,
and tells me there is no longer any town of Auraria,
as it was united with Denver on the very day that we
left home! The poor man came west in search of a
much younger brother who started for the gold fields
almost a year back, and has never been heard from."
"Then the West is a new country to this min-
ister?"
Luther nodded.
"He crossed the plains a few weeks ahead of us,
and went by way of the Platte River. We should
have had more company along that trail, for it seems
that after passing Fort Kearney there was an almost
unbroken line of covered wagons extending for many
miles in the valley of the Platte. It would have been
an inspiring sight."
"But if we'd gone by the Overland we would never
have known Mrs. MacTavish," Persis reminded him.
"And to have met that lovely woman and heard her
tales of the old days, was worth many miles of soli-
tary travel. What of the route ahead, Luther? Is it
wise for us to start out late in the afternoon?"
"I see no reason why we shouldn't. A good trail
leads to the north along a tributary of the Fountaine
qui Bouille, called Camp Creek. The Reverend Old-
ham says there is wonderful scenery along the way
which it's a pity to miss by traveling after dusk; but
we can easily reach Monument Creek, where there is
fine grass and timber."Persis' tired face brightened.
"And we can go on as soon as our young people
return?"
"Why not? It seems that after crossing Monument
Creek the road continues north over the divide of
the South Platte and Arkansas, a dangerous spot, for
it is sometimes fatal to be caught in one of the bad
storms common in that locality. Such a thing is un-
likely this time of year, but I'll breathe easier when
the crest of the divide is behind us; and even a few
miles that can be saved to-morrow morning will
help out."
"True,” agreed Persis, "and thanks to this dry air
the washing I did before I took my nap is ready to
fold away. We'll do all we can, Luther; but let the
oxen graze till the last moment. Those faithful beasts
deserve a rest."
"And so do you, Mother," the man said anxiously.
"I hate to urge you on, but think you may sleep
better away from this spot and its horrid memory.
I wish Michael were here right now. He's been a
tremendous help these many weeks. That young fel-
low is no shirk when there's work to do. I'll admit
that."
"And here he is!" cried Persis, as a distant "hello"
fell on her ears.
"We heard the dreadful thing that happened,
Mother," exclaimed Mary breathlessly, as they ap-
proached. "Michael felt sure you'd want to move on
as soon as possible, so we hurried back."
It was plain that the girl was much wrought up,and after kissing her mother she ran to Luther and
threw her arms about him.
"Oh, Father! could you do nothing at all? We saw
the poor fellow and the men who captured him.
They galloped past us near the soda spring. It seems
so heartless that we could have enjoyed ourselves
when such things were happening, but-but we
didn't know."
"Where did you hear the ghastly tale?" asked
Persis. "News seems to travel fast in this wild coun-
try."
"We met two boys on horseback who had witnessed
the affair," responded Michael. "They left no lurid
detail to our imagination, though they plainly con-
sidered it merely a part of the day's work. I hope,
Mrs. Alden, that you weren't present when they tried
the man."
Persis said nothing-a sufficient answer; and
Luther broke in quickly: "Let's put it out of our
minds, if we can. But we've lost our liking for this
camp site, Michael, and are making preparations to
start again, going a few miles to the bank of Monu-
ment Creek, unless you young folks are tired after
your excursion."
"As for me, the sooner we get away, the better,"
replied Mary with a shudder. "I didn't realize that
such affairs would be a common part of the life out
here, or I might have hugged closer to New England.
What is a horse compared with a human life, Father?
Somehow, I can't see why such things have to be."
"Don't try to," advised Michael gently. "We'vehad a glorious day, Mrs. Alden, though your daugh-
ter didn't enthuse over the soda water! Now let's get
to work. I am eager to be on the trail once more;
and was told last night that for some miles the
scenery is magnificent."
These cheerful words brought back a more normal
atmosphere; and before long everything was ready
for the start, which was delayed a bit by a call from
the Reverend Oldham, begging them to inquire for
his brother, should they fall in with other travelers.
"And we'll meet again," he said at parting, "for I
shall return to Denver City when I've satisfied myself
that the boy is nowhere in these parts. The trip here
was a wild goose chase, I fear; though I'm repaid by
having met your husband, Mrs. Alden. He's the sort
needed in this new country. Are you young people to
make your home here, too?" he questioned, turning
to Mary as she stood by Michael behind the others.
Perhaps this inference was not unnatural; but the
girl flushed with embarrassment and did not speak.
It was Michael, his self-possession never leaving him,
who replied so quickly that no pause was noticeable:
"That remains to be seen, sir!" And giving the good
man's hand a hearty grip he sprang into the saddle
and was away, without one glance at Mary's crimson
face.T
CHAPTER XX
HEN we have reached," said Luther Alden
solemnly, baring his head as if the occasion
demanded ceremony, "the last lap of our long, long
trail."
This was toward sundown the third after break-
ing camp at Colorado City, when Michael (who had
stopped at a saw mill to inquire, while the prairie
schooner continued on) brought the glad tidings
that their destination was but about twelve miles
distant.
"In six miles we should come to the South Platte,"
he told them, "and in another six the mouth of
Cherry Creek, which leads us straight to Denver
City. The question is, shall we strike camp in an
hour or two, or press on with only the stars to
guide us?"
There followed a thoughtful silence before Persis
answered: "I dare say a morning arrival would be
more cheerful; yet I can't help feeling a desire to
go on to-night. What do you think, Luther?"
"I too, would like to travel farther, for we've gone
leisurely these two days, and the oxen have had an
easy time. But as you say, my dear, everything looks
brighter in these sparkling mornings, and a frontier
town is dreary when night shuts down.""Oh, let's go on!" cried Mary with impatience.
"It may be that Uncle Dan is watching for us this
very minute. What are twelve miles, Father, after
such a journey? Nothing at all. Look at Zebulon
Pike! His ears are up, which means that he agrees
with me!"
"And what do you say?" asked Luther, turning to
the Englishman.
"Like all the rest of you, I feel impatient to be
moving now that the end of our journey is in sight.
It is a pity we haven't a full moon to-night, but these
mountain stars are wonderfully brilliant, and will
light our way almost as well. My only fear is that
Mrs. Alden may be too tired."
"But I'm not!" Persis spoke briskly. "The last two
days of easy travel, and this fine scenery have rested
me more than I would have thought possible; and
the air is bracing. As far as I'm concerned, Michael,
we could go on till midnight. But you mustn't hope
to find your uncle when we arrive, Mary. For all we
know he may be miles away at the gold fields."
"I don't believe it!" laughed the girl happily. "It's
far more likely that he's so impatient to see us he
can't work. I know my precious uncle well enough
for that. Come! Start those slow beasts, Father. Poor
Shamrock is dancing with impatience to be off."
"Michael's a good lad to stick by us," observed
Luther as he watched the mare dart forward at a
gallop. "If Shamrock had had her way, our young
man would have reached the gold fields days ago; and
counting the number of times he has ridden on andreturned to join us, he's traveled many more miles
than we have."
"I find it hard to imagine what we should have
done without his help," said Persis.
Only Mary remained silent until her father, glanc-
ing sideways surreptitiously, remarked: "A penny for
your thoughts, daughter."
The girl laughed, meeting his eyes with merri-
ment.
"They're not worth all that! I was only remember-
ing that minister from Iowa, and his long coat which
made him look so foolish here in the wilds."
Said Luther, with an unlooked-for touch of
humor: "It was not the coat which made the poor
fellow appear foolish, Mary, but his mistake in taking
a chit like you for a married lady! Perhaps, though,
it was not unnatural since Michael was traveling with
us."
Recalling the episode, Persis laughed softly as she
commented: "It struck me that Michael's response
and speedy departure showed quick wit as well as
tact."
"And I was wishing the ground would open and
swallow me," confessed Mary, "though all the time,
Father, I thought you didn't even notice your friend's
remark."
"I notice a-plenty that I'm given no credit for,"
retorted Luther. "And I'll say now, Mary (though
no doubt your mother disapproves of my meddling),
that you should be careful not to encourage that
young man.""Meaning the Reverend Oldham?" questioned his
daughter, a twinkle lighting her dark eyes.
"Meaning young Michael Warwick, as you well
know, child," he reproved her. "Not for the world
would I have you hurt him; and I feel sure you
would not consider marriage yet a while."
"Then for goodness' sake why fuss about it?" asked
Persis with some irascibility. "And have you forgotten
that at Mary's age I was a mother?"
"Need you remind the girl of that?" snapped
Luther, at which the girl herself broke into a merry
laugh, finding her parents most amusing.
"Come, dears!" she soothed. "You are practically
quarreling, and about nothing at all! Here comes the
subject of this hot debate, so smooth your ruffled
feathers quickly. I can remember Grandma Alden
saying it was unseemly to bicker before outsiders;
and Michael Warwick is only a boarder. What a
wonderful run Zebby has had! The poor dog would
have suffered for lack of exercise without Shamrock
to race with."
They allowed only a short time for supper, and
continued on, the way lighted first by a gorgeous
afterglow: huge clouds, gold-tipped and softly pink,
piled one upon another-then deepening twilight-
stars and a great stillness save for the heavy breath-
ing of the oxen-the creaking prairie schooner.
On, on for hours, in an enchanted silence that
Mary Alden was never to forget. Night. Late night.
She sat alone now, the collie's head upon her lap,
keeping guard while her parents rested under cover,and Shamrock paced sedately within reach. A coyote
barked.
The collie raised his nose, sniffing the
air, stirring uneasily. Far on a distant mesa a
camp fire sprang up and then another, their flames
as they shot skyward revealing the dim outline of a
covered wagon: a lonely sentinel there on the wide
plains. The sight stirred Mary's imagination-set her
to dreaming, while, almost unconsciously she reached
into the darkness, her hand seeking that of the solitary
rider at her side. Neither spoke; yet the girl knew
that her mood, born of the time, the place, the very
atmosphere, was shared and understood.
Thus they were riding when the collie sat sud-
denly erect, his ruff rising, a deep growl coming
from his throat. Shamrock, too, pricked up her ears
nervously; while Mary, startled out of her dreams,
reached for the rifle and laid it in readiness across
her knees.
"What do they hear?" she questioned in a whisper,
alarmed yet not wishing to disturb the sleepers within
the wagon; but before Michael could reply, the beat
of horse's hoofs fell on her ears, and grasping the gun
firmly, she uttered the dread word: "Indians?"
"Only one," the young man reassured her; yet as
he spoke a fear that other savages might lie in am-
bush swept over him-fear for the girl's safety which
showed in the tense voice that added: "Call your
father-at once, Mary!"
But the lone rider, approaching at a gallop, was
already on them, the oxen stopping of their own
accord as he reined in his mount. It was then thedim starlight showed the girl that this was no Indian,
but a bearded stranger; and in another moment his
voice rang out cheerily on the silent night.
"Welcome to Denver City, friends, which, you will
be glad to hear, is less than three miles on! Have you
seen anything of a wagon from Massachusetts? My
brother”
He never finished that sentence, for with a joyous
cry of: "Uncle Dan! Why, it's my Uncle Dan!" Mary
almost threw herself from the lofty place where she
was sitting, into the waiting arms below.
("And for almost ten minutes," she wrote in her
diary next evening, "we completely forgot our
boarder!")
This was indeed true, yet the young Englishman,
who had dismounted and stood beside his horse, felt
no neglect. It was a strange time-a strange place
for a family reunion, and he was content to watch
it. At Mary's exclamation her parents (who had
roused when the prairie schooner ceased moving)
came almost tumbling from the wagon, eager for
sight of their long-absent brother. The girl was
dropped from her uncle's arms with little ceremony,
while, for what seemed a long moment to the on-
looker, Persis took her place. The two brothers
clasped hands silently; then, emotion getting the
better of even Luther Alden's New England reserve,
they hugged each other as they never had before.
"When I heard those hoof-beats in the darkness,"
cried Mary, "I thought you were an Indian, Uncle
Daniel, and wondered how long I should keep myscalp! And for a moment I didn't know you, because
of that horrid beard, of course. You must shave at
once."
"Give me till morning, sweetheart," laughed her
uncle. "It is a land of bearded chins you've come to,
where no man has time for a smooth face. But as
always, your word is law, young lady, and this mag-
nificent beard, one of the finest in the Rocky Moun-
tains, let me tell you, is doomed at sunrise. Or rather,
at the first opportunity. Damn it all!" (he threw an
apologetic glance at Luther, who never used pro-
fanity) "but it's good to see my family again! Some-
thing warned me that you were near; and as we've had
trouble with pilfering Indians to-day, I decided on
the spur of the moment to ride out, hoping to stop
you from entering the town till morning, when things
will be quiet."
"Then we shall camp now," asked Luther, "and
proceed by daylight?"
His brother nodded.
"If you can spare me a blanket, Persis, I'll roll
up in it here on the ground beside your wagon.
There's nothing I like better than sleeping under
the stars these summer nights; and if”
He stopped abruptly because Shamrock, perhaps
thinking her beloved master neglected, raised her
head to whinny long and loud.
"My goodness gracious!" exclaimed Persis, “we've
forgotten Michael! How could we? Come here, dear
boy, and meet another adventurer." And as the young
man came forward she slipped her arm through his,adding: "This, brother Daniel, is our young friend,
Michael Warwick, who has traveled with us all the
way from Kansas Territory, and without whose help
our journey would have been much harder."
"And my own much lonelier," amended Michael,
gripping Daniel's outstretched hand. "It is a pleasure
to meet you, Mr. Alden. Your niece has sung your
praises for a thousand miles!"
"I trust you'll not be disappointed then," laughed
Daniel. "You must make allowances for Mary's
partiality for an adoring uncle. Are you, too, a New
Englander, young man? Your accent sounds more like
Boston than"
"He's an old Englander," broke in Mary, with a
laugh, "from across the ocean. And if he's disap-
pointed in you, Uncle Danny, I'll cut his acquaint-
ance on the spot. Truth is, Michael can hardly wait
to hear you tell of your adventures aboard the clipper
ships. If there were time now"
"There's not!" Dan Alden interrupted, "and
though I'd like to sit up till dawn listening to all
the details of your journey, I advise getting to bed
at once and rising early, for to-morrow will be a busy
day. Give me a kiss, Mary, and a blanket, so this
young man and I can wander over to that clump
of cottonwoods for a few hours. Good-night, dear
child. Good-night, dear sister Persis. Luther, old
man, have they told you that you look five years
younger? And your women folk are blooming! Didn't
I say they possessed the stuff of pioneers?"D
CHAPTER XXI
ENVER CITY, Jefferson Territory, August-
1860," wrote Mary. "Yesterday was so exciting
from sunrise to sunset and beyond, that there was
no opportunity to put down a single word, but now
that we're settled (temporarily) in a comfortable
little cabin which a gentleman from Boston insisted
on vacating for our use, I can take time to record the
happenings since our midnight meeting with Uncle
Daniel.
"The next morning (yesterday) dawned as spar-
kling as all prairie mornings seem to dawn, and de-
spite retiring so late, we in the wagon awoke early,
though not so early as those who slept upon the
ground, for when I stepped down from our house
on wheels, a cheerful camp fire was burning not far
away.
"I cannot recall ever eating so jolly a breakfast
as was that which followed. In spite of the beard
which disfigures his handsome countenance, Uncle
Daniel was at his best, and was so thrilled at Mother's
flapjacks and coffee that he must needs pay her with
a kiss right in the midst of our meal, which caused
much merriment, and actually brought a girlish
blush to her cheek! In fact, I never saw my uncle sopleased with his relations; and he seems to have taken
a great liking to our boarder.
"We broke camp as soon as possible, and proceeded
along Cherry Creek until we reached the City, which,
Uncle Daniel tells us, embraces five thousand acres
of fine building lots. On the way we passed a number
of rude shanties where liquor and tobacco were for
sale; and some miners washing gold in the shallow
waters of the creek, which was a new and interesting
sight. My uncle says one could scarcely recognize the
Denver of to-day with that of a year, or even a few
months ago. Then most of the buildings were mud-
roofed log cabins; but now frame or brick edifices
are replacing them with incredible rapidity.
"There are already two theatres; but though the
population is improving and many families settling
here, gambling and dissipation are universal, I am
sad to say, though I suppose I should have known
they would be a part of frontier life. As Uncle Dan
observed, 'temples to God, Mammon and Satan jostle
each other,' for churches, trading houses, and dens
of vice stand side by side. Michael declares that Blake
Street is 'gay as Broadway,' and on this thoroughfare
is the Denver House, a low one-story log building
with only white sheeting for windows and roof! In-
side the partitions are canvas-covered frames not over
seven feet high; the whole front part being a spacious
saloon where they sell enormous quantities of cigars
and liquors, and where gamblers are busy day and
night-a contrast indeed to our peaceful New Eng-
land farm. I noticed a blacksmith shop across thestreet; and nearby a Jewish merchant was advertising
'White, red and orange Flannels.' There were also
'Vienna Bakeries' and the office of the Leavenworth
and Pike's Peak stage line, as well as cabins with dirt
floors by way of variety.
"Larimer Street boasts a theatre, Simm's Restau-
rant, a billiard saloon, and the Broadwell House,
which is a finished frame building where one can
obtain (to quote Uncle Dan again) 'fair accommoda-
tions at Astor House prices.' But St. Charles Street,
for all its aristocratic-sounding name, has, apparently,
no inhabitants save prairie dogs; and as one looks
down F Street one sees the waters of the Platte, with
Indian lodges and the tents of pioneers along its
banks, a strange sight when one considers that the
City Drug Store (built of brick) would adorn the
streets of Springfield, Massachusetts, or any eastern
town.
"As for churches-the Episcopalians are worship-
ing in the library reading room; and a Catholic
church is in the course of construction. There are
doubtless others of which I have not yet heard. The
town has three daily papers which are delivered at
one's door for fifty cents a week; and we can secure
Eastern newspapers (ten or more days old) for
twenty cents. Though the city has its own mint, un-
coined gold is still in circulation, and on every store
counter stand little scales for weighing the bright
'dust.'
"The Express Office we found to be a large, two-
story edifice with a low wing in which are the win-dows of the postal department. At the time we
passed, there was a long, long line of men extending
far down the street, each waiting patiently (or im-
patiently) for his turn at the window, hoping for
letters from mothers, wives, sweethearts in a word,
from Home. It was a sight that moved me strangely,
and I could not help wondering what hopes and fears
stirred the hearts of that strange assembly, for it was
a motley crowd indeed.
"Now as to our present dwelling place-Uncle Dan
had meant to have a cabin ready for us; but since no
future plans had been made, and he feared that
Father might not wish to go into the gold fields,
leaving his women folk to fend for themselves at
Denver City, it seemed wiser to await our arrival
before building, especially since the time of that
arrival was so uncertain. He had, indeed, planned
to send Mother and me to the Broadwell House for
a few days, while he and Father occupied some de-
serted cabin; but on hearing these plans, a gentle-
man from Boston who had built himself a snug little
house, insisted on giving it up for as long a time as
we care to stay, since (as he said) we are 'fellow
citizens of the good old Commonwealth of Massa-
chusetts.'
"So here we are, in what is a palace compared with
some of the mud-roofed cabins, for our dwelling is
constructed of upright boards, with battened cracks
to protect us from rain or wind, not to mention the
dust. It sets back quite a distance from town on an
imaginary street which Michael has christened'Prairie Dog Terrace' (since we are surrounded by
the homes of those little animals), and is on high
land with fine views in all directions.
"We have two small rooms with a shed at the back,
a cook stove, a battered writing desk and shelf of
books, three chairs, table, and two beds, one of which
was provided by my thoughtful uncle. The only
fault I can find with this temporary home is that it
possesses a square board front like those so popular
in Colorado City, which offends my artistic eye! For
the time being Michael and Uncle Dan will sleep
in the prairie schooner which is far more comfortable
than a deserted cabin; and we shall, of course, have
our meals together.
"I was surprised while on another exploring ex-
pedition with Michael this afternoon, to meet several
tastefully, even expensively dressed ladies, whose
gowns made my old brown gingham appear countri-
fied. But the men are attired mostly in buckskin
trousers and flannel shirts, and, as Uncle Daniel
warned me, almost the whole male population is
bearded, while each man carries at least one revolver,
and perhaps a knife suspended from his belt. In
striking contrast to these rough looking customers,
we met, on our homeward way (after visiting the
mint of Clark, Gruber & Company where we saw
many bags of gold dust and shining nuggets), a
benevolent Catholic priest, who gave us a kindly
greeting in passing by. He is, we learned later, an
arrival from Santa Fé, New Mexico, sent here to
build a chapel and establish St. Mary's Academy."This, I believe, completes my first, perhaps hasty,
impression of Denver City. Now I must run to help
Mother prepare the evening meal, after which we
shall consider future plans and decide whether to
remain where we are for a time, or seek our fortunes
higher in the mountains. We had, of course, looked
forward to seeing the boys who came West with
Uncle Daniel, and giving them all the home news,
but it seems that they left for California six weeks
ago.
"Here comes my uncle now-minus that beard,
and his handsome self again!"T
CHAPTER XXII
O tell the truth," admitted Daniel Alden when
discussing the situation, "I don't quite take to
the idea of leaving Persis and Mary here alone."
They were outdoors in the cool of evening, the
"old folks" occupying a rough board bench built
along the side of their cabin-the two young people
seated upon the ground.
"Nor do I," said Luther. "Suppose they should get
sick, Dan, with no one to go for help."
His brother smiled.
"Sickness is the least of my worries. But though
Denver City looks civilized enough for a frontier
town, there's a rough element here now-thieves
and worse, low scum from California, most of it, who
consider themselves superior and have been heard
to call the rest of us (if you ladies will pardon my
language!) the Pike's Peak skunks! These 'high-
toned' gentlemen are all too quick with the trigger.
Murders have been frequent this summer; and lynch-
ings, even when justified, aren't pleasant spectacles
for unprotected women to witness, or even hear
about."
Remembering the experience which had driven
them from Colorado City, Persis winced, and ques-
tioned: "What of the mining towns, Daniel? Couldn'tMary and I live comfortably enough in such a place
as Mountain City?"
"Central City now," Daniel corrected. "Names
change frequently out here, Persis, where one settle-
ment is gobbled up by another almost over night.
You see, as a starter, each cluster of tents and cabins
takes a name for itself. In Gregory diggings there
were among several others, Black Hawk, Gregory
Point, Mountain City, Nevadaville and Central City
which has now wiped out many a smaller settlement.
As I wrote you, my dear, it's a thriving place for a
mining town, and you women could make your-
selves as comfortable there as here; but, since my
letter to you, things haven't looked so hopeful in the
gulch."
"Is the gold giving out so soon?" asked Michael.
"No; but as we dig deeper the quartz is too hard
for pick and shovel; and after the surface veins are
stripped, no more blossom rock comes down the
slope. I've watched more than one courageous fellow
sweat himself half dead to dig through solid granite,
only to see his vein taper off or pinch out entirely."
"This does not sound very encouraging to me,"
observed Luther, his brows drawn into worried lines.
"Cheer up!" smiled Daniel. "Such disappointments
are only the fascinating lottery of a miner's life;
and I believe there is still much gold to be found
in the gulch in surface veins that have not yet been
discovered."
"Do you know of any other part of the mountains
which seems more favorable?" questioned Michael.The older man looked thoughtfully into space, and
shook his head.
"It would be hard to say, for I've grown to dis-
trust the stories brought out by a gold excitement. A
year ago news of discoveries at the head waters of the
South Platte and Colorado reached Denver, and the
city went wild. As many as five thousand gold seekers
migrated across the Divide, thinking to pick up thou-
sands of dollars a day with no trouble at all; and most
of them returned with empty pockets. Besides, I
think so much of these two women that I'd hate to
take them to a new mining camp where they'd have
to rough it."
"Mercy sakes!" exclaimed Persis, "you mustn't let
consideration for us keep you from seeking the most
promising locality. I feel rested now, Dan, and able
to cope with any hardships if that's best for us all
in the long run."
"There'll be hardships enough in any case, my
dear," responded Daniel with his illuminating smile.
"It's a tough climb to Central City, but once there
we'll set to and build you a jig-saw trimmed cottage
if you desire one!"
"Which I most certainly do not!" asserted his sister-
in-law emphatically; while Mary added:
"And spare us one of those hideous false fronts!
The pioneer idea of beauty is not mine, Uncle Danny.
Something plain and simple will suit me better."
"There speak our Puritanical New Englanders!"
laughed her uncle. "Your austere taste will save us
men a lot of work, Mary. And after all, nothing suitsa mountain setting better than a log cabin, and
nothing's more comfortable either, provided it has
a good board floor and well-filled chinks."
"Then when do we start out?" asked Persis, her
voice eager.
"Will you listen to the woman?" cried Daniel
in mock despair, though his eyes rested on her with
admiration. "Having just arrived from a journey
of some two thousand miles, she's ready to set forth
again at a moment's notice! Such untiring energy
would put many a man to shame."
"Her husband among them," admitted Luther with
a smile. "Anxious as I am to get to work with pick
and shovel, it seems good to loiter for a few days.
But as the summer's so nearly over, Daniel, I sup-
pose we should get into the mountains as soon as
possible."
"You're right, brother; but we must take a day
or so to look over our belongings, purchase any
necessities, and give your oxen a rest, for the climb
ahead is a hard one. Besides, there's a show at Apollo
Hall to-morrow night which I'm sure these young
folks would enjoy."
"A show!" exclaimed Michael, his face brighten-
ing at the idea. "By local talent?"
"And may we dress up?" asked Mary, before Daniel
could reply. "I'm tired to death of these old ging-
hams, and felt that I belonged in the back woods
when we passed two prettily dressed ladies on Blake
Street this afternoon."
"You will belong in the back woods soon enough,"retorted her father dryly; while Daniel, reverting to
Michael's unanswered question, said with downright
scorn:
"Local talent! I'll have you know, young man,
that we'll have the privilege of seeing a troupe from
Leavenworth or some other civilized community.
They are to play La Tour de Nesle, whatever that
may be. In any case, it sounds high-toned enough;
and you may dress in your best, Mary, though if you
and your handsome mother appeared in calico you'd
still surpass in beauty all other females in the audi-
ence."
This flowery compliment brought laughs from all
of them; and Mary answered: "It's a miracle that such
a flatterer as you are, Uncle Dan, could have escaped
matrimony. But I shall be thankful to wear my
finery, and though she may not be willing to admit
it, Mother will simply jump at the opportunity her-
self. How fortunate that we arrived at just this
time!"
"What is the cost of this performance, Dan?" asked
Luther with characteristic caution.
His brother laughed.
"This party is my treat, Lute, so that needn't worry
you. I believe the charge is one dollar in gold dust-
little enough considering the journey these actors
take to entertain us. The hall seats three hundred
or more; and I'll admit the audience is often more
amusing than the play. But though the wooden
benches are back-breaking after an hour or two, and
the rattling billiard balls in the saloon below aredisconcerting, it's the best that Denver City can offer
us at present, and worth the price."
Daniel Alden had not exaggerated, as all agreed
when they took their seats in the crowded theatre
next evening.
"If I live to be a centenarian," declared Michael
Warwick, looking over the heads of that motley
gathering, "I shall never forget this scene. As your
uncle hinted, Mary, the audience itself is worth the
price of admission; but I wish the proprietors had
been more lavish in the matter of illumination. I
can count only twelve candles."
"Perhaps a dozen for each performance is all they
can afford," smiled Mary. Then, as the young man
started to speak again: "Hush, Michael! The curtain
is going up."
"What good does it do to hush while those gam-
blers downstairs are making such a pandemonium?"
he retorted. "You are about to witness a tragedy, my
dear, so get out your handkerchief and prepare to
weep."
"It was indeed a tragedy," wrote Mary in her
journal, "though I confess we young folks did more
giggling than weeping; and in the dim light my uncle
threw us many a reproving glance which had not
much good effect for it was plain to see that behind
his stern exterior he was well nigh convulsed him-
self.
"The acting was not bad by any means. Even
Michael Warwick admitted that; but when Queen
Marguerite exclaimed with what was a splendidsimulation of distress and horror: 'Then I am lost
indeed!' a bearded miner seated directly in front of
us replied with great enthusiasm: 'You bet!' at which
even Father was forced to laugh aloud, while Michael
disgraced himself by choking. And at the tragic end-
ing some one not far away shouted at poor Gaultier,
Marguerite and Buridan, 'Looking forward to a big
funeral, aren't you?' There were other interruptions
quite as amusing; but to our surprise there was no
display of coarseness or even profanity, though few
ladies were among the spectators. And it was really a
pity that the clinking glasses, noisy billiard balls, and
roistering songs in the saloon below were so distract-
ing that we missed many of the lines.
"It is decided definitely now that we shall try our
luck at Central City, and we expect to depart be-
fore the Sabbath day, or as soon as Uncle Daniel says
the word. Mother is like a girl, eager to set out again,
perhaps because she knows there will be time to rest
once we are really settled, and that it will be easier
to relax when the hard journey into the mountain
heights is over; but her vitality is amazing now that
the Kansas heat, which wilted her so terribly, is left
behind.
"Here comes Michael, asking me to walk the few
blocks to town and witness the departure of the
Leavenworth and Pike's Peak stage coach, which,
Uncle Dan assures us, is quite an event in Denver
City. So I will close this book, not to open it, perhaps,
until we are once more 'at home,' settled in the snug
log cabin our men plan to build."T
CHAPTER XXIII
ELL me, Michael, did I say too much about the
charm of my Uncle Daniel?"
It was the evening before their departure for
Central City, when Mary and the young Englishman
were returning from a visit to the watchmaker on
Ferry Street, since Michael's timepiece needed atten-
tion. A shower that afternoon had laid the dust,
making the air so sweet with moisture that, despite
an early morning start ahead of them, both felt
loath to go indoors, and were loitering as much as
possible on the homeward way.
"You know the answer to that question already,"
replied Michael, his hand on her elbow guiding Mary
across the dusky street. "Did our voices keep you
awake last night, I wonder? We talked for hours-
or rather, your uncle talked while I listened spell-
bound to his adventures. If only we could persuade
him to put them in a book he might make more
money than by discovering a gold mine!"
"Mother has often told him that very thing,
Michael, and he always replies that he couldn't sit
still long enough to write it! I'm sure he would never
have stayed on here like this if he hadn't expected
us to join him. My uncle's had a wonderful life since
he was twelve years old.""Has he?" said Michael. "If you were to ask me,
my dear, I see some lacks in it."
Mary turned, looking up in genuine surprise.
"But he's done everything he wanted to do,
Michael! His life has been his own. What more could
a man ask?"
"A lot," answered the Englishman, then was silent
so long that she echoed questioningly:
"A lot? What can you mean?"
"That his has been a one-sided life; and that he's
paid a heavy price for his freedom, Mary mavour-
neen."
The girl flushed at this unexpected endearment,
and moved away a space; while her escort, smiling
a bit, went on: "What's more, it's my opinion that
the man knows it, but is too good a sport ever to ad-
mit the fact."
"What nonsense!" Mary stopped short and faced
him. "I never knew a happier, more care-free person
in my whole life!"
"Yet you told me long weeks ago that your uncle
sometimes looks sad, when he thinks no one is by. Of
course it's quite possible that with his restless nature
he couldn't have lived differently. Perhaps in his
youth he had no regrets; but unless I am very much
mistaken, there are moments now when he envies
your father."
"Why?" she questioned, too astonished to utter
more than the one word.
Said Michael, his eyes drawing hers as with a
magnet: "Has he kissed the mouth of the woman heloves, Mary? Has he held a child of his own in his
arms? That's what I meant when I said Dan Alden
had paid a heavy price for his freedom; and why
there are times when, looking at your father, his
eyes grow wistful."
"But-but perhaps my uncle never wanted those
things, Michael."
A laugh greeted this naïve idea, and Michael an-
swered: "No one as human as your uncle Dan can
have escaped wanting them. Look at myself! I love
adventure as much as any fellow, or would I have
crossed an ocean to find it? But there is something
that every normal man loves more, Mary. Don't you
know that?"
For some reason which she could not explain, even
to herself, a teasing note in the young man's voice
hurt just a little at that moment. Thus Mary an-
swered, meanwhile trying to keep the words from
trembling: "I-I really cannot imagine what you
mean!"
"And I thought she possessed extraordinary in-
telligence!" observed the Englishman, apparently ad-
dressing this remark to the stars above; while his
companion, still hurt by the continued raillery,
walked on in silence. Strange, she was musing, that
those stars which seemed so comfortingly near during
the long migration, looked far away to-night. Only
the town which lay behind them now, its dimly
lighted cabins, stores, hotels and dens of wickedness,
was real to her. Even the tall form at her side seemed
to take on a strange remoteness, so that the girl feltsuddenly alone and lonely until Michael stopped,
resting his hands upon her shoulders trying to see
her downcast eyes, and, as if comprehending the hurt
behind her silence, all banter and lightness left his
voice.
"Look at me, Mary. Please, please don't turn away.
Of course you know just how I feel about you.
Haven't I been saying it in everything but words
for the last month? But when we were crossing those
Kansas plains, dear girl, I vowed to wait till I had
something worth-while to give you. It seemed only
the fair thing to leave you free. And then the look
in your uncle's eyes last evening showed me that,
after all, there is nothing more worth-while than
honest love. Will you take that, mavourneen? It is
all yours, for (believe me, won't you?) I have never
squandered it."
Still silence-but such a singing silence! (Oh,
stars of evening, how near, how near you are!).
"If you could love me a little. . . ."
...
Michael paused again, dropping his hold on the
girl's shoulders as if no touch of his should influence
her decision; so after a breathless moment it was
Mary who stretched out both hands to him, confess-
ing with a new, shy joyousness: "But, Michael
darling, I-I could not possibly love you more!"
It was late when the young people reached home;
and as Mary stepped softly across the threshold her
mother spoke in a cautious whisper:
"Mercy, child! Where have you been? Even youruncle Dan retired long ago; and your father has been
asleep an hour."
She groped for a candle, but Mary stayed her with
a glance toward Luther. "The light might rouse
him" (did her voice tremble a little?), "and I can
undress without it on this bright night. We took a
walk, Mother-a longer one than we intended, the
air was so soft and sweet after the shower. Have you
worried about me? I thought you wouldn't, knowing
I was with Michael."
"I worried only because you need sleep, dear, with
the hard day ahead of us. Daniel says that part of the
way we must climb afoot in order to lighten the
oxen's load, for the grade is almost unbelievable.
Hush!" (as Luther stirred), "we mustn't wake your
father. Good-night, child."
"Good-night," Mary responded in a whisper, and
kissed her mother, wondering a bit later when she
slid into her narrow cot, if Persis had ever felt as
she did now. No, of course she hadn't. That wasn't
possible; for, dear though her father was to all of
them, never, never could he have been as thrilling
a lover as Michael Warwick! Pondering this the girl
could easily have wept for all her precious mother
had been denied. And poor, darling Father! It would
be a blow when he discovered that his little daughter
was indeed grown up. Michael, who wished to share
this amazing fact with the whole world, had been
persuaded with difficulty to wait a while, "At least,"
urged Mary, "till we're away from Denver City; for
why upset him just as we start out?""Your father won't be upset once he gets used to
the idea," Michael assured her with a smile, "but your
word is law-that is, unless you make me wait too
long, Mary. Besides, I fear our own faces may be-
tray us. If you look one-half as starry-eyed to-morrow
morning as you do to-night, your mother, if no one
else, will guess the truth."
"I'll try then, to appear as gloomy as I can," she
told him, sighing so deeply that the young man
laughed. "But it will be hard work unless you keep
out of my sight, darling. You must ride ahead with
Uncle Dan and make him talk of his adventures, so
you'll forget me."
"Now," observed Michael, "you are asking the im-
possible! But I'll be good, sweetheart. I promise.
Here, kiss me again!"
So it happened that when the Alden party set
forth into the mountains early next morning, only
one member of that small cavalcade suspected the
secret the young people were guarding. Neither
Persis, busy at leaving the Bostonian's cabin in ship-
shape order, nor Luther, yoking up the oxen with
Michael's help, realized that something vastly mo-
mentous had occurred. It was Daniel Alden-the
fancy-free-the vagabond-the man who had never
"taken time to court a sweetheart," who, after one
glance at Mary's face declared not audibly, but
solemnly: "God bless her! And God pity young
Michael Warwick if ever he brings the precious girl
an hour's unhappiness!"U
CHAPTER XXIV
TP, up into the very sky itself! Thus dreamed
Mary Alden when late that afternoon the
patient animals were turned out to graze, and the
none-less-patient gold seekers dropped wearily, thank-
fully, onto the hard earth. Though from Denver City
the foot of the range had looked scarcely a stone's
throw distant, it was in fact fifteen long miles. And
first the whole party must be ferried across the Platte,
an expense of two dollars and fifty cents a-piece, at
which Michael protested, saying with humor that
since the river's current did all the work, the charge
was far too high! Once safely across the stream, how-
ever, all expenses ceased and their way continued
along a succession of low, treeless sand hills-past
occasional ranches where cattle and horses could be
boarded while their owners sought a fortune in the
high mountain "diggings."
Clear Creek at last, not swollen this time of year-
an easy crossing; while ahead, on a beaten trail so
steep that to the amazed New Englanders it seemed
that nothing less foot-sure than a mountain sheep
could scale it, they beheld miners toiling upward
under heavy burdens others more fortunate, climb-
ing beside pack mules-a prairie schooner drawn by
a dozen oxen, at which Persis cried:"Look, Daniel! How can our four beasts carry us
to that awful summit?"
"They'll not be asked to!" replied Dan Alden, in
great good humor at being on the trail once more,
"though that wagon you see ahead, Persis, is prob-
ably much heavier than ours. Here's where we men
begin to sweat, my dear, while for a few miles you
women can mount our steeds, if this lazy beast of
mine can be called anything so grand-sounding.
Mary's been wild to try Shamrock these many weeks,
and now she can do so with no one to criticize the
lack of side saddle. And you, Persis, can have the
novel experience of riding mule-back! Come,
Michael! Lift that girl into the saddle. We must be
off."
"But it's wicked to make the poor creatures carry
any load at all on such a grade," Mary protested.
"There's worse to come," promised her uncle,
laughing. "You'll have plenty of chance to use those
ornamental feet of yours, my girl. Here, Persis, I'll
help you up. And you're to forget your modesty and
ride like a man this morning. Be sensible. Do you
want to take a header on this rough trail?"
Considering his status as an "old bachelor,” Daniel
Alden arranged his sister-in-law's skirts with exceed-
ing deftness; while Michael Warwick did the same
for Mary, taking occasion (a wary eye on their
elders) to kiss the girl's hand as he bent to arrange a
stirrup.
"How long must this deception continue, sweet-
neart?" he whispered. "More than once I have caughtyour uncle looking at me with strange intentness. Do
you think he suspects?"
"Not from me," replied Mary, "though I've found
it impossible to look unhappy, as I'd meant to do.
Every time I pull down the corners of my mouth,
Michael, they turn right up again! And, try as I will,
I can't forget"
"Hush!" warned her lover, for Luther was draw-
ing near, a questioning eye on the young English-
man.
"Comfortable, daughter? Then let's move on,
Michael, so that the ladies can bring up the rear."
This modest suggestion was due to the fact that
Persis or Mary might unwittingly display an inch or
two of ankle, since their skirts, long and billowy
though they were, had not been cut to fit a horned
saddle.
"Call out if Shamrock misbehaves," admonished
Michael, as, taking the hint, he started forward; while
the two women waited until the prairie schooner and
pedestrians were on their way. "I have always wanted
a chance to ride astride," Persis confessed with a smile
when her husband was out of hearing. "A woman's
clothing is proper enough, I suppose, but far from
comfortable on a trip like this. Many a time when we
were crossing the prairies I envied your father the
ease with which he could get about."
on.
The girl laughed as their reluctant mounts moved
"So did I," she admitted, "but how shocked Father
would be if he could read our thoughts! Was he al-ways so good, Mother, even when you were both
young?"
"Do you think him too good?" countered Persis,
amused at the question.
"Not for a man of his age, perhaps; but I can
hardly imagine him as ever being well, impetuous."
The older woman pondered this as they rode

comparing your father with our friend Michael?"
Mary's color deepened just a trifle.
"Having no brothers of my own, Mother, Michael's
the only young man I have ever been with con-
stantly; and I can't help wondering if-if when he's

Persis laughed so merrily that the mule stopped
short in his tracks, and Michael Warwick, climbing

"I think it's safe to say he never will, child," she

happy heart. As for your father" (her face grew
serious), "I think he was never what you'd call im-
petuous. He liked good times as much as any young
fellow, but too often they were cut short because he
must be at home to read a chapter from the Bible
