"You mean that Grandma would let him leave a
party to do that, when she had eyes of her own?"
Mary's voice sounded indignant, and her mother
nodded."She did not ask it, perhaps; but she expected
it, and your father was burdened over-young with a
New England conscience. From a little boy he read
the Bible to her at bedtime, and I doubt if he missed
the duty more than a dozen nights till the day of
her death."
"Which accounts for his quoting it so often,"
observed Mary thoughtfully. "I've sometimes won-
dered if he knew the Book by heart."
"He can repeat whole chapters, anyway," responded
Persis, "and the quotations fall from his lips more
naturally, I believe, than his own thoughts. It's a
fine thing to be so familiar with the Bible, Mary;
and a wonderful thing to be so good a son yet..."
She paused, and the girl said: "You mean, I sup-
pose, that Grandmother was selfish?"
It was Persis' turn to flush, though she admitted:
"I often thought so; but perhaps I was unjust, being
only a young girl, my dear, and sometimes so lonely
there on the farm that I begrudged the evenings my
husband gave his mother. It may be that I was the
selfish one, for"
"The idea!" interrupted Mary. "There's not a self-
ish thought in your head, Mother, and never was.
What's more, in my opinion Father shouldn't have
given time to Grandma that belonged to you."
"Hush, child!" reproved Persis. "The dear boy
was only doing what seemed his duty, and I suspect
that at times it was not an easy duty by any means,
though I never heard him complain. That's why
I was thankful when he decided to make this journey,for in all his life your father's had hardly a day's
vacation. Even when we were married, Mary, he
couldn't leave his mother to take me on a honey-
moon."
Neither noticed until that moment that the prairie
schooner was at a standstill, for Luther had stopped
to rest the oxen and his own legs. The three men were
seated upon the ground, and as the riders drew near,
the word "honeymoon” reached those masculine ears
distinctly.
"May I ask whose honeymoon is the subject of
discussion?" said Daniel Alden as both Shamrock
and the mule (who went by the mournful name of
"Moses") paused, panting from the exertion of their
stiff climb. "Not yours, I hope, Mary my dear?" he
added, slyly.
His eyes twinkled wickedly; but the girl responded
with a self-possession that surprised her lover: "Far
from it, Uncle Dan; but I've just discovered that my
darling mother never had one."
"She is having it now," asserted Persis quickly,
and, fearing that Luther might be hurt by his
daughter's words, she blew him a light kiss, some-
thing so unexpected that the shy man flushed as he
arose; and Daniel observed with kindly tact:
"That was only because her bridegroom's brother
was sailing the high seas just then. He should have
been at home like a good boy, Mary, milking cows,
sawing wood, and keeping his mother company so
that the bridal pair would be free to enjoy a honey-
moon without worry-though, not having receivedan invitation to the wedding, how could the poor
chap know that he was needed?"
"But how could he receive an invitation when his
family never knew whether to address him at Cali-
fornia or Timbuctoo?" retorted Persis. "And a honey-
moon is not a matter of locality, Mary, but a state
of mind, as any happily married pair will testify.
Dear me!" she added, wishing to change the subject,
"poor Moses is terribly out of breath, but I dread
to relieve him of my weight because it's so difficult
to mount again. What shall I do?"
"Stay where you are," said Daniel. “In another half
hour you'll be forced to climb for yourself. There's
a gorgeous view from a spot a few miles on where
we can rest and have our noonday meal. Are you
girls comfortable?"
"Far more comfortable than the creatures we're
riding," smiled Persis. Then, as her husband drew
nearer: "What's the matter, dear man? You have a
worry frown between your eyes."
Dan Alden had already started on, and as Michael
seemed to be occupied with tightening Mary's saddle
girth, Luther looked up and answered: "I'm sorry
about the honeymoon, Persis. Truth is, I have al-
ways been sorry, only-only I never found a good
chance to say so. You were so young, my dear. I see
now that I should have asked Cousin Lizzie to stay
with Mother, and taken you on a little trip. You
would have had it to remember, and—and tell Mary
about."
There was something so pathetic in this late ad-mission, that Persis reached down to smooth the
wrinkle from his forehead with a loving hand.
"I have much to remember as it is, Luther," she
said gently, "and perhaps if we'd gone gallivanting
when we were young, this journey would not seem
so wonderful to us now."
"Perhaps not," her husband admitted, though his
eyes were still somber, "but we're young only once,
Persis, and I don't like to feel that I cheated you of
something every girl has a right to expect of the man
she marries."
"Come, come!" she said, leaning down from the
saddle to give his shoulder a little shake, "you mustn't
get into a mood like this, dear. Did you ever hear
me complain?"
Luther looked up at her, his eyes filled with such
a wealth of loyal devotion that a lump rose in his
wife's throat as he responded: "It's not that which
troubles me, Persis, but the thought that I never
realized in those old days that you had aught to com-
plain of. Can you forgive me?"
"Not unless you promise to forget such foolish-
ness," she answered lightly, in an effort to change his
unhappy mood. "There! Dan's calling for us to start.
Run along, my dear; but remember that I love you,
and that this trip is honeymoon enough for me."
But Persis was silent as they started on, so thought-
ful that Mary questioned: "What's the matter,
Mother? Did I really hurt Father's feelings when I
mentioned your lack of a wedding journey?"
Her mother nodded, adding when the girl's faceclouded: "Don't let it worry you, child. Under the
circumstances your innocent remark was natural
enough. What troubles me now is my own lack of
understanding. It seems that for years your father has
reproached himself on this very matter-yet I never
guessed it!"
"And I unwittingly touched the sore spot!" ex-
claimed Mary. "What a pity, Mother. We must try
to cheer him up before the day is over. It was clever
of Uncle Dan, I thought, to step into the breach and
take the blame himself. Not till then did I realize
that I'd said something clumsy."
"Look!" cried out Persis suddenly. "An ox team
is coming down this dreadful hill! Let's move close
to one side and give them all the road. What on
earth are they dragging behind the wagon?"
"A forest tree," replied her daughter, eyes bright
with interest at the sight. "Uncle Dan was saying
yesterday that they use them as brakes in these steep
places."
"It looks to me as if descending such a mountain
would be harder on the beasts than climbing up,"
responded Persis. "Hug close to the bank, Mary. I'm
thankful we are on the inner side."
The approaching wagon, a prairie schooner like
their own, went by all too fast for comfort, the
branches of its improvised brake switching the legs
of their mounts smartly, at which Shamrock snorted
her indignation, though the sluggish Moses merely
appeared to sigh.
It was more than an hour later when, breathlessand weary (for the women took the last mile on
foot), they reached the summit, looking down on the
top of Table Mountain where a tiny lake sparkled
like a jewel in the sunlight; and two miners, toiling
up the trail below, appeared as dwarfs. Far away, at
the foot of the tremendous slope, lay the river valley
-dark timber-the Platte rippling slowly, lazily, on
its never-ended journey to the sea. Around them
mountains were crowded one upon another-some
green with pine, spruce, and trembling aspens; others
towering above timberline were bleak and forbid-
ding, while patches of brilliant flowers brightened
the crevasses between the rocks nearby.
"Even in Switzerland I have never seen such
grandeur," declared Michael Warwick as he dropped
down at Mary's side. "Are you worn out, mavour-
neen? That was a stiff climb for one unused to scal-
ing mountains."
"Be still!" warned the girl, in fear that her uncle,
who stood gazing down upon the scene from a van-
tage point only a few feet off, might have caught the
endearing word. "Behave yourself, Michael," she
added, trying most unsuccessfully to look severe.
"What would my father think if he should hear you
call me anything so so intimate?"
But her light-hearted companion was not to be
rebuffed.
"That is a subject which I refuse to contemplate,"
he replied, laughing at her. Then, drawing himself
up formally: "Are you hungry after your long climb,
Miss Alden? If so""Did I hear you say hungry?" demanded Daniel,
turning so suddenly that Mary jumped, as if de-
tected in some crime. "As for myself, I could eat the
bark of a tree! Come, start a fire, young fellow.
These girls shall rest while we prepare the meal.
There's still a good hard climb ahead of them before
we can settle down to our night's sleep."CHAPTER XXV
T was two days later when Mary opened her
journal to write:
"Calico Cottage, Central City (and Journey's End
for the time being, I hope). Here we are at last,
high in this mountain gulch, and a part of the strange
community which makes up a mining town. Our
trip from Denver was one long to be remembered,
I am sure-up a wide, rough path as steep as the
roof of a house, with enchanting vistas on every side,
especially at our first nooning place, where my
thoughtful uncle insisted that Mother and I rest
our weary limbs while the men prepared lunch, and
a good lunch it was, we both admitted. We stayed
at this beautiful spot for a full two hours, giving
the remains of our meal to a couple of hungry gold
seekers who arrived just as we departed, and who
were so ravenous after their long climb that they fell
upon our 'scraps' as if they'd seen no food for days.
"Our way that afternoon led up and down, in and
out, across running streams, over huge, jagged
boulders, and alas!-past two tragic, new-made
graves, until at sundown we turned the tired animals
loose, ate a simple supper, and dropped gratefully
onto the pine boughs which Michael had cut for our
camp beds, and which felt like down because we wereso weary. I fell asleep even while trying to force my
eyelids open to watch the leaping flames of a big
bonfire, beside which Uncle Dan, whose strength
seems unlimited, sat for long hours talking with
three discouraged miners who are returning to the
States, one of whom told us next morning that mining
in Gregory Gulch did not pay so well as 'staying
at home with one's wife'!
"Oh, how I longed to keep awake in that wild
forest scene! The stars seemed so near the wind in
the pines sounded so musical! And I had so much to
be thankful and happy about that I was reluctant to
lose myself in 'sleep's sweet oblivion,' yet it seemed
but a moment before I was in dreamland, and then
awake again with morning sunlight dancing across
my face.
"We made an early start, and after descending a
hill almost as steep as those we had toiled up a few
hours since, we reached the city made famous as the
Gregory Diggings. It lies in a narrow gulch sur-
rounded by precipitous slopes on every side, which
are sparsely wooded when compared with those we
had scaled to reach it. Despite many fine pines, the
general lack of timber gives the place a disappoint-
ingly bare appearance; though a mountain stream
ripping through its midst is a delight to the eye. Log
cabins, grocery tents, wooden stores crowd one an-
other; and at the brook a half dozen women were
busily engaged in washing clothes, for which, we
learned later, they receive as much as three dollars for
a dozen pieces. (Mother declares that she sees nowwhy Uncle Dan urged us women folk to come into
the wilderness!)
"Central City is a curious settlement, boasting a
few fairly pretentious houses, while others perched on
stilts on the high hillsides, overhang road and creek
in a manner that looks perilous to me. Our men had
intended setting to work at once on a three room
log cabin; but we had hardly arrived when Uncle
Dan was offered one of the 'calico' cottages that over-
looked the gulch, at so low a price (the former owner
having built it for his family, who, at the last mo-
ment, refused to leave their Missouri farm) that it
seemed wiser to buy, though Mother had vowed she
would never live in a jig-saw trimmed mansion!
"Nevertheless, it is a snug place inside, well built
for such hasty building, and so much more comfort-
able than many of the neighboring cabins that we
feel ourselves fortunate to have procured it. Michael
even pretends to like the trimmings, and says that
painted white they would make the little house re-
semble a paper lace valentine-a transformation
which he plans to bring about in his first idle mo-
ment. Indeed, even Mother appears resigned to her
new home's fanciful exterior (perhaps because two
pine trees guard the door), and we have made it look
cosy and homelike within.
"The prairie schooner is, at present, our guest
room, in which Uncle Dan and Michael will con-
tinue to lodge for the time being. It has been a busy
day and"
Here Mary's important entry came to an abruptend, for Michael Warwick, entering the room quietly,
took the book out of her hands, and said, with
cautious glances in all directions: "Mary-my-darling,
I begin to wonder if, after all, you possess that use-
ful organ known as a heart. Stolen kisses may be
sweet as we're led to believe, but to my way of think-
ing they're too infrequent; and besides, it is high
time we confided in your parents. Lay down that
abominable pen, young lady. I loathe the sight of
it! Stand up and put your arms about me so I can
believe you are really flesh and blood and then
come out and face the music. I miss my guess if
your uncle is not already aware that you intend to
make me a member of his family."
"You may be right," admitted Mary, "for he had
the audacity to wink at me last evening when we
started out for our little walk. I suppose we must
tell them sometime, Michael, only I dread so to
upset my father. But if you think me cold-
hearted"
She arose suddenly, throwing her arms about his
neck with all the ardor an impatient man could de-
sire; but as their lips met, a shadow darkened the
room, and drawing hastily apart the lovers con-
fronted Luther Alden, who stood as if petrified in
the doorway.
Even the Englishman was surprised out of his
usual urbanity; so it was Luther who spoke first, say-
ing in a voice they hardly recognized as it thundered
through the little cottage: "Is this the way you re-
pay our trust and hospitality, young man?""Oh, Father!" cried Mary, running to him, and
drawing one of his arms about her, "don't speak like
that! It's my fault that we didn't explain sooner. You
mustn't blame us for loving each other. How could
we help it? And ever since we left Denver City
Michael has been urging me to tell you."
"That is the truth, sir," said Michael, coming for-
ward with quiet dignity. "I am sorry for the shock we
gave you, Mr. Alden; but I think you should know me
too well after all these weeks together, to accuse me
of anything dishonorable."
"Dishonorable!" echoed Persis, who, unbeknownst
to them all had come in softly, and, catching the last
words, had sensed the hurt behind them. "What is all
this? Of course Michael has done nothing dishonor-
able! What's the matter, Luther? You look"
"You might look strange yourself, Mother," broke
in her husband, "if you'd discovered our daughter
embracing this young fellow, as I did. How should I
know he wasn't taking advantage of her youth and
innocence? There, Mary, don't cry! If I said anything
unjust to Michael, I apologize. But I didn't
guess"
"And a great dunce you were!" exploded Daniel
Alden, blowing into the small room like the March
wind Mary once likened him to. "And you also,
Persis my dear, if you'll forgive my saying so. One
look into our girl's face on the morning we left
Denver City was all needed to tell me what had
occurred during the long stroll she took with your
young boarder the night before. You doting parentshave been blind as bats; and for the best part of
four days I've been laughing in my sleeve at you.
Come here, Mary. Dry those unnecessary tears and
let your old uncle give you a kiss of congratulation;
for you've chosen a damn fine man, my girl, or I
don't know the cut of one."
These hearty words had (as Daniel Alden in-
tended) changed the atmosphere of the little room;
yet instead of obeying his command the girl clung
to her father as she repeated: "It was my fault, truly,
not telling you at once. Won't you forgive me, Father
darling?"
"There is nothing to forgive, dear child," said
Luther gently, "but you're too young to marry now,
and if at the end of a suitable time you still feel drawn
to Michael"
"Oh, wake up, man! Wake up!" his brother in-
terrupted with vast impatience. "Mary is older by
two years than was Persis the first time I came home
from my wanderings and found you'd presented me
with a sister. I recall wondering if you'd snatched
her from the cradle, so young and sweet she looked.
Don't be a dunce twice in one day, brother. Sup-
pose Mary had set her heart on some roistering,
drinking gold seeker! Then you'd have cause to
rebel. Come now, don't be a wet-blanket on the
happiness of your only child."
"That's the last thing he would be," defended
Persis, coming close to encircle both Mary and
Luther with her arms. "He is just surprised, though
dear knows we had warning a-plenty! As for you,Michael" (she turned now, stretching out both hands
in welcome), "you are everything I could have
asked for in a son, dear boy, and I hope I can replace
in some small measure the mother you have lost."
It was then that Mary ran to throw her arms about
her uncle, whispering, as he kissed her a dozen times:
"Oh, Uncle Dan, you are so wonderful!"
"'All's well that ends well,'" quoted Daniel, his
eyes twinkling as he glanced over her head. "Your
father is shaking hands with Michael; and your
mother's weeping, which means (blast the perversity
of woman!) that she's happy beyond the power of
speech. Hi, Michael!" he roared. "How many times
do you allow another fellow to kiss your girl?"
"That depends on the fellow!" replied Michael,
laughing; at which Dan Alden kissed his niece again.
Late that same night, by light of a tallow candle,
Mary Alden finished the interrupted page in her
journal.
"It has been a busy day," she repeated, "and a
happy one, for Michael and I have confessed our-
well, it is difficult to put it in black and white, but
we are to be married soon as Michael makes his
fortune, and Father is outwardly reconciled to the
fact, while both Mother and Uncle Daniel are re-
joiced. I know that down inside my poor father is
really disconsolate, for he still regards me as a baby;
but, as Uncle Daniel pointed out, he always will!
We had a gala supper by way of celebration, with a
lighted candle at every place-a wanton extravagance,perhaps, but very festive looking and Uncle Daniel
produced a bottle of good wine, which however,
Mother refused to let him open as it would have
horrified Father, who is strict temperance. So our
health was drunk in pare mountain water, much to
Uncle Dan's chagrin, and (I fear) to Michael's
amusement, he being used to the drinking of wine
in his own country.
"I must not write more upon this subject so near
to my heart, for after all, this is not a personal diary,
but a record of these stirring times which we are
living; and it would be wrong to waste the pages with
accounts of my own trivial affairs, however absorbing
they are to me.
"This evening we sat on our small jig-saw decorated
porch, and listened to the songs of some miners who
are encamped not far away. Some of the voices were
very fine, and seemed in perfect harmony as they
drifted down the slopes above us. It was so impres-
sive that for a time we had no words, though Michael
was clasping my hand in his, hidden by the folds of
my full skirt; and I saw in the moonlight that Father
was holding Mother's quite brazenly. It made me a
little sorry for my Uncle Daniel, who has never had
a sweetheart. Perhaps Mother felt as I did, for when
the miners (their voices soft and husky in the dis-
tance) began 'Drink To Me Only With Thine Eyes,'
she reached out the hand that Father was not hold-
ing, and gave it to my uncle, who, I perceived,
clasped it gratefully, with a quick smile of thanks
and understanding. Watching that little scene I:
wondered who, save my darling mother, would have
been so thoughtful.
"To-morrow begins a new chapter in our ad-
ventures, for with the dawn our men set forth into
the gulch in search of gold. May success attend
them!"W
CHAPTER XXVI
ITH something akin to alarm, Mary Alden
wakened early one October morning to find
the gulches blanketed in white, while a stiff wind,
whistling through the pines, caused the "calico cot-
tage" to tremble ominously, although its builder had
chosen a sheltered spot for the home he had never
occupied. Her mother was still sleeping, and the girl
dressed quickly and quietly, for the air which seeped
through every crack and crevice was icy cold, and
she wished to start a fire before Persis stirred from
her warm bed.
Ten days before, discouraged with their lack of
success nearby, the three men had set out for a
more distant part of the mountains, and no word
had come from them since. This was not unexpected,
for Daniel Alden had warned the women not to
worry even should their men folk be absent for some
weeks, but this early storm seemed little short of a
calamity to Mary, despite the fact that each man
carried a heavy blanket, and her uncle had said that
snow might come.
The girl's hands were stiff with cold as she kindled
a fire, thankful for the good cook stove which a dis-
appointed miner had provided for the family that
failed him. This task accomplished, she went to awindow and peered out to where a half dozen cabins,
almost buried in snow since the night before, were
hardly to be distinguished from the hillside to which
they clung. Indeed, it was impossible to see any great
distance, for the air was thick with flying snowflakes;
and Mary's heart sank still lower, remembering the
three men who were, perhaps, without sufficient
shelter. It was then that an especially ferocious blast
shook the cottage with such force that Persis, rudely
awakened, sat up in consternation.
"Mercy! What's happened? Don't tell me we're
having a blizzard in October."
"Lie down," commanded Mary sensibly. "Keep
covered until the house warms up a little more. This
is a blizzard or I never saw one, Mother. How I pity
some of our poor neighbors with only dirt floors
under their feet, and leaky roofs overhead."
Said Persis, troubled eyes on a snow-covered win-
dowpane: "But your father, Mary-and Dan-and
Michael!"
Her daughter drew in a sudden breath.
“I know. I've been thinking of them every minute;
but we must try to put them out of our minds as
far as possible. Perhaps they're on the way home
now, Mother; and Uncle Dan's an old-timer, re-
member. If only for our sakes he wouldn't let Father
or Michael take any foolhardy chances. There! I do
believe the room is more comfortable already. It's
fortunate we carried in those kindlings yesterday.
I'll start the coffee while you're dressing, Mother;
or would you like me to bring you some in bed?"This suggestion moved Persis to gentle irony.
"Are you mistaking me for Queen Victoria, my
dear?" she questioned. “Or a helpless invalid?" Then,
after a moment: "What's that outside, Mary? I
heard"
"Only Zebulon Pike," broke in the girl, opening
the door just wide enough to admit the collie, a
gust of wind, and a flurry of snowflakes. "We're not
likely to have callers a day like this! I found the scamp
on my bed when I woke in the night, Mother, and
let him stay to keep my feet from freezing. He was
wild to run out into the storm this morning, but
now he's glad to hug the stove again. Watch him lick
the snow and ice out of his paws!"
Though in order to distract her mother's thoughts
Mary cooked a more elaborate breakfast than usual,
it was plain to see that during lapses in their table
talk, the minds of both women were with the absent
ones. Thus it was almost a relief when Tom Otis, a
young gold seeker from a nearby cabin, burst in upon
them covered with snow from head to foot, his ruddy
face tense with anxiety.
"Will you come home with me at once, Mrs,
Alden?" he gasped, quite breathless. "My wife's time
has come, and the doctor is flat in bed, raving with
some sort of fever. I rushed back to the cabin, afraid
to leave Polly alone a moment longer; but I know
nothing about such things and she needs help badly."
Almost before the words had left his lips Persis
was gathering up her wraps, slipping into the "bosom
friend" she had knitted when preparing for theirmigration, and tying a heavy woolen scarf over her
head.
"Bring your father's ulster from the closet, Mary,"
she called from the next room. "If I pin up my skirts
it will cover me from head to foot. That's it; and
if I'm not back by noon you might bring us some
warm food in a tin pail, unless the storm's too bad.
This is a first birth and there's no knowing how long
poor Polly will have to suffer. I sha'n't leave till
everything is over and she's comfortable again. What
a time for the doctor to be taken sick!"
"Wouldn't I be some help?" asked Mary as she
buttoned the worn old coat about her mother.
The older woman shook her head.
"If I need assistance I'll send Tom after you; but
like as not you'd just be in the way for that cabin
has only one room and is crowded enough as it is.
Keep the door bolted tightly, my dear, though you
won't be bothered with intruders in such a storm.
There! I'm ready at last. Let's not delay another
moment."
They were off hardly more than five minutes from
the time the frightened young husband had entered
the cottage; and though Zebulon Pike begged
piteously to follow, Mary kept a firm hand on his
collar as she closed the door behind them.
"I need you for company, if not for protection,
Zeb," she told him. "It looks as if we'd be alone for
hours, perhaps all day. Dear me!"-with a glance at
the clock on a shelf above the stove-"it's not half-
past seven! What shall I do to fill the time?"As if in answer, the dog sniffed at the breakfast
table, and Mary smiled.
"You're right," she said. "I can at least wash up
these dishes and cook what provisions we have on
hand. I'll start a soup out of that bit of beef we
bought yesterday, and make biscuits when it's nearer
noontime. That's a sensible dog!" as the collie lay
down with a long-drawn sigh. "You may as well be
resigned to your fate, Zebulon my dear. Oh, how I
wish the door would open and our men return!
Michael and I have certainly been separated long
enough."
The morning seemed endless. Mary tidied the little
house, and worried about Michael, her father, and
Daniel Alden. She wrote a long-promised letter to
Mrs. MacTavish, and another to small Lewis, Zeb's
former owner ("Though goodness knows I can't af-
ford the postage," she told herself.) The soup was
simmering fragrantly at last-a pan of biscuits brown-
ing in the oven; but the storm seemed to have in-
creased in fury, and, looking from a window, the girl
wondered if she could reach the Otis cabin should
she try.
"If only you were a St. Bernard, Zeb," she ob-
served whimsically, "I'd strap the food to your back
and send you out alone into the storm. As it is"
Mary paused, startled by a step on the little porch.
"Who's there?" she called, as a sharp rap sounded
on the door.
"Only me, Miss Mary," came the voice of their
young neighbor; and as the girl threw back the doorhe stumbled in, more spent and breathless than he
had seemed on his first arrival.
"Is is it all over?" she questioned as the fellow
sank limply onto a kitchen chair.
Tom shook his head.
"It's hell, Miss Mary. I hope to God we never
have another. Your mother sent me for the food, if
you've got it ready. She says to make a soup this after-
noon, and a pan of biscuits."
"I must be something of a mind-reader," smiled
the girl, "for they're already made; and if you'll come
back later I'll have a meat pie ready for your supper.
I was just this minute wondering if I could reach the
cabin with my hands so full and the wind blowing
a gale. Hold this pail as carefully as you can, please;
and I've left the biscuits in the pan to keep them
hotter. I'm so sorry Polly is having such a dreadful
time."
Again Zeb whined impatiently as the door closed.
An hour dragged slowly by-two-three. No word
from the cabin. Snow had ceased falling, but a wintry
blast still raged through the pine trees, and every
now and then the cottage trembled. At four o'clock
Zeb begged so piteously to go outside that Mary
relented, sure he would return after a moment of
freedom, for he disliked the wind; but when a half
hour passed without him, and the shadows of after-
noon began to deepen, the girl was filled with a sense
of loneliness, almost of fear. Twice she opened the
door to call: "Zeb!-Zebby! Come home. I need
you." But there followed no doggish answer, thoughonce, listening intently as the wind died down for
a brief moment, a faint, faint yelp from the hill
above seemed to reach her ears.
Had something happened to that faithful comrade?
Heartsick at this idea, Mary barred the door again,
a sense of helplessness sweeping through her, for
night was descending all too fast, and even if she
searched there was little hope that she could find the
collie. Save for wild gusts of wind there seemed no
sound in the whole world, and the lonely girl hoped
that her mother would send early for the supper
which, even now, stood ready in the oven. What a
relief it would be to hear the young man's voice
again to hear any voice! Besides, wasn't it possible
that Zeb had gone to the cabin in search of Persis?
If so, Tom Otis would bring him back, of course.
Why hadn't she thought of that before?
Buoyed up by this faint hope Mary's spirits bright-
ened; and when ten minutes later a welcome step
sounded without, she ran to the door and flung it
open fearlessly, only to utter a cry of terror as a
swarthy, cruel-mouthed Indian pushed rudely past
her into the warm room.S
CHAPTER XXVII
WEEPING a ruthless path through the Rocky
Mountains, that October blizzard beheld strange
sights. It passed a man battling against exhaustion
and wind and cold as he fought his way down the
steep slopes, through icy streams and unfamiliar for-
ests, refusing the tempting shelter of friendly, over-
hanging boulders, not stopping even to catch his
breath, for something-an elusive something he
could not name-had warned him that Mary Alden
was in danger. It passed a collie struggling to escape
the cruel trap that held him prisoner, lifting his
long nose to wail his anguish to an unheeding world,
fighting in impotent anger, tearing at the pitifully
torn flesh to free it, wailing again, for with the un-
canny wisdom of the dumb animal, the faithful dog
knew that his beloved mistress needed help.
Raging down beyond those wooded heights, the
blizzard tarried for a moment to peer in at a cottage
window, a cottage whose trimmings of "paper lace"
were white as flying snow; and, as if in anger at what
it saw, shook that small house until a tawdry bracket
on the wall, made with a jig-saw and painted gold,
slipped from its nail and fell to the floor with a
resounding crash. Then, winging its way down the
gulch again, the storm paused once more, just longenough to harken to a miracle: the faint, protesting
cry of a new-born child-before it swept on to
Denver City and the plains below.
To the three gold seekers high in the mountains,
the blizzard had come as a surprise. Still unsuccess-
ful, they had tramped most of the day before, and
night found them with no level spot to spread their
blankets. Necessity being "the mother of invention,"
Daniel Alden proceeded to drive stakes into the
ground, after which he and Michael rolled a log
against them, while the older man stood watching,
curious as to what it was all about.
"We'll lie with our feet against it, don't you see,
Lute?" explained his brother, somewhat amused at
Luther's puzzled frown. "I'll admit it's a strange
place for a bed, but it's better than rolling a half
mile down this hill. I've slept upright before, and
can do it again, I suppose. Let's turn in now. I for
one, am dead tired; and the night will be cold or I'm
no prophet."
"But it's clear, thank our lucky stars!" amended
Michael as he rolled into his blanket, "for it looks
to me as if we shouldn't get much rest."
Nevertheless, all three were so weary that despite
their unaccustomed position they were soon asleep.
It was Michael who waked first, conscious of some-
thing wet against his face. Moving cautiously, so as
not to disturb the others, the young man sat up, sur-
prised to discover that the stars, so bright when he
had closed his eyes, were entirely obscured, and the
air alive with stinging snowflakes. Even then, beingunused to mountain blizzards, the Englishman did
not realize their danger; and with a smothered:
"Blast this weather anyway!" he pulled his blanket
over his head and lay down again. But not for long.
It was scarcely five minutes before a gust of wind
whipped the covering from his face; and Daniel
Alden, starting up, exclaimed:
"Ye gods! we're in for it! Wake up, brother. It'll
soon be hell let loose, and we must find a better
shelter than this or we'll never see Central City
again."
Dazed by this rude awakening, yet thoroughly
alarmed, Luther Alden stared about, even as he
ducked his head against a second blast. Then, real-
izing that his brother had already started down the
slope they had climbed so laboriously a few hours
earlier, he staggered up, and throwing a blanket
across his shoulders for protection, followed blindly.
Stumbling, falling, grasping at tree trunks, rocks,
anything to help them keep a foothold, the three men
struggled on. Wind seemed to come from all direc-
tions now, sometimes helping them on their way,
then driving them east or west as it saw fit, until after
an endless, heartbreaking time Dan Alden turned
to shout over his shoulder: "We've done it ...
Reached
cave .."
those rocks
Though the blast took the words out of his mouth,
the two who followed understood that some sort of
shelter was at hand. And none too soon, for Luther,
having stepped into a hole and twisted an ankle, was
near exhaustion when at last they crawled into asort of cavern made by an overhanging pile of enor-
mous boulders.
Dawn found them huddled close together for
warmth, and covered by all three blankets. Not until
then did Luther Alden mention his accident, though
the ankle had been giving him exquisite anguish for
hours. It was badly swollen by this time, but as he'd
managed to loosen a shoe string they were able to
remove his boot without cutting the leather.
"Gosh, man!" cried Daniel as he applied a band-
age concocted from one of Michael's shirts, "even
if this storm should let up you can't travel to-day
unless we carry you! It's lucky we found this shel-
ter, for I'll admit now that I was afraid we'd missed
it. I noted the spot on our way up yesterday, and
thought then that come a storm it would be a good
place to dig in. We're safe enough here for another
twenty-four hours, and by then this swelling should
go down. It's a wonder you didn't break a bone."
Luther said nothing, for the pain was making him
a trifle faint. Michael also was silent as he peered
out through an opening at the swirling snow. It was
Daniel Alden who spoke the thought that was in all
their minds when he remarked suddenly: "I wonder
if this blizzard has reached the gulch, and if our
girls have got in a good supply of firewood."
His brother groaned, but whether from pain, or
distress at the possible plight of his dear ones, the
poor man hardly knew himself. Then Michael,
throwing an anxious glance toward the bandaged
ankle, said quietly: "If I were sure you'd be ableto step on that foot to-morrow, Mr. Alden, I would
be tempted to start down now and see if they're all
right. If I kept going steadily I could reach the gulch
before evening. Though we've been gone so long,
most of our time has been spent in digging; and I
doubt if we're more than a day's hike from home
right now. But on the other hand, if it should be
necessary to give you a lift to-morrow, your brother'll
need me."
Luther groaned again, at which Daniel broke into
a laugh, a strange sound considering their predica-
ment.
"If that bellow is meant to relieve your pain,
Lute," he observed with humor, "why keep it up,
for a sprained ankle is plain hell, as I know from
experience. But if you're groaning on account of
your women folk, it's just silly. Both Persis and Mary
are pioneers and will look upon an October blizzard
as a joke, or I don't know either of 'em. Besides,
haven't they got Zeb Pike for a defender?"
Michael's face brightened at this optimistic view
of the situation; but the older man said thought-
fully: "I know, Dan, but it's the unexpected things
that bother me. I'll confess I've been worrying, and
would have turned back yesterday if I hadn't been
afraid you'd think me chicken-hearted. Even a brave
woman, you know, is not proof against cold, and fire,
and-and plundering savages."
This was unanswerable, and there followed a
silence until Luther continued: "You're thinking.
perhaps, that I should have weighed these things inthe balance before starting out. Truth is, I did, Dan-
iel; but Persis knew how anxious I was to go and
wouldn't listen to my staying with her and Mary.
But all yesterday I felt uneasy, though I can't say
why. Perhaps it's just my cussed imagination-but
I'd give my eye teeth if one of us could get away."
"I didn't know you were over-strong on the im-
agination," returned Daniel, still speaking cheer-
fully; but his eyes looked troubled, and the English-
man knew that he felt disturbed. "Well," he went
on after a moment, "suppose we let young Michael
here start down? Why not? Did I ever tell you of the
time I carried a man weighing a good one hundred
and eighty pounds through ten miles of Mexican
jungle?" (He forbore to mention that at the time
of this exploit he was twenty years younger!) "What
I've done once I can do again, brother; though the
chances are that your ankle will be good as new after
a day's rest."
"But suppose that Michael should lose his way in
the storm, Dan?"
"He won't if he uses sense and keeps going south
and down," replied Daniel. "And if we're not safe
in Central City within a reasonable time he can get
a posse together and hunt the remains! Joking aside,
Luther, I think we'll all feel easier if he goes. Then
too, I've been planning. This storm will pass, of
course, and we'll have mild weather; but perhaps for
the winter we'd best do our prospecting nearer home,
for I see now how a woman (let alone two of the dear
things!) complicates the life of a would-be wanderer.How do you feel, Michael, about starting out alone
in a wild storm like this?"
"Feel!" If it had been possible to stand erect in
their low cavern, the young man would have been
upon his feet. "If I'd followed my inclination I
would have been off at daybreak. Still" (his face
clouded), “we mustn't decide this too impulsively
for if Mr. Alden's sprain shouldn't improve"
"It will," promised Daniel. "There's a stream not
far off, and when the wind dies down I'll fetch some
water and bathe the swelling, which is the best treat-
ment for an injury like this. We'll be 'snug as a bug
in a rug', Michael, so don't waste time worrying
about us."
"I must leave you my blanket," the young man
began, when Dan Alden stopped him with an em-
phatic gesture.
"No, Michael; you may need it more than we do.
I know you're thinking that Lute can't warm him-
self with exercise, but if he gets too cold he can have
my blanket as well as his, and I'll go outside and
dance a sailor's hornpipe to keep the blood moving!
Now remember this: whatever happens, don't lose
your head, boy. Keep south and down, and you'll
reach the gulch before sunset, though from the looks
of the sky I fear that little act of nature will not be
noticeable to-night! Hug Mary for me; and tell
Persis I'll bring her man home safe or die in the
attempt. And that's no joke, either. I mean it." Dan
Alden's eyes were serious a moment, then brightened
as he added gaily: "It looks as if my big brother andI would have a chance to get acquainted after all
these years! Now be off."
But Michael hesitated, turned, knelt down by
Luther.
"You want me to leave, sir? You'd rather I went
ahead?"
"Much rather, unless-well, unless it's unfair to
Daniel," the man answered with a wince of pain.
"Unfair!" echoed Dan Alden, laughing. "With a
warm cave, a pack full of grub, a couple o' blankets
and a brother to keep me company? Why, it's not
even a hardship! Quit worrying, Luther, and let the
boy go on. There's a long, cold trail ahead of him,
and the sooner he starts the sooner he'll reach the
women. Good-bye, lad. Good luck to you, and happy
days!"
So with this cheery farewell ringing in his ears,
young Michael Warwick set out alone into the
storm.
Afterward the Englishman tried to recall just
where he was when the first strong sense of Mary's
need swept over him. There had been many times
during the long, long hours of afternoon when he
thought he had reached the gulch, only to find an
endless wilderness stretching out ahead. Weary al-
most to the breaking point, his legs seemed to move
mechanically; and at last, reaching an overhanging
boulder, he paused a moment, wondering if it would
not be wise to rest and fortify himself with some of
the scant supply of food he had brought with him.Was it then, after scarcely a bite, that something told
him to press on? Was it then that he first felt, eerily,
that danger was approaching his beloved? Or was it
when he stopped at the brink of a mountain stream,
dreading to splash into the icy water, trying to peer
through flying snow to find a narrow crossing, that,
almost as if speaking out of the air, a voice cried:
"Hurry! Hurry!" while for a dazed moment Michael
wondered if he had lost his mind, so clear were the
words that urged him into the cold brook. Was it
then? He could not say.

CHAPTER XXVIII
OU have a beautiful little daughter," said Persis
Alden, smiling down into the new mother's
haggard eyes.
It had been a hard day for all three occupants of
the small log cabin; and Tom Otis looked almost as
spent as his girl wife. During those long hours Persis
had forgotten her own daughter, waiting alone while
the strange October blizzard raged outside. The rôle
of midwife was a new one to the New England
woman, for, having lived on an isolated arm so
many years, she had never been called in at such a
time. In fact, more than once during that weary day
her heart had failed her, wondering if she were doing
all in her power to ease the suffering of her young
neighbor; and now that the ordeal was over, she felt
suddenly so weak and exhausted that her hands trem-
bled as she bathed the baby and laid it, snug and
safe, at its mother's side.
"Now sleep," she said. "Sleep soundly, and by
morning you will have almost forgotten the anguish
you've been through, incredible though it seems to
you now. Good-night, my dear."
"I think," whispered the young man softly, "that
she's asleep already."
Persis bent closer."You're right; and no wonder after what the poor
girl's suffered to-day. Now let's sit down and have a
cup of tea. We won't bother to get the supper Mary
has ready, for you need sleep more than food, Tom.
You are almost as weak as your wife, which is no
wonder, either. The day my own child was born Mr.
Alden was so upset that he couldn't manage the
chores, and had to send to the village for someone
to help him."
"I-I didn't suppose it would be so awful," con-
fessed the youth.
"I can see no sense in it myself," admitted Persis,
laughing softly. "Though it would shock my hus-
band to hear me say it, perhaps, I have often won-
dered why the good Lord arranged things in such a
way. There! Tea revives one, doesn't it? and I must
get into my ulster. No" (as the young man arose),
"you're not to escort me. The storm has subsided
since that last terrible gust of wind, and I'm amply
able to take care of myself. Besides, if Polly woke up
and found herself alone she might be frightened."
"I'll go down the gulch and get somebody to stay
with her," he suggested.
But Persis was firm.
"You look as if you'd been drawn through a knot-
hole," she declared with truth, "and I'm not so young
and beautiful that I'll be in danger going a quarter
of a mile alone! Finish your tea, my boy, if you can
keep your eyes open long enough; and I'll be off.
Poor Mary has had a lonely day all by herself, and
will be thankful to see me back.""But I'd feel easier to go with you; and it seems
ungrateful”
"Ungrateful!" scoffed Persis as she tied the scarf
firmly about her head. "Who would refuse help at
such a time? I'll be down bright and early in the
morning; and if you follow my directions everything
will be all right. Don't worry about me," she added,
reassuringly.
"I do believe the worst of this storm is over,"
observed Tom hopefully as, still reluctant to let this
helpful neighbor go alone, he opened the door and
peered up at the sky. "I'll leave a lamp in the win-
dow, Mrs. Alden, till I'm sure you're safe home. I-
I don't know how to thank you for all you've done
for us to-day."
"Pass a kindness along to someone else when you
have a chance," smiled the good woman, waving her
hand cheerily as she set out up the gulch in the
drifted snow.
She had forgotten the possibility of drifts, and the
way seemed longer and steeper than she'd antici-
pated; but though no stars were visible, snow bright-
ened the night, and Persis was not afraid as she
ploughed her way steadily toward home. Not until
the cottage came into view, and she saw no light
shining from its front window, did her heart misgive
her. Surely Mary would not sit in the dark alone!
Besides, she would have lighted a lamp to guide her
mother up the hill. What could have happened?
Had the girl started out bringing their supper, and
lost her way? That wasn't possible. The path wasstraight enough even drifted as it was now; and in
any case, Zeb would have led her home.
Thoroughly alarmed, Persis hurried as fast as
weariness and lack of breath would let her; but it
was impossible to make swift progress through such
drifts. The wind, which had risen again, was blowing
straight in her face, and the frightened woman was
trembling with exhaustion when at last she stumbled
blindly up the steps; and at that very moment a man
carrying some heavy burden, rounded a corner of
the little house.
"Mrs. Alden! Why are you out on such a night?
Where's Mary?"
Michael's voice! From sheer relief Persis sank
down on the top step; and as the dog gave a quick
bark of joy at sight of her, she realized that it was
Zeb the young man carried. But she had no breath
for reply, and, alarmed at her stillness, Michael cried
out again: "Where's Mary?"
It was then that Persis saw blood dripping from
the collie's foot. The sight filled her with fresh for-
boding, and she sprang up, explaining: "I was called
away and left her here with Zeb, early this morning.
But-but the house is dark, and"
Michael Warwick needed no further words. He
was up the steps, had dropped the collie on the
porch, and bursting in at the unbarred door, called:
"Mary! Mary, where are you? Mary!"
No answer save silence, and a rush of wind through
the little house.
"Strike a light!" cried Persis. "The matches are ina tin box on Charlie Emerson's gilt bracket." Then,
after a moment: "Why, it's not here, and"
But Michael had already produced a match from
his pocket, and in another minute the lamplight
showed them a disordered room. On the table lay a
half-devoured meat pie, and a loaf of bread which
had been bitten or torn apart. Dresser drawers stood
open, their contents flung ruthlessly about. Matches
were strewn upon the floor, the tin box lying over-
turned near the stove. There had been a struggle.
The story was there for anyone to read; and for the
first time in all her forty efficient years, Persis Alden
felt the world slipping as she sank down onto a
kitchen chair.
"I must not faint," she told herself sternly. "There
are things to do. I-I must not faint," while from
far, very far away she heard Michael cry out: "My
God! where is she?" and knew, though her vision
blurred, that he was searching every nook and corner
of the place. "Mary!" he called again, distraught
with fear, "Mary!" And the next minute, sounding
above the young man's far-off voice, a bark told Per-
sis that the collie was still outside, and, fighting back
deadly faintness she arose, opening the door while
Zeb limped in, a mangled foot upheld though his
tail wagged gratefully.
Then, all in a moment, the dog raised his head,
growling; and as if forgetting his torn foot, ran all
about, sniffing at this and that, uttering strange, short
cries, lifting his nose to smell the air again.
"He he knows something has happened,"
...breathed Persis, awe in her breaking voice; while
Michael, his young face curiously aged in those, few
moments, cried: "Find her, Zeb! Find Mary! Where
is she?"
The dog looked up, his big eyes almost human
in alert intelligence. Then, nose to the floor, he cir-
cled the whole room, ran on three feet through the
woodshed, stopped at the closed door, whining, and
as Michael opened it, dashed out into the snow,
leaving a red trail behind him.
"Light a lantern," called Michael over his shoul-
der; while Persis, her fingers shaking clumsily as she
obeyed, stood for a moment at the door to hold her
beacon high, until, unable to bear further suspense,
she too, followed that trail of blood.
"This way," shouted Michael, for as if to aid them
the freakish wind had died again, and the lantern's
beam shone brightly in the still night. Yet it was
scarcely needed in that thin, snow-lit air, and in
another moment Persis Alden saw that the good
collie had led them straight and true.
Mary lay face down, sheltered and hidden by a
pile of boulders, hardly more than a hundred feet
from the cottage door. Kneeling beside her, Michael
turned the girl gently, while Persis gasped: "Is-is
she"
"God knows," broke in the young man, his voice
harsh with feeling. "There's a bruise here on her fore-
head, and-" He was stumbling up, the limp form
in his arms. "Lead the way, Mother Persis, so I won't
trip on any of these hidden stumps. Thank heaventhe dog found her so soon! We might have searched
for hours."
It was two days before Mary opened her dark eyes
-long hours of living death to the four who watched
her; for true to Daniel Alden's promise, he and his
brother were home before noon of the second day.
Persis had hardly rested in all that time; though the
Englishman, sheer fatigue overcoming him, had
dropped asleep at dawn after bathing and binding
up Zeb's mangled foot. Persis found him on the floor
beside the dog, when, having done all possible for
Mary, she went into the kitchen for a glass of water.
"Poor boy!" she murmured, and covering Michael
with a blanket, let him sleep; though in a few hours
he was up again, conscience stricken, and made her
rest while he watched by Mary's bed.
"If there were only another doctor in this curséd
place!" he blazed; and turning back, Persis said
gently, trying to give the comfort she craved herself:
"I've been thinking about that; but if this is a
concussion from striking her head when she fell
against those rocks, Michael, there is nothing to do
but wait, and even a doctor could not help us very
much. Our cousin Hezekiah White at Enfield had
just such an injury when his horse ran away and he
was thrown from the carryall. He lay unconscious for
a whole week, if I remember rightly, and then came
to. Please God, Mary will come out of this in time
if pneumonia doesn't set in from the chill she suf-
fered. Wake me in a couple of hours whether shestirs or not. Oh, how I wish her father were here
now!"
Michael arose to put his arms about her. He could
not speak, but the mute embrace left the girl's
mother vaguely comforted.
An endless day dragged by a day in which
kindly frontier neighbors came and went, speaking
in whispers offering help bringing cooked food
for the weary watchers; while young Tom Otis vol-
unteered to fight his way to Denver City and return
with a doctor.
But Persis shook her head.
"It would be suicide, Tom; and even if you suc-
ceeded it might be too late. We must just wait, I
suppose....”
Another night. Another morning bringing Luther
and Daniel, who heard the tragedy from a neighbor
before they reached the cottage. Thus they came in
silently, Luther Alden's eyes so stricken that, for the
first time, his wife gave way to tears.
Yet had they but known it, Mary was to come back
to them before many hours. It was near midnight,
and there had been no rest in the little house. At
intervals one after another the four who loved her
tip-toed in and out of that small, quiet bedroom,
looking down hopelessly on the girl who lay so still,
so ominously still; but as it happened, Michael was
there alone when Mary opened her dazed eyes. For
just a moment, while the young man held his breath,
she looked about. Then slowly, wearily, her eyelids
closed again.
...At dawn she spoke.
"What-what has happened? Why are you here-
beside me, Mother? Where"
"Thank God!" cried Luther Alden, the words
wrenched out of his throat like a harsh sob.
"Hush!" warned his wife, and bent above the girl.
"You have been hurt, darling," she answered, "but
all's well now. Just sleep. . . .”
"I wish," breathed Mary softly as her eyelids
drooped, “I-I wish Michael-would-come-
home....”
But she was fast asleep when Michael touched
her hand.

CHAPTER XXIX
OU see," said Mary, vaguely troubled, "I can't
remember beyond a certain point."
This was ten days later, and the girl, propped up
against pillows, was wearing a soft jacket trimmed
with rosy swansdown which made her look (to quote
Michael) "like a pink angel." Yet this same garment
had been a subject of hot dispute between her par-
ents; for it was a gift of a woman of unsavory char-
acter who lived over a saloon, and Luther Alden was
truly horrified at the thought of his adored Mary
wearing it.
"But the jacket's brand new," defended Persis.
"She took pains to explain that she had never used
it; and the poor woman meant kindly. Think what
you will, Luther, she was one of the first to come,
bringing a pail of delicious soup for our lunch. This
is a frontier town, remember, and good, bad, or in-
different, we're all neighbors. How could I hurt her
by refusing the gift? And Mary never had so pretty
a thing before."
"It looks like the devil's own garb to me," com-
mented Luther, so gloomily that his brother burst
into a hearty laugh.
"It would take more than that fuzzy pink trimming
to make our Mary resemble a lost soul!" he declared."And the instinct of a good woman seldom goes
amiss, Lute. If Persis had rebuffed the kind impulse
of such a creature she'd have been pushing her down
a step, wouldn't she, instead of giving her a lift up?
Where's your Christian charity that our good old
parson preaches every Sunday in the white church
at home?"
Luther flushed a little, and sighed deeply as he
retorted: "But doesn't the Good Book tell us that 'he
that toucheth pitch shall be defiled therewith'?"
"And who said: 'He that is without sin among
you, let him first cast a stone'?" countered Daniel.
"You're not the only Alden who can quote the Bible
to his purpose, Luther," he boasted, "though I'll ad-
mit my knowledge of the Book was imbibed in early
youth! Perhaps, not having led so blameless a life as
you, I'm more lenient with sinners; but let's ask
Michael if he can take his eyes away from Mary
long enough to reply."
"What's that?" asked the young man, turning his
head at the mention of his name.
"We wish to know if you've any objection to your
sweetheart's wearing the gift of a"
"Hush, Dan!" broke in Luther with a glance
toward Mary, not knowing how his scapegrace
brother meant to proceed; but the girl only laughed
softly as she said: "Don't worry, Father. How could
I be contaminated by anything so lovely as this pink
swansdown (which I've always adored and never
hoped to wear); and perhaps it helped the poor soul
to give it to me.""There!" exclaimed Daniel. "Didn't I say you
could trust a woman's instinct? Do you agree with
me, boy?"
"I'm willing, anyway, to trust Mary's!" the young
man answered. "Now let's be quiet while she tells
us what happened that dreadful night. I'm impatient
to hear."
It was then that Mary said: "But you see, I can't
remember beyond a certain point."
"What point?" questioned her uncle as he drew
a chair close to the bed. "Or rather, begin at the be-
ginning, Mary, and tell us as far as you can. When
was it that the savage burst in upon you?"
"It was getting dusk; and I'd opened the oven door
for fear the meat pie I'd made for Mother's supper
would be ruined. When I heard a step outside I
thought it was Tom come for the food, and I was
so tired of being here alone that I ran to the door
without stopping to ask who was there."
"Oh, Mary!" cried out her mother. "How could
you be so careless?"
"It would have made no difference," observed
Daniel wisely. "The man would have gotten in if
he'd had to break a window and crawl through.
What did you do, my dear, when you saw that your
caller was an Indian?"
The girl closed her eyes, while Michael said gen-
tly: "Don't go on, Mary, if it tires you too much
to talk."
"It's not that," she responded. "I was just trying
to remember. I think I screamed. I-I was terrified,you see. He was very tall, or that's how he looked to
me-and very dirty, with a cruel mouth, not like that
nice old Indian we talked with at the grocery tent
one day. He looked me all over from head to foot
(I was against the wall there, clutching at it, for my
knees felt as if they had turned to water); and then
suddenly he spied the pie in the oven, and pulled a
chair up to the table and pointed to the food, grunt-
ing out some words in his own language. It was plain
he expected to be fed, so I set the pie and a loaf of
bread before him; and all the time I was shuddering
because of how he'd looked at me, and remembering
how other women have been carried away into cap-
tivity."
"Oh, my poor child!" broke in Luther Alden;
while Daniel, his eyes never leaving the girl's face,
asked: "Where was the rifle?"
"Behind the stove, Uncle Dan. I thought when he
got absorbed in his meal I might reach it; but he
kept an eye on me every minute, and when at last
he was through, the beast began turning up the
room, searching for something-gold, perhaps, for
he emptied the drawers in the dresser and flung
things about. It was then I saw my chance and
grabbed for the rifle, but he was too quick for me,
after all. He wrenched it out of my hands, and for
a moment I thought he was going to beat out my
brains with the butt end. I-I almost wished that
he would. Anything seemed better than going away
with him. When he took the gun I struck at him
with both my hands. I scratched and bit like a cat!I didn't suppose that I had so much strength, but-
but he got one arm 'round my shoulders and a hand
on my throat
دو
"Don't go on!" cried Persis. "I-I cannot bear it!"
But Mary seemed not to hear as she continued:
"It was then-just as my strength was giving out,
that something happened, a—a sort of miracle. There
came a furious gust of wind that shook the whole
house so that I thought it was going to collapse and
deliver me out of the hands of that wild beast. And
suddenly, the gilt bracket that Charlie Emerson made
for us fell to the floor with a crash"
Mary drew a deep breath, while Michael, his voice
husky, prompted: "What-what then, dear?"
"It-it caught his attention the gilt paint, I sup-
pose. Don't you see, Michael, the Indians love bright,
gaudy things: beads, trinkets, gay colors? Perhaps
he thought the bracket was made of gold, for he
dropped his hold of me and picked it up off the
floor and laughed, like a child pleased with a new
toy. There was not much strength left in me, and
I was out of breath, but I remember wondering if
I could possibly steal to the shed door before he
noticed.
"Well, that's all every word I can tell you. I
don't recall another thing till I woke up safe in bed
with Mother beside me. I must have eluded him
somehow; but it's far more likely that he thought
a gold bracket would be easier to carry away through
the storm than a squaw who would fight and scratch
as I did!"There followed a silence, each one, perhaps, giv-
ing inward thanks for Mary's miraculous escape.
Then to break the tension Daniel Alden said with
a wink at the Englishman: "You'd better think twice,
Michael, before wedding a lass who can get the
better of a wild Indian. You'll be putty in her
hands!"
"I'm that already," confessed the lover, smiling;
but Luther Alden was in no mood for levity just
then. He said, his deep voice solemn:
"Surely, daughter, 'God moves in a mysterious
way His wonders to perform.' "
"True,” agreed Persis, "and yet" (her mouth
curved in a reminiscent smile), "and yet, Luther, I
seem to recall someone who was distinctly irritated
at Charlie Emerson for giving us such a useless part-
ing gift as a gilded bracket!"
A soft, tired little laugh broke in upon them, and
the girl said: "She's right, Father. You called it an
'infernal contraption,' don't you remember? And-
well, I do believe that it saved my life!"
Later that day, while Mary rested, the others held
a family council, speaking in low tones so as not to
disturb her.
"For all the gold in the Rocky Mountains I
wouldn't leave the women here alone again, Dan,"
said Luther Alden.
"Nor I," answered his brother. "But if I hadn't
thought we had a chance of striking something be-
fore snow fell, Luther, I'd never have brought youall into this gulch. There'll be no digging from now
till spring except on occasional days when it'll be
possible to do a little prospecting near at hand. As
it happens, I had a bit of luck last June that netted
me enough dust to see us through the winter, so don't
worry on that score; but as you all know, I'm a bad
one to sit still long in any place, and there'll be
nothing to occupy us here for the next five months."
"I believe you're uneasy already!" teased Persis,
with a smile for the man's restlessness.
Dan threw her a shamefaced grin, and turned to
Michael.
"How do you feel about all this?" he queried.
"Do you want to sit by the fire and twirl your thumbs
through a long, cold winter-a lad who must dig a
fortune out of the hills before he can marry his
sweetheart?"
"Who said he must?"
This question, coming from the next room, star-
tled them all, for none had seen that the door
between the rooms was ajar, and that Mary was lis-
tening. "I've done lots of thinking while I've been
lying here," the girl went on, much to the conster-
nation of Luther Alden, and the utter surprise of
them all. "Life looks pretty short and uncertain at
best; so, if it won't break Father's heart entirely,
and if Michael wants me, I-I think maybe I'll marry
him next week!"I
CHAPTER XXX
Fanyone had dared to prophesy that Luther
Alden's beloved daughter was to marry an Eng-
lishman-that her wedding would take place in a
raw, rough settlement high in the Rocky Mountains,
two thousand miles from the peaceful New England
farm where she was born-that among those who
witnessed the ceremony there would be, besides a
handful whose names were to go down in history,
miners, gamblers, bankers, lawyers, two Kansas Bor-
der Ruffians, a small, small sprinkling of the gentler
sex, and (oh, shades of Luther's straight-laced, Puri-
tanical mother!) a "scarlet woman," the good man
would have considered this prophet a fit subject for
an asylum.
Had he been told that Mary's first home was to
be a two-room log cabin perched in a lofty niche of
those rugged heights, and that in the course of years
she would see granite quartz mills, school houses,
brick blocks, hotels and churches crowding each other
in those quaint and narrow streets that she and her
husband (not to mention Persis and himself!) were
to participate in the opening of an "Opera House"
built of granite from those very hills, and so perfect
in architecture and decoration that more than half a
century later, when its doors had been closed fortwenty years, and the place was little more than a
deserted "ghost town," it would be opened again as
a memorial to those Empire Builders who had blazed
a trail through that very wilderness-that four of
the hickory chairs which seated the audience would
bear their names, and that they themselves would
be impersonated by Mary's children and grandchil-
dren-would the New England farmer, ploughing
his rocky fields, have believed such a fantastic tale?
Yet all these amazing things were to come true.
As Mary feared, Luther opposed her plan with
vigor.
"Married next week!" he exploded. "A girl still in
bed after a fractured skull! Have you lost your
mind?"
"No,” smiled his daughter, "I think instead that
I've just found it! We can be young only once,
Father; and didn't you want to spend your own
youth with the girl you loved?"
"But I had a home to offer her, my dear," retorted
Luther, making an effort to speak quietly. "And,
such as it was, a decent living; while Michael hasn't
even a shelter for your head."
The young man smiled. Not once had he spoken
since Mary's amazing proposition. Now he said: "I
think I could manage that, sir. There'll be many idle
men in the gulch for a while now, who will be glad
to help me put up a good log cabin; though I admit
it will take more than a week to build one com-
fortable enough for this young lady.""I should say so!" This was Persis, finding her
voice at last, for she was every bit as surprised as
Luther. "Let's say a month from now, Mary, if your
father's willing; which will give me time to concoct
a wedding gown. My only daughter is not to be mar-
ried in a red and green plaid poplin, whether she
wishes to be or not."
The woman delivered this ultimatum with such
spirit that all save her husband were forced to laugh;
and Mary said: "But you can't go down over that
dreadful trail to Denver City to buy material, even
if you were sure of finding something suitable when
you got there."
"I don't need to," replied her mother briskly. "My
own wedding gown, as fine a piece of white taffeta
as ever was, is in the cowhide trunk. Yes, it is, Mary,
though you don't believe me. I repacked the whole
thing one day when you went to the village, and put
my dress at the bottom where you wouldn't see it
and think me addled for taking it along. I had a
feeling that some such contingency would arise; and
it's fortunate I'm handy with my needle. I can prom-
ise you a wedding gown that you'll not be ashamed
of. What do you think of this plan, Daniel?"
"I know what I'd think if I were in Michael's
shoes," replied Dan Alden. "I'd take the girl up on
her first proposition, Persis, if her wedding gown had
to be rags. Who knows but she'll change her mind
in another month?"
"It strikes me that you've lost your minds, every
one of you," declared Luther angrily. "I'm surprisedat you, Persis, for aiding and abetting the girl in
such a wild idea."
"You didn't think it any wild idea when Parson
Gilder advised Aunt Sarah to let us be married in
the fall instead of waiting till spring, as we first
planned," his wife reminded him, her eyes twin-
kling.
"But as I said before, I had a home to give
you"
"And you have one now to give Mary if trouble
comes to her,” broke in Persis. “Will she be less our
daughter when she's married to Michael? Would you
be unwilling to share our last crumb with her,
or"
"And besides," Dan Alden interrupted, "I'll give
them my luck of last June for a wedding present,
to be laid aside in case of an emergency, if that makes
Lute feel easier; though in my opinion it's a poor
gift that has a string tied to it."
"I did not know you were such an advocate of
marriage," observed his brother with a touch of
sarcasm.
"But you see," Daniel responded, the corners of
his handsome mouth curving upward, "in my hum-
ble opinion there are only two women in the world
worth the bother of marrying. You got one of 'em,
Luther, and Michael is pledged to the other; so
what is there left for an old bachelor like me?
Besides" (the banter went out of his voice, leaving
it bleak), “I don't want to feel that Mary will face a
lonely old age like that which is ahead of me.""You need never have a lonely old age while
Michael and I are living, Uncle Dan," Mary assured
him. "But of course we sha'n't be married now if
Father thinks it is so dreadfully unwise."
Though these words sounded docile, they were, the
girl suspected, the quickest way to win her father;
and Luther answered: "Far be it from me to deny
you any happiness, dear child; and if your mother
thinks best I suppose I can get used to the idea, for
I'm no spoil-sport."
"I should say not!" spoke up Michael warmly. “I
think you have a right to feel just as you do, Mr.
Alden; and in your place I should probably act the
same. But somehow I'm sure that I'll find a way to
take care of Mary, or I wouldn't marry her; and as
she says, we want to spend our youth together."
"Well," sighed Luther, "what can I do if you are
all against me? But Thanksgiving Day is soon enough
for a wedding, and not one hour before."
"What a grand idea to be married on Thanks-
giving Day!" exclaimed the girl. "And we already
have a wedding present the beautiful silk quilt that
Granny Phillips pieced for me!"
"And she forgets my gold dust as soon as this,"
returned Dan Alden, shaking his head in mock
reproach.
“Oh, no, I haven't!" contradicted Mary. "But we
can't take the whole of your summer's earnings,
Uncle Danny. That would be wicked."
"The whole or none," declared her indulgent
uncle. "What's money for, if not to give away whenothers need it? I've made a plenty of filthy lucre,
Mary my dear, rolling stone though I am; but it
never stays long in my pocket, and a bank account
is something I have no use for."
"It's a thing that comes in handy when folks get
old and gray, Daniel," reproved his brother.
"Perhaps," agreed Dan, "but I don't see myself tot-
tering just yet, Luther; and if worse comes to worse
there's always the poor farm, you know, or"
It was on the tip of his tongue to add "or the
river," but he thought better of it, not wishing to
strike a gruesome note in the midst of these plans for
Mary's wedding.
"The poor farm!" sputtered Persis. "What talk,
Dan Alden! If you end at the poor farm we'll all be
there to keep you company. I promise that."
"Thanks, sister," said Daniel, his eyes smiling
at her; and then, rising suddenly: "Come, Michael.
With only a month to hew the logs and build a cas-
tle for your queen, we mustn't let grass grow under
our feet. Let's go down the gulch and gather in re-
cruits. Tom Otis will jump at the chance to pay his
debt to Persis; and I can name a dozen others who'll
be glad to help during this dull season. Then next
we must pick a building lot. Will Mary trust us to
choose it for her?"
"It looks as though she'd have to," smiled Persis,
"but let it be nearby, Dan, for with Michael away
prospecting half the time, her home mustn't be in a
lonely place, or I'll worry my hair white."
"Why not build on that spot by the old pine, ashort way above here?" questioned the bride-to-be.
"Don't you remember, Michael, how often we've
spoken of the fine view from there?"
"The very place!" agreed the Englishman. "We'll
take a look at it now. Come, Mr. Alden," he added
tactfully, for though Daniel was already at the door,
his brother had not stirred. "You must have a voice
in choosing Mary's homesite; and these two women
can spend the afternoon planning the wedding
gown!"
He stooped to give the girl an ecstatic kiss, and
would, in sudden exultation, have embraced Persis,
but she was already on her knees before the cowhide
trunk.A
CHAPTER XXXI
WEDDING in the gulch! The news ran from
mouth to mouth till even Luther Alden was
caught up in the excitement, though there were
times when such gloom descended on him that, had
it not been for long hours of work on the log cabin,
he would have had difficulty in keeping it to him-
self. And as the good man said, he was "no spoil-
sport." Not for worlds would he have marred the
pleasure of his wife and daughter in their plans and
preparations for the most momentous day of Mary's
life.
"Look!" exclaimed Persis, when she lifted her
gown from its long hiding place. "The silk has
hardly yellowed at all, Mary, though I haven't worn
it for eighteen years, when Julia White was married
in the church at Enfield. Strange isn't it, how styles
come back again? This won't need near so much
changing as I feared; but we'll slit the sleeves at the
bottom, perhaps, and put in full undersleeves of lace,
and fill the neck in the same way-that is, if the idea
pleases you, my dear. After all, it's your wedding
gown, not mine," she added with a laugh. "What do
you think?"
"It would be lovely, Mother; and the skirt is beau-
tiful as it is with those deep scallops above that underpleating; but as I said before, you can't get down to
Denver City; and I don't see you buying lace fit to
go with that taffeta at-at a grocery tent!"
"Don't worry about that." Persis Alden's sweet
face was bright with interest. "Do you remember
the scarves your Uncle Dan brought from Spain
when you were a wee mite of a girl? One for his
mother, and one for me, Mary; though we never
wore them, of course. Spanish lace was too dressy for
strawberry festivals or any other festivities in the vil-
lage. But I often took mine out to look at it, just
for pleasure, and wondered if when the time came,
it would make you a wedding veil. It will, I'm sure;
and, though it's almost a crime to cut into such beau-
tiful lace, we'll use your grandmother's to trim the
gown. It will please Dan to think he had a hand
in it."
Mary sank back against the pillows.
"You-you are a prestidigitator, Mother! Who else
would have brought such scarves into the wilderness?
Hand me the scissors and I'll rip the gathers of this
skirt, for I'm not so slim as you were at eighteen. I
didn't expect a wedding veil, let alone one so grand
as this. Do you think our neighbors will feel that
I'm putting on airs?"
"What if they do? A wedding day comes but once
in a lifetime, and"
"Not always," broke in Daniel Alden, who, return-
ing to the house for a hatchet, had appeared in the
doorway unnoticed. "Your own grandfather was mar-
ried twice, Persis, or you wouldn't be here; andwhat about Uncle Noah Smith at Oxford? He buried
four wives, and was courting a fifth when the Lord
took him."
"And high time!" retorted Persis. "I never could
abide your Uncle Noah, Dan. He was always offer-
ing his cheek for me to kiss, as if he were doing me
a favor. I could hardly bear to peck at it."
Daniel Alden grinned his amusement.
"No wonder! It was a very red cheek, I remem-
ber, and rarely shaved. I didn't care for Uncle Noah
myself, Persis, and always marveled that so many
women were willing to marry him. Is that your wed-
ding gown, sister? I never saw it."
Persis lifted the dress, holding its shining folds in
front of her.
"What things time does to us, Dan!" she said re-
gretfully. "I couldn't get this 'round me now if I
tried. My waist measured only twenty inches when
this dress was made; and even Mary can't wear it
without letting out the seams. Isn't it beautiful?
Aunt Sarah was set on my being married in brown
wool, because it was serviceable, and I do believe
Luther would have sided with her if he hadn't real-
ized how bad I felt. I'd set my heart on a real wed-
ding gown, you see, and was so bitterly disappointed
that I cried myself to sleep one night."
"Poor girl!" sympathized Daniel. "How did you
manage to prevail against Aunt Sarah in the end?
You couldn't dislike my uncle any more than I dis-
liked your aunt, Persis. If you'll excuse my saying
so, she was an abominable woman-though she neverpresented her cheek for me to kiss, thanks to my
lucky star!"
Persis laughed merrily.
"Aunt Sarah didn't believe in kissing. I never had
a good-night kiss, Dan, after Mother died, except
when I visited Julia Moore at Enfield, she who mar-
ried Hezekiah White, you know. Aunt Martha, her
mother, was a sweet woman, and gave me all the
affection I craved. It was she who decided about my
wedding dress, Mary. She was visiting at the Gilders'
for a few days, and walked in one morning with a
bolt of white taffeta under her arm, having heard
from Hannah Pease, our hired girl, of the battle that
was raging. She paid for the making, too, so Aunt
Sarah couldn't grumble about that; and she scared
Luther almost out of his wits by giving him a piece
of her mind for not standing up for me better-
though dear knows the poor boy did what he could.
He was always tongue-tied before Aunt Sarah."
"If I'd been in his shoes," exploded Daniel, “I'd
have been tempted to tweak Aunt Sarah's ugly nose
for her!"
"And a pretty scandal that would have made in
the village!" laughed Persis; while Mary changed the
subject, saying:
"Do you think I'll look silly in a wedding veil,
Uncle Danny?"
"I think you'll look like a frosted angel on a
Christmas card," was the prompt and gratifying re-
sponse. "But do you mean to say that your resource-
ful mother is prepared for even that emergency?""Thanks to you, she is," smiled Persis, and open-
ing a tissue-wrapped parcel, held aloft before his
puzzled eyes the shining lengths of creamy lace. "Do
you remember these scarves, Dan?"
The man's face lighted.
"Great Scott! Of course I do! And Mary's to use
one for her wedding veil?"
Persis nodded, happy in his obvious delight.
"It will be a veil fit for a queen, Dan; but if you
stay here talking any longer I'll never get started on
the gown to wear with it."
"She's right, Uncle Danny," agreed the bride-to-be.
"You're much too fascinating. Run along now, and
build me a house!"
Although the first plan had been for a simple fam-
ily wedding, it was soon apparent that no such pro-
ceeding would be tolerated by the kindly neighbors.
Let the excitement of a wedding slip through their
fingers? Never! In vain did Persis point out the lack
of a church to accommodate the witnesses. Why scorn
the hall over the Post Office? Hadn't the Methodists
been meeting there for months? If it would serve for
a church service, it would serve for a wedding. Too
bare and ugly? Nonsense! In the hills beyond Black
Hawk there were spruces and pine trees a-plenty.
That hall would not be bare when they got through
with it. It would be a bower! Leave it to them.
"They must have their way," said Persis when she
and Mary were alone. "After all, it will be an event
long remembered, and since you have a gown worthyof any sort of ceremony, we can hardly refuse the
kindly offers of these friends."
Thus it was settled, and no month ever slipped
by so fast. The weather-as if to make amends for
all the trouble it had wrought before-stayed almost
miraculously fair after that one October blizzard,
some days being even balmy, which was unusual
that time of year so high in the mountains. In an-
other week Mary was herself again, and torn between
the dual fascinations of assisting Persis with the wed-
ding gown, and watching the work on her new home
progress. There was no lack of willing helpers, so,
logs once hewn, the walls of that cabin went up like
magic. And a snug, warm place it would be, Daniel
assured them, beaming. No carelessly filled chinks to
