let in wintry winds-a good board floor-and not
only a cook stove for warmth, but the unheard-of
luxury of a fireplace in the bedroom!
"Never mind," replied Michael, when Luther
pointed out that since this dwelling was likely to be
temporary, so much labor was hardly worth while.
"I wouldn't feel at home without an open fire; and
nothing's too good for Mary, Mr. Alden."
To this the girl's father had to agree. Indeed,
Luther was inwardly gratified that his daughter's
cabin would be the most comfortable and complete
in the whole gulch. As for its furnishings, in an
astounding manner wedding presents began to ar-
rive. One after another those kindly frontier neigh-
bors climbed the rough path with gifts for Mary-
gifts which in most cases meant real sacrifice: a chairthat could be ill-spared a linen tablecloth-towels
with crocheted borders which had been laid away as
treasures a braided rug from Vermont-good china
dishes that a year before were laboriously packed
across the Kansas plains-two old brass candlesticks
from a New Hampshire farm house which brought a
cry of pleasure to Mary's lips-even an exquisite
Seth Thomas clock, which a gruff old miner had
carried from Connecticut, made its way into the girl's
astonished hands.
"No, no!" he exclaimed, when she protested that
the gift was far too much. "It was my mother's, Miss
Mary, and there's a look about you that reminds me
of her. She set great store by that clock; and when
the old home and all its furnishings were sold at
auction I couldn't bear to see some greedy stranger
snap it up for a song; though if the boys I came West
with had known what was in my pack they'd have
called me a fool. Take it, my dear. I'll be happy to
know it's in safe hands."
"It's hard to believe that all these things are mine,
Mother," declared Mary one afternoon when Persis
was putting the finishing touches to the white taffeta.
"How wonderful these people are! One would think
they had known me all my life like the folks at home.
How can I ever repay their kindness?"
"They'll feel repaid when they see you in this
gown, daughter, or I miss my guess. None of them
have laid eyes on anything so pretty since they left
the East; and I, myself, never saw a lovelier weddingdress. The lace is just creamy enough for the silk,
Mary; though if your grandmother can look down
from her heavenly mansion and has seen me cutting
into her precious scarf, she'll have a good scolding
ready for me when I meet her in Kingdom Come!"
The girl broke into a happy laugh.
"Oh, Mother, you're always so human! I can't im-
agine why you haven't shocked Father to death long
before this."
"Perhaps your father is more human, or more long-
suffering than you suspect, child," replied Persis,
smiling. "Turn a bit to the left, Mary, and let me
put a pin into this shirring. No one would dream
this was a made-over gown, my dear. I'm not ashamed
to have the whole town see it, which no doubt they
will; though I hadn't bargained on so many wedding
guests."
"Will everyone in the gulch expect to come,
Mother?"
Persis nodded, removed a pin from her mouth,
and answered: “How can we draw the line when the
men are helping Michael, and the women have given
you the best they had? Surely the least we can do in
return is to bid them welcome."
"But I shall be scared to death in front of all those
people!"
"Don't worry about that," soothed her mother.
"Most likely you'll see only Michael, and forget that
the rest of us exist. And that reminds me!" (Persis.
who had been kneeling on the floor to make sure that
every fold of the full skirt fell even, sat back sud-denly.) "What on earth can Michael wear for a wed-
ding suit? He can't stand up to be married in his
worn old trousers and flannel shirt!"
Faced with this appalling thought, Mary sank
limply onto the bed, at which her mother cried:
"Don't sit down in that dress, child! It's full of pins!
Here, I'll help you out of it; and now slip into your
gingham for someone may come. To go back to
Michael, I think we have all been stupid enough to
forget that, to some extent anyway, he must be
dressed to match his bride."
"Stupid does not half express it, Mother," returned
Mary, her voice tragic. "I've been so taken up with
my own finery that not once have I given a thought
to Michael's. Perhaps he can borrow something from
-from somebody; but such clothes wouldn't be apt
to fit, and Michael is fussy about things like that, I
fear."
"Who says Michael is fussy?" questioned the young
man himself, with a rap on the door. "May I come
in? I am here to ask the lady of my house if she wants
a shelf over the stove. Her father and uncle are argu-
ing about it now, one declaring that it's a necessity,
and the other that it's only a trap for dust; but both
agree to leave the decision to Mary. Give me a
kiss, mavourneen. It is three hours at least since the
last, and I'm almost ready to die."
"You will be completely dead when I tell you
what's on our minds, Michael,” she answered gravely.
"Here I'm to be dressed like a queen at our wed-
ding; and-and you've nothing to wear but roughold trousers and flannel shirt. I don't dare let you
go to Denver City to buy others, for what if another
blizzard should snow us in for the winter and we'd
have no wedding at all? Do you suppose you could
borrow something from that nice lawyer from Cin-
cinnati?"
"And he, three inches shorter than myself, lady?"
Michael laughed, despite the fact that his sweetheart
was near tears. "Do you want me to stand up before
the élite of Central City looking like a country
bumpkin? Come here, my dear, and listen to a story.
Once upon a time, in the merry month of May-no,
it was June, wasn't it? a weary traveler from the
British Isles was crossing the hot Kansas plains on
horseback, when, turning into a strip of woodland in
search of water, he discovered the fairest lady he had
ever beheld and"
"Oh, Michael!" broke in the girl with real dis-
tress, "how can you joke when this matter is so
serious?"
"But I'm not joking, mavourneen. Listen. Right
then the traveler felt a palpitation in his heart and
suspected that his days of freedom were over. In fact,
he hoped devoutly that this was the case, but dared
not confide this hope to the lady in question for fear
she was not yet ready for such a confidence; but in
a tactful manner he prodded her mother (who was,
in her own way, quite as adorable as the girl),
and-"
"Goodness gracious, Michael!" interrupted Persis
with a laugh, "this is no time to sit here paying com-pliments. If you've anything important to say, do say
it quickly, but if not"
Young Michael held up a warning hand.
"Curb your impatience, dear lady. I was merely
trying to explain that during a confidential moment
this lovely creature's mother disclosed the fact that,
thinking her daughter might find a use for it, she
had tucked her own wedding gown into the cowhide
trunk. Yes, she told him that, Mary-my-dear, though
almost instantly she regretted this indiscretion and
swore him to secrecy, fearing that if her sensible
daughter should learn the truth she would be ridi-
culed for her pains."
"But what, please, has this long yarn to do with
the subject?"
"Everything, my darling," responded Michael, un-
perturbed by the girl's obvious impatience, "but if
you prefer I'll skip the fine touches and get to the
point. Knowing, you see, that the lady of his heart
must inevitably marry someone, and being deter-
mined on the occasion of her wedding to play the
part of bridegroom himself, this intrepid Britisher
managed to steal a few hours during a brief stop in
Denver City, when, with the aid of a tailor from
Boston who was cooling his heels after an unlucky
search for gold, he procured raiment eminently
suitable for the event. Thus"
"Michael Warwick!" cried Mary, putting both
hands on his shoulders in order to shake him, "if
this is not true may-may you boil in oil!"
"My soul!" exclaimed Michael, "what a horriblewish from one's future wife! But on my word of
honor, young lady, this entire story in which you
have shown so little interest, is true as truth. What,
may I ask, did you think was in that package I stowed
away so carefully in the covered wagon?"
"I never gave it a thought. And if you hadn't been
so so disgracefully close-mouthed you would have
saved us a lot of worry. Don't you ever do such a
thing again."
"No, ma'am,” replied Michael meekly. "I promise
that after Thanksgiving Day all my secrets shall be
yours. I'm hungry, mother-in-law. May I have a slice
of bread and butter to munch on the way back? And
Mary hasn't decided about the shelf."
"Help yourself to a couple of doughnuts," an-
swered Persis as she resumed her sewing. "As for the
shelf, I'll wager a third doughnut that it was Luther
who wanted it, and Dan who voted the other way.
I dare say that man of mine couldn't imagine a
kitchen stove without a shelf behind it, like the one
at home that has bothered me for twenty years. Your
uncle is right, Mary. It's too dusty a place for such
a thing. Have your shelf between the two windows
instead. And now be off, Michael. There's much
to do."C
CHAPTER XXXII
ENTRAL CITY-December 21st, 1860," wrote
Mary Warwick. "It is long since I have opened
this book, my time being occupied with strictly per-
sonal affairs; but as Father wisely points out, since
ours was a frontier wedding, and a somewhat unusual
one at that, an account of it should be set down
herein, and I have asked my Uncle Daniel to record
the event for two reasons-first because he has a more
colorful pen than I can ever hope to possess, and sec-
ondly because we are snowbound, and the poor man
is so restless that such an occupation will be a god-
send to him.
"For my own part I will say that Thanksgiving
Day though cold, dawned clear as crystal, which fact
even Father regarded as a happy omen. Also I must
acknowledge the wonderful kindness of our good
neighbors, who insisted on taking the matter out of
our hands and providing not only the decorations
(which were truly beautiful) but the entire wedding
feast! It was most extraordinary. No sooner had news
of our approaching marriage gone abroad, than a
baker from the city of St. Louis begged permission
to donate the wedding cake. He little knew that with-
out his offer there would, most likely, have been no
cake at all, for I should not have allowed my parentsto spend money for a mere luxury just now. And
such a cake as it was! I have since wondered if it cost
the generous man all the gold dust he has been able
to accumulate (which I've been told was little
enough), for it was a real masterpiece, baked in an
enormous dish which had been previously used for
panning gold, white as mountain snow and decorated
with rosebuds of white icing, with our initials en-
twined in the center of an icing heart. It did seem
a pity to cut into the lovely creation; but at the end
of the supper not so much as a scrap was left, Zeb-
ulon Pike and two of his friends being allowed, as
a special treat, to lap the crumbs!
"Nor was this all. Our grocer provided coffee
brewed by his wife; and with the assistance of
another neighbor, Polly Otis made dozens upon doz-
ens of fluffy biscuits; while the lady who, during
my recent illness, was so kind as to bring me a swans-
down-trimmed bed jacket, arose early to bake three
huge meat pies, made from fresh game provided by
a nameless gentleman! Indeed, from almost every
house or cabin in the gulch came some donation,
making a spread fit for the wedding of a princess.
How can we ever hope to repay such kindness?
"Just one word more (lest a mere man fail to do
justice to the subject), for I wish to state that the
appearance of the bridegroom took away my breath,
and for a few moments I wondered if I were marry-
ing a stranger. Having never before seen my sweet-
heart save in raiment suitable for the trail, I did
not realize how handsome a man he was. In fact,wearing black broadcloth, white shirt, and high
choker, Michael looked so different (even from the
young men at home when in their Sunday clothes)
that a terrible embarrassment swept over me until,
sensing my mood and much amused by it, he said:
'Can it be possible that this superbly attired lady is
the girl who promised to cast her lot with a poor
immigrant? Fine feathers make fine birds indeed!
He feels most uncomfortably in awe of her.' And
thereupon the scamp proceeded to behave in a
manner which showed anything but awe, and I knew
that after all, this was no stranger, but my own dear
Michael.
"And now, since Uncle Dan is fairly pacing the
floor in eagerness to begin his part of the story, I
will relinquish my pen to his more gifted hands."
"More gifted!" exclaimed Dan Alden, who was
perusing the last page over Mary's shoulder. "Why,
it reads like a book! In spots your language is almost
flowery. I didn't know you had it in you!"
"It is a gift inherited from my vagabond uncle,"
laughed the girl, rising. "From the time I was big
enough to spell out the words I have treasured your
letters, Uncle Danny, and read them over and over
till they dropped apart. Now take care to make your
writing as cramped as possible, for though this wed-
ding is important to our special family it mustn't
be allowed to fill too many pages. Will it bother you
if I get dinner while you scribble?"
"I shall be lost to the world," Daniel assured her
as he took the pen; and very nearly was, thoughfrom time to time he would raise his head to ask:
"Say, does beginning have two n's in the middle,
Mary? I haven't seen a spelling book since I was
twelve years old, you know." Or, after a silence:
"What's the name of that handsome wrap your
mother wore over her shoulders?-An Alsatian?
Glory be to St. Peter, what a ridiculous thing to call
a cape!"
Mary smiled.
"Don't you dare take too much space describing
clothes, Uncle Dan," she warned him.
"Who's writing the account of this wedding in
high society?" he countered. "Tell me, was it a velvet
gown worn by the lady-well, the one whose very
name brings a blush to your chaste father's cheek, my
dear?"
His niece sank into a chair and laughed.
"How can you be so so frivolous, Uncle Dan?
Yes, that gown was velvet-a beautiful gown too, in
my opinion. And did you know that Polly Otis's
brown taffeta was her wedding dress? It had six
flounces on the skirt, and the collar was real lace!"
"Was it indeed!" returned Daniel properly awed.
Then, sniffing the air: "Dinner smells good, Mary.
Am I invited?"
"Not only invited, but compelled to stay and keep
me company. Michael is helping Father build a cup-
board in Mother's bedroom, and if the work's not
finished he will have dinner with them, though it
seems as if he'd been gone a lifetime already."
"Dear me!" responded Daniel, his eyes twinkling,"what curious things this marriage business does
to us."
"You're right," confessed Mary. "Even this soon I
can't imagine myself married to anybody else."
Her uncle laughed.
"Thank me, then, for urging your father to join
the gold seekers. If you'd stayed on the farm, Mary,
no doubt by now you'd be the bride of Jim Emer-
son, or Tom Pease, or maybe that knock-kneed Liver-
more boy who's been casting sheep's eyes at you
ever since you put up your hair."
"Stop, Uncle Danny!" begged his niece, covering
her ears. "You make cold shivers run up my spine.
And besides, you're just wasting good time chatter-
ing nonsense, for I wouldn't have married Sam Liver-
more if he'd been the last man left in the world. Now
get back to your story, and don't forget to say how
handsome my parents looked that evening. It was a
grand idea of Mother's to fashion a collar for herself
out of the remains of your Spanish lace, wasn't it?
And you might mention that the bride's uncle from
foreign parts, who stood up with the groom, was
obliged to borrow suitable garments from another
gold seeker, who (poor fellow!) appeared himself in
buckskin trousers and flannel shirt."
"The man had plenty of company, my dear,"
Daniel reminded her with a grin, "though truth to
tell, I did not suppose there were so many white col-
lars in the gulch as graced the occasion: not to men-
tion that gaudy miner from Black Hawk with his
checkered coat and red tie topping buckskin pants!"Mary laughed softly.
"It was his wife," she said, "in the pink tarlatan
and silver stars. Whatever could have made her bring
such a gown into the wilderness? Be sure and tell
how beautifully our Cincinnati lawyer played the
wedding march on his fiddle, Uncle Dan; and what
a wonderful Virginia reel we had at the end. Would
you like a bread pudding for dinner? I've a few old
crusts in the bread box; and made with molasses like
Grandma Alden's it won't need an egg, though I
can't get used to thinking of eggs as a luxury."
"There was a time, when we were shipwrecked off
the coast of Portugal, that I'd have sold my soul for
an egg," said Daniel, his eyes dreamy. "It had stormed
for days, and"
"Excuse me for interrupting you," broke in Mary
sternly, "but once you get reminiscing, Uncle Dan,
the account of my wedding will be delayed, and I
can't wait to read it. Besides, I know that story about
the coast of Portugal by heart."
Dan Alden looked at her, his eyes reproachful.
"So I'm getting to be a doddering old pest who re-
peats himself endlessly and bores folks to death with
tales of his wanderings?"
Mary came close to tip back his head for a kiss.
"You couldn't bore anyone if you tried, darling;
and I have a notion you're aware of the fact and
just bidding for compliments. Did anyone ever tell
you how handsome you are, Uncle Danny?"
"Yes," replied Daniel, entirely unabashed at the
question. "There was a slip of a girl in Liverpool(Irish, she was, with black-lashed eyes as blue as
sapphires) who seemed set on my finding it out.
Luckily for young Dan Alden his ship was sailing at
noon, my dear; though at the time it seemed a pity
to part with a lady who admired him so much and
was not ashamed to say so! Now take yourself and
your flattery away, child, and I'll get at this job."
Years afterward, when the Civil War had reaped
its harvest, Mary Warwick often looked back upon
that day-the cosy meal with her Uncle Daniel-
his amusing questions as he penned the story of her
wedding-the sense of comradeship and affection
that pervaded the small room-all these good
memories were to come back for special treasuring.
At three o'clock, with a stamping of feet to shake
off snow, and yelps of welcome from Zebulon Pike,
Michael came in upon them.
"I feel as if I'd been gone a year," he confessed, one
arm encircling his bride, the other about the limp-
ing collie. "Is your story finished, Uncle Dan? My
in-laws are so crazy to see it that they've invited
themselves to supper, though your mother insists on
bringing most of the food, Mary, and says you're to
prepare only bread and tea. We'll build a fire on
the hearth and sit around it while you read us what
you have written, Uncle Dan. I must see if there are
plenty of dry logs in the shed."
So, a few hours later, that small company of
pioneers gathered about a crackling hearthfire and
listened to Daniel Alden's account of his niece's wed-
ding, while snow blew softly against the window-panes, and sifted through the branches of a giant
pine tree beside the door.
Strange, mused Michael Warwick, his eyes caress-
ing the dark head that rested against his knee, strange
and wonderful, too, that he, who felt so homeless on
leaving England, should have crossed an ocean and
a prairie to discover this!
Strange, Daniel Alden was thinking as he took his
place, that after years of footloose wanderings he
should find himself snowbound high in the Rocky
Mountains, yet, somehow, not minding very much!
And Persis, exchanging a smile with her new son-
in-law, thought happily that it was a kind Providence
indeed which caused this young Englishman, out of
all the gold seekers bound for Pike's Peak that sum-
mer, to cross their path.
But in spirit, Luther Alden was far away. Led by
those dancing flames he was back once more in his
own loved Massachusetts back in the big east room
with its carved woodwork and shallow fireplace-a
flatiron overturned upon his knees, a hammer in
his hand, a bag of hickory nuts ready to be cracked,
a yellow mixing bowl that had been his mother's.
And through all these musings a quaint Seth
Thomas clock on the mantel above, ticked on as
reliably as if it had not traveled from far-off Connecti-
cut in a miner's pack; while flanking it on either
side, tapers in tall brass candlesticks from a New
Hampshire farmhouse, shed their soft luster on the
scene below.Ο
CHAPTER XXXIII
Na smiling day late in January, Luther Alden
set out on a solitary expedition. His brother,
accompanied by Michael Warwick and Tom Otis,
had been gone since early morning in search of game;
and Persis was spending the afternoon with Mary,
helping the girl hem muslin curtains for her kitchen
windows. So, leaving a hastily scrawled note to say he
would return by supper time, Luther armed himself
with belt ax and skinning knife, and struck off into
the wilderness.
It was one of those rare winter days that, even
high in the mountains, bring a promise of spring.
Perhaps, mused the New Englander, it was this feel-
ing in the air which accounted for the sense of
nostalgia which had grown upon him steadily since
dawn. All morning he had busied himself with one
small task after another, hoping to stifle the un-
welcome restlessness that possessed him; but no wind
stirred, and the warm sun on his weather-beaten face
only accentuated his longing to gaze once more on
the home acres.
Possibly, the man argued, if he climbed high
enough for physical fatigue, this mood would leave
him; yet by now Luther was well aware that it would
return. During the past weeks there had been timeswhen his homesickness was so intense as to be actual
pain, for, distance lending its strange enchantment,
a kindly veil seemed drawn over the discomforts of
a New England winter on the farm: the deep snows
shutting them in for weeks-the sudden thaws which
transform the roads into veritable rivers of slush and
mud-the daily "chores" begun and ended by dim
lantern light-the dark mornings early twilights-
all these were, if not actually forgotten, so softened
by memory and space that they seemed as naught.
Sometimes, regarding her covertly, Luther mar-
veled that his wife felt none of this keen longing for
familiar scenes. The thought hurt him, as if he had
detected in her some disloyalty. Mary's defection was
easier to understand, for had not the girl found love
and a home of her own here in this frontier country?
But if the memory of old times and life-long friends
tugged at Persis Alden's heartstrings, she made no
sign-appeared, in fact, quite satisfied with this rough
life. Their migration across the plains which to her
husband savored of an adventure-a long-due vaca-
tion from which they would return, was, apparently,
regarded by Persis as something permanent. Why
else, he pondered, did she bring with her such
treasures as the Alden cream pitcher, and her grand-
mother's teaspoons, which would have been far safer
back at the farm?
And suppose she prevailed upon him to stay-to
make a home in this bleak place? Could he do it and
be happy, even for Persis? Luther considered this and
came to no conclusion. But what if by some miraclehe discovered gold? the man asked himself. A com-
fortable living. Perhaps wealth. Was it a dream of
riches which had lured him from his native hills?
No, not that, his heart answered. The dream was
there, of course; but it was a longing for change-
adventure-and the plea of his daughter Mary that
decided the momentous step. Nor did he regret it,
Luther admitted honestly. Come what would, that
long, long trek across the Kansas prairies had taught
him many things: the vastness of his own country-
its possibilities and how small a part of it his own
New England really was.
As for the gold, why dream of it longer? Even
Daniel, patient for an amazingly long time, had still
to make a fortune; and judging from talk in the
gulch any precious metal still left in these rugged
mountains was far too hard to dislodge with pick and
shovel. No, he would put away the hope of riches,
mused Luther sensibly; and if when spring came he
could accumulate enough to take his family home
again, they would be fortunate. But in any case, three
long months of inactivity lay ahead.
The man paused, partly to catch his breath after
climbing too rapidly, partly because he had reached
the entrance to a small ravine which he could not
recall having explored before. It was a sheltered place,
the snow melted entirely in spots where the sun
penetrated; and, still moving aimlessly, Luther pro-
ceeded up this gulch for a half hour until, suddenly
realizing his fatigue, he dropped down on a sun-
warmed rock for a short restBack at his daughter's snug cabin at "the dig-
gings," Luther himself was the subject of conversa-
tion.
"He's restless," said Persis when Mary remarked
that her father had seemed a bit low-spirited of late.
"The truth is, my dear, he has worked so constantly
for years that the poor man doesn't know how to
enjoy leisure. He has no hobbies, and once the daily
chores are over is at a loss for what to do with his
spare time. If we had some books they'd be a god-
send, for your father likes nothing better than to
read; but he has already been twice through a copy
of 'David Copperfield' loaned him by that Cincin-
nati lawyer, and the only book we brought ourselves
is the Bible, which-thanks to your grandmother!-
he knows by heart."
"I wish he had gone hunting with the other men
this morning," observed Mary, measuring a hem with
a bit of cardboard.
"I urged him to, but since your experience with
that Indian he is afraid to leave us alone for a whole
day. When I started up here after lunch he said he
might stroll down the gulch for a chat with Bill
Douglas, that shoemaker who comes from Boston.
Dear me! If your father's unable to do any prospect-
ing for three months, Mary, he'll be in a state and so
will I! I sometimes fear the poor dear's homesick."
"Have you never felt homesick yourself, Mother?"
questioned the girl.
For a moment Persis gazed out of the small window,
down that lonely valley where smoke issuing fromsnow-hidden chimneys was the only sign of human
habitation. Then she replied: "I won't say there
haven't been times when I longed for a chat with
some of the old friends, and more times when I've
worried for fear we might know poverty here worse
than any which faced us on the farm; but why should
I be homesick when all my nearest and dearest are
with me? Then, too, I believe that at heart I'm an
adventurer! Looking back, it seems that almost every
mile of our long journey was what the children would
call fun."
Mary laughed softly.
"You're a wonder, Mother! Sometimes I think you
should have married Uncle Daniel."
There followed a silence broken only by the cheer-
ful singing of the kettle on the stove. Then Persis
answered, a faint flush on her cheek: "What an idea!
I am two years older than Daniel Alden."
"Thirteen months, Mother, corrected Mary, "and
any one would take you for five years younger.
Though he's so youthful at heart, so straight, and
so handsome, Uncle Dan has always looked more than
his years. Perhaps it's because he has taken care of
himself since he was a small boy."
"And of others too." Persis spoke gently. "Dan
never forgot his mother or or any of us, Mary. All
these years he has shared his earnings generously,
thinking, perhaps, that your dear father had all the
burdens, and he all the fun. Since his first visit home
when he was only a lad of seventeen, Dan's never
failed us.""It's strange that two brothers should be so dif-
ferent, isn't it?"
Persis paused in the threading of a needle to
reply: "I'm not sure they're so different. Sometimes
their eyes are so much alike that it startles me. I've
often wondered how they would have turned out if
they'd changed places, early in life, of course. Surely
your father enjoyed every step of our migration,
Mary; and if Dan hadn't run away"
"But he couldn't have helped it!" broke in the girl
eagerly. "You're trying to imagine the impossible!"
"Perhaps I am, daughter. Still, if Dan had fallen
in love with some nice girl in the village she would
have been an anchor."
"And would you want my darling uncle anchored?"
retorted Mary, such distress in her voice at the very
idea, that Persis laughed aloud.
"It would be a hard thing to contemplate, I'll
admit, child. It's a marvel, isn't it, that he has been
contented here for so long. I dread the day when the
wanderlust gets him again."
Mary's eyes widened.
"You think that he'd desert us in this strange,
wild place, Mother, after urging Father to leave New
England?"
"I think your uncle's an old dog to learn new
tricks, Mary. Some day the yearning to be off will
overpower him and sweep him away, just as it always
has. Perhaps it's as well that Dan never fell in love.
He's not the sort that makes a comfortable husband."
"Stuff and nonsense!" bristled the bride of eightweeks. "Uncle Dan would succeed at anything he at-
tempted, even matrimony. Though I dare say," she
added with a smile, “that he'd expect his wife to tag
along on his travels."
"Which would be pleasant indeed if they had a
dozen children," returned Persis dryly.
"Just the same, he would have made a darling
father," said Mary, her eyes dreamy. "Still, no more
darling than my own. I am glad, after all, that you
didn't marry him, Mother."
"Goodness gracious!" exclaimed Persis, "what
makes you harp on that idea? Dan Alden never saw
me till I was his sister-that is, not to notice me, I
mean. I was a plain, long-legged thirteen when he
ran away, Mary; and I recall his throwing a 'spit ball'
at me one day in school."
The girl broke into a merry laugh.
"We must remind him of that! Here, Mother, this
pair of curtains is finished. Let's run it onto a
string and tack it in place as a surprise for Michael.
It's time they were home again, I think."
"And here they are!" Persis was at the window.
"At least, there goes Tom with a fine fat sheep as his
share of the spoils. If the others have done as well
we'll have some fresh meat for supper, Mary. Hello,
Michael! Did you have good luck?"
The two men had entered by way of the shed door;
and Michael, intent on the reunion with his bride,
made no reply. It was Daniel Alden who answered:
"We sha'n't starve for a while yet, Persis. It was a
great day and I wish Luther had been along. Come,you two love-birds? Break away. I want a kiss from
the bride myself."
"And I," laughed Michael, "would appreciate a
cup of tea. Is the kettle hot, Mary? Let's”
He turned, for the front door had burst open as
by a gust of wind and Luther Alden was among
them, his eyes wild, his hands shaking as he flung
down upon the table what appeared at first glance to
be merely a quantity of rocks and shale.
"Look!" he cried out, so breathless that the words
were wrenched from him with an effort. "Look,
Persis! Look, Mary! It's gold! Do you understand?
Gold! Gold!"H
CHAPTER XXXIV
OW strange it is," said Daniel Alden, when the
pandemonium which followed his brother's
entrance had at last subsided, "that steady old Lute
should be the one to make a fortune here in the
mountains, while young bucks like Michael and my-
self, who've been scratching gravel frantically all fall,
should have nothing to show for our labor!"
"Hush!" reproved Persis. "We mustn't set our
hearts on a fortune, Daniel, or anything like it. Think
how your own claim petered away; and how many
of our neighbors hoped for wealth out of what ended
in a few hundred dollars at best. Besides, it will be
months before we'll know the truth; and we mustn't
let the expectation of riches turn our heads in the
meantime."
"Oh, you cautious, cold-blooded New Englander!"
jeered her brother-in-law. "Why, half the fun of a
lucky find, Persis-my-dear, is the excitement that
follows. And what's the harm in a few day dreams?
If I'm any judge, there must be ten dollars' worth of
gold in what Lute brought home in his pockets,
perhaps more. We'll call our mine the Persis-Mary,
which will bring luck if anything can. I hope to
God you marked the spot carefully, brother."
"Don't worry!" Luther's eyes, thought his wife, hadnever been so bright with excitement. "I had dug
quite a hole with my ax, but I filled it all in and built
a fire on top so there'd be only ashes to show. Then
I marked a half dozen nearby pines, and cut the
top off a small one that stood in a direct line from
the spot. I could find the place with my eyes shut,
Dan; but just the same, if the weather holds fair
we'd better tramp up there to-morrow. It would be
safer for the three of us to know where it is in case
something should happen to me before spring."
"Mercy!" cried Persis. "Don't speak such forebod-
ing words, Luther Alden. They give me the shivers.
You say there's ten dollars here, Dan? That will go
a long way. It may last us all winter."
Dan Alden laughed.
"You'll soon be throwing ten dollars to beggars at
the door, lady," he told her with a grin. "I can see
you and Mary trailing your silken skirts down Blake
Street in Denver City, and turning up your noses at
the shabby old rover who's your poor relation."
"And only a few moments ago he was describing
himself as a young buck!" laughed Mary. "But I
wonder if I'll ever swish 'round in silk skirts, Uncle
Danny. It would be delightful."
"If I can make enough to take us safely home
again, and secure a comfortable old age for Persis,
I'll ask no more," said Luther, suddenly grave. "But
why talk as if the wealth, if it proves to be wealth, is
all mine, Dan? Surely we are all partners in this
undertaking, and anything that comes of it I'd want
to share equally with you and Michael.""Well, we won't quarrel about that," returned
Daniel, throwing his brother a quick smile. "It's all
in the family, anyway. My soul! I've a thirst for some-
thing stronger than mountain water! Why were you
such a dunce as to sign the pledge at ten years old,
Lute? This is a day to celebrate, or I don't know one;
and there sits Michael Warwick sipping his tea like
an old woman. Tea a time like this!"
Michael threw back his head and laughed.
"I know how you feel, Uncle Dan; but tea is said
to be soothing to the nerves, so perhaps if you fol-
low my example that ungodly thirst will depart.
After all, it's your brother who discovered the gold,
and it would be a shame to celebrate in a way he
would not approve, and couldn't join."
"You're a dear boy, Michael," said Persis grate-
fully, while Mary reached out to give her husband's
hand a squeeze. "As for this wild brother of ours,"
the older woman continued, "I've a bottle of rasp-
berry shrub, a parting gift from Miss Mattie Whee-
lock who taught Dan's class in Sabbath school years
ago. I've saved it for an occasion, and this is as good
a one as any, I suppose. Perhaps it's a bit fermented
by now, Dan," she added, her eyes lighting with
amusement as they met his.
"I'll hope for the best, sister," he told her, laugh-
ing.
No three men ever watched the heavens for a
promise of good weather more anxiously than did
those three before they went to bed that night. Nextmorning dawned cloudy, but mere clouds could not
deter them from making an early start into the moun-
tains; while Mary came down to spend the day with
her mother, Michael refusing to let her stay alone
at the cabin.
"Your father twisted and turned all night," said
Persis as they washed the breakfast dishes; "but
Daniel declared that he slept like a top."
"Uncle Dan is used to excitement, I suppose,”
responded Mary, turning to hang a willow-ware tea-
cup on a hook. "As for Michael, he didn't close his
eyes till long past midnight. Oh, Mother, won't it
be wonderful if if we strike it rich!"
Persis was silent for a moment; then said: "I sup-
pose it will be; but such a thing is beyond my wildest
expectations. As your father says, if we can provide
for a comfortable old age, it will be enough; though
I'd like some over to do for others. I've been think-
ing about it half the night, and spending my money
like a drunken sailor."
"So have I!" confessed Mary. "Uncle Dan said
there was no harm in day dreams, so I built a few
castles in Spain as I lay awake. Father little knows
what hunks of his good gold I gave away!"
Her mother smiled.
"Who profited by this sudden burst of generosity?"
she questioned.
"Almost everyone in the village at home, I must
confess. First thing I did was to send a check to
Parson Gilder, so he need never worry about the
future, though it's not likely he does, for he's alwayssaying that 'God will provide for His own.' Still, it's
well to be on the safe side, isn't it? So I sent him the
check! Then I bought a couple of fine, strong work
horses for Cousin Hezekiah; and a piano for Cousin
Julia, which she wants above anything else; and
made a trip to Worcester to get a wheel chair for
Granny Phillips, as well as a pony for Lewis Day,
a gift from Zebby, of course."
"Did you forget Charlie Emerson, whose golden
bracket saved your life?" asked Persis smiling, but
seriously.
"Goodness, no! I gave him the most beautiful set
of tools to be found in Boston. He loves to tinker
about his little shop, Mother, and maybe I'll add a
gallon of gilt paint, just as a matter of sentiment,
you know!"
"You did pretty well," laughed Persis, "but I'm
glad you told me your plans or Julia White would
have received two pianos, and no place to put them.
I never thought of horses for Hezekiah, but presented
him with a Goddard buggy, which has long been his
heart's desire."
"What else did you do?" questioned Mary, who
was hugely enjoying this distribution of imaginary
wealth.
"Much that you did, my dear; and I gave Mattie
Wheelock a gray silk dress. All her life the dear
woman has wanted a gray silk. She told me so when
your uncle sent me a length of such silk from New
York, before starting on one of his journeys. It was
a fine piece of grosgrain, and was turned twice be-fore it gave out. I had it made up for Gussie Whee-
lock's wedding in the Congregational Church."
"I dimly remember that gown, Mother. Wasn't
there a touch of scarlet about it?"
Persis nodded.
"A scarlet cord, which Dan sent with the silk for
a trimming. But your grandmother thought it too
gay for a married woman, and when the dress was
made over I ripped it off and used black gimp in-
stead."
"Oh, Mother! How could you be so meek? I'm
sure the scarlet was more becoming."
"So it was," agreed Persis, with a sigh of reminis-
cent regret, "but your grandmother had a way of
making me feel uncomfortable if I stood out against
her, even when your father took my side. Possibly
it was weakness, my dear; but I dislike family dis-
cord, and had my share of it while living with Aunt
Sarah."
"Perhaps life is simpler without a mother-in-law,"
responded Mary with raised eyebrows.
"Now that I'm one myself I must keep that in
mind," laughed her mother. "I shouldn't want
Michael to feel as irritated at me as I often felt at
Mother Alden, good woman though she undoubtedly
was."
"No danger! I sometimes think he's as much in
love with you as with me, Mother! There! The dishes
are done. Let's go down and watch Polly bathe the
baby. I promised to come some morning, and we'll
never have a better chance."Before nightfall the three men were home, more
gold in their pockets, and fresh enthusiasm in their
hearts. Filled with this hope the winter slipped
rapidly away, the one excitement being a visit from
a band of friendly Indians. Not until later did the
dwellers in that lonely gulch discover that in Febru-
ary President Buchanan had signed the Colorado
Bill after it had passed both houses of Congress.
Thus Jefferson Territory gave way to Colorado, a
name suggested by William Gilpin, an army officer
and Indian fighter, because the Colorado River was
a great feature of this part of the country; but
singularly fitting, too, for translated from the
Spanish, Colorado means "colored red," and the new
territory was a land of red soil and red rocks.
Now, planning the work ahead, Luther's nostal-
gia was silenced for a time; and as if for his especial
benefit, spring came early that year. The gulch, which
Michael declared had been "hibernating" for months,
awoke one bright March morning and resumed the
search for gold which that memorable October bliz-
zard had interrupted. Fortunately, Luther's find was
not too far off for the men to return each night; but
they were often away before dawn, taking turns at
riding Shamrock, for Daniel had long since sold the
lazy Moses to a prospector who planned a trip to
California Gulch.
"Every day," remarked Daniel Alden when the
work had been progressing for some weeks, "I expect
to see the vein run into solid rock, or pinch out en-
tirely; but so far, so good, Persis. We got all offorty dollars to-day, which makes let's see-nine
hundred and sixty-two since we started digging. Not
so bad when you think how long Luther would grub
for half that sum back on the farm."
"It sounds like a fairy tale to me," answered Persis
as her brother-in-law dropped wearily onto a kitchen
chair. The two were alone, Luther having stopped
for a word with Mary on his way down. "Even should
your fears be realized, Dan, it is almost too good to
be true. What's nine hundred and sixty-two divided
by three? In any case, it will take us back home the
way we came."
"And when do you start?" questioned Daniel, his
eyes twinkling.
"Perhaps not at all," replied Persis, somewhat sur-
prisingly. "Much as I love my native state, Dan, and
sad as I'd be if I thought we were never to see the
dear old friends again, this new country has done
something strange to me, for I've a longing to have
a hand in settling it-in making it more livable for
those who are coming after. Do you see what I
mean?"
Dan Alden nodded.
"It's the pioneer spirit, which I always said was in
you, Persis. Surely it's fortunate for the progress of
the world that some of us possess it, though I realize
that Luther can't change his nature, and will never
love this new home as he does the old. But even for
his sake, don't be in a hurry about returning yet a
while, my dear. If this claim we're working peters
out, there are more to discover; and I for one sha'n'tturn my back on the Rocky Mountains while there's
hope of becoming a millionaire, for when I cross
Jordan I must leave something behind for you folks
to remember me by!"
Persis touched his shoulder with a caressing hand.
"It won't take a million dollars to do that, Dan.
I wonder have you any idea what a bright spot you've
been in our lives all these years and in mine
especially."
"Why yours, may I ask?" said Daniel, his eyes
lifting suddenly at this admission.
Persis had gone to a window to peer up the gulch.
She answered, not turning: "Have you ever thought,
Dan, that life there on the farm wasn't so easy for a
girl reared in a village? And I was so young when I
married!"
"Young!" echoed Dan Alden. "You were an infant!
You looked fourteen at the most on my first visit
home. I remember wondering however you came to
care for a sober old chap like my brother Lutherl
That's the truth, Persis, though it may sound like
heresy to you now. As for life on that farm, it's a
wonder you stood it so long without complaining. In
two years it would have killed me dead. Don't think
for a moment that I didn't see how Mother-well,
how she never made things easier for you, my dear."
"She meant well," defended the woman, turning to
stir something on the stove. "It was her home before
it was mine, Dan, and she wanted things done her
way, I suppose. I've never admitted any of this to
Luther, for he wouldn't have understood; but you,being away so much, could get a different view of the
situation."
"Weren't there times, sister, when you'd have
given your soul to cut and run?"
Persis laughed lightly.
"Once, when Luther made me leave early after a
church supper so he could get back to read the Bible
to your mother before she slept, I-well-I packed a
bag! Don't ever breathe it, Dan Alden. It was the
nearest I ever came to a sin. But that night was the
first time I'd got down to the village for days, and I
was starving for a little fun, I dare say. And to be
dragged off when others were well, I'm not en-
tirely without temper, as perhaps you know. So I
packed my bag and was all set to start out come
morning; and then ..."
"Well?" prodded Daniel, deeply absorbed in this
revelation.
"Nothing," said Persis. "I was in bed pretending
to be asleep when Luther came in. He undressed
quietly, and leaned down to kiss me good-night, but
I never stirred. Maybe he knew I was shamming for
after a while he said out of nothing: 'I shouldn't have
made you come away early, Persis, but I didn't think
about it in time. Would you like to go over to En-
field to-morrow and make a visit to Aunt Martha?
The change would do you good.'
A sudden grin lighted Dan Alden's handsome
countenance.
"I didn't know my big brother possessed so much
tact," he observed dryly."Well, my bag was all packed," Persis reminded
him, "and perhaps he'd seen it standing behind the
door! At any rate, I had a lovely visit with Aunt
Martha, and came home a day early because I was so
crazy to see my husband! It was the first separation
we'd ever had."
"You're a good girl, Persis," said Daniel gently,
after a silence.
"Thanks, Dan; but for the most part I've been a
happy one, remember. And your visits were the
high spots in my life-in all our lives, I am sure-
something to look forward to when life got too
drab. We were always so sad to have you go, Dan,
though we knew, of course, that some day you'd
come back."
"As if I could have stayed away!" murmured
Daniel Alden. Then suddenly: "Hark! Is that some-
one running?”
And the next moment young Tom Otis burst in
upon them, too excited to knock as he cried out
breathlessly: "News! Just through from Denver City.
There's war! Do you hear? Fort Sumter was fired on
a week ago, and President Lincoln has declared war!"CHAPTER XXXV
AR! Even in those high mountain reaches far
W from the field of battle, a sense of combat
filled the air when the news came.
"Thank God, Michael is an Englishman!" thought
Mary Warwick, and wondered, even as she hugged
this sense of safe security, if she were at heart a
coward.
"Thank God, Luther is past fifty!" thought Persis.
"They won't want men of his age-yet!"-and did
not worry as to whether she was cowardly or not!
But both women kept a wary eye on Daniel Alden.
War! It had lured others in days gone by. Would it
lure him, their dear adventurer?
Neither could face the thought; nor did they
speak it to each other. As for the object of their
anxiety, he kept steadily at work-too steadily, per-
haps, for sometimes it seemed to Persis that Daniel
was working against time-working a bit feverishly,
as if to accomplish something in a hurry. Voluble as
a rule, he fell silent in the evenings now, often
enough letting his pipe go out while his eyes rested on
Persis, busy at setting neat patches in worn garments,
or knitting socks. Or the man would become uneasy,
moving about to fuss at this and that until his sister-
in-law inquired with gentle irony: "Haven't youhad exercise enough for one day, Dan?"-at which
he would slump into a chair again, smiling sheep-
ishly like a small boy caught in some misdemeanor.
Once, when thinking to calm him Persis invented
an imaginary errand at Mary's cabin, Luther glanced
up from his third perusal of "David Copperfield" to
say dryly: "Thanks, my dear! It's good to get that
caged beast out of the way for a half hour. I can't
attend to my book with such a prowling going on
behind me. What ails the man?"
"War," answered his wife, much as she disliked to
voice the thought. "It's in the air, Luther, and makes
him uneasy, I suppose. There's to be a Union mass
meeting at Denver City in a day or two, and it may
be that Dan wants to go down."
"It would take more than a mass meeting to drag
me down these mountains if I had to come back
again," said Luther, returning to his volume of
Dickens. But in a moment he closed it, to ask
abruptly: "Can it be possible that Daniel would
consider enlisting?"
"Has the thought just come to you?" she smiled.
"It has bothered me since the very night Tom
brought the news about Fort Sumter. Probably if
he were alone Dan would have gone at once; but he
may feel that he shouldn't leave after getting us to
come so far from home."
"And he's right," asserted Luther irritably, though
Persis knew that the strain in his voice came, not
from selfish motives, but from anxiety about his
brother; although he added: "Suppose he goes, andsomething happens to me, with you and Mary here
alone in this far-away place?"
"Have you forgotten Michael?"
"No, but the boy's young, with his own way to
make in a new country. I'd never have left the farm,
my dear, if I hadn't counted on Daniel to stand by."
"Well, he has stood by pretty well for a rover, you
know," soothed Persis. "And could he foresee that a
Civil War was coming? Let's not cross the bridge
until we reach it, Luther. So far as I know, Dan
hasn't said a word about enlisting; and almost
everybody in the gulch is acting restless these days.
Perhaps it will quiet his nerves if he goes down to
that meeting. The poor man's been cooped up so
long here in these hills."
"More likely he'd be swept away in a rush of
patriotism and not come back at all," replied Luther
gloomily. "But a man who's managed his own life
since he was twelve, Persis, is too old to ask advice. I
hope he'll stick with us a while longer, though, for
Michael and I need his help badly at the mine just
now."
Perhaps because he realized this need, Daniel
Alden said nothing about his urge to attend the
proposed meeting, though as the day approached,
Michael, seeing the man's craving for action, of-
fered him Shamrock for the trip down. But Daniel
shook his head.
"I'm afraid to trust myself, boy. A war's the one
thing I've never been in, and like as not if I heard
the speeches and saw the flags I'd do something rash.Time enough to get into the thick of it later; but
for the present I'd best not play with fire for I'm
made of inflammable material, and that's the truth."
Michael laughed and responded: "But you're wilt-
ing for a change, Uncle Dan. Couldn't we trust you
if you promised to come back?"
Daniel shook his head.
"Didn't I just say I was afraid to trust myself? And
anyhow, there's talk of a mass meeting here in the
gulch. Don't urge me, Michael, for it would be a
crime to desert my brother till he's better fixed than
he is now."
Nevertheless, Dan envied the men who left their
work to trek down the mountains to that meeting,
young Tom Otis among them, having arranged for
Polly and the baby to stay with Persis during the
days of his absence.
"It was a thrilling time," he told them on his
return. "I wouldn't have missed it for a hundred
dollars. They sent a message to President Lincoln
after the meeting. Here! I wrote it down so I'd re-
member to tell you. It said" (Tom produced a
crumpled paper from his pocket), "The eyes of
the whole world are upon you; the sympathies of the
American people are with you; and may the God of
battles sustain the stars and stripes.' "
"Those are stirring words," commented Luther
after a silence, casting a furtive glance upon his
brother. "It's too bad you didn't go, Dan," he added
kindly.
"If I had," Daniel confessed with a short laugh,"I'd no doubt be aboard a Leavenworth and Pike's
Peak stage coach this very minute, bound for some
battlefield! But I'd have enjoyed the excitement at
Denver City just the same. What other news did
you pick up, Tom?"
"Not much. War was on everybody's tongue;
though there was some hubbub too, about a big re-
ception for the new Governor. It's to be held May
twentieth at the Tremont House, and the hotel will
be gorgeously illuminated and decorated with flags.
The élite of Denver will be there, and everyone else,
I suppose. I'd like Polly to see it if the trip down and
back wasn't so exhausting for a woman."
"Are there many Confederates in the city?" ques-
tioned Daniel Alden, leaning forward eagerly.
"Too many," replied Tom. "They are far from
popular just now, but will probably learn to keep
their mouths shut after a while. And there are Con-
federates here in the gulch too, Mr. Alden. I've
been wondering how they'll behave when we stage
our own mass meeting in Central City."
Weeks passed, while Daniel kept faithfully at work.
Every few days news drifted up from Denver; and on
these occasions men dropped their picks and women
their pans as they ran to hear the last reports:
A Confederate flag had, it seemed, been raised
over a store on Larimer Street. A growd had gathered,
uncertain what to do; but at last the indignant men
resorted to such jeers and humorous remarks at the
expense of the owners, that they promptly removedit.. John Slough had received a commission to
recruit two companies for the Army. They were to
proceed to Fort Garland and relieve the regular
troops, which would be sent to the States. There
...
...
might even be a company recruited from Gregory
Point, Black Hawk and Central City!
Hand-
bills were to be posted, offering to buy arms of any
description.... And so it went.
Yet through all this excitement there were times
when the war seemed so remote as to be unreal-
something that could not be happening-to those
dwellers in the isolated mountain gulch. This was
a busy season to the New Englanders, for Persis and
Mary spent several hours each day baking bread for
the miners, an occupation which brought in a sure
if modest recompense, and gave them the satisfac-
tion of feeling themselves useful while their men
were toiling in the hills. But as the summer waned,
Daniel Alden began to wonder if his brother's find
was, after all, the rich vein he had predicted, al-
though the three men had returned each night with
gold that more than repaid their labor; and the event-
ful day when they took out more than one hundred
dollars, would never be forgotten. Indeed, these
earnings seemed not less than miraculous to the
Massachusetts farmer and his wife, who for twenty
years had counted every penny before spending it.
But there was no doubt that the digging grew
harder day by day; and in early September there
came to pass the very thing that Daniel had been
dreading. The vein ran into rock so solid that it washopeless to follow it with pick and shovel. What
next? he asked himself, standing still for a moment
to watch a widening circle of dampness on Michael's
shirt as he bent his back to the grueling task. The
arastra, a crude Mexican grinder used for pulveriz-
ing the ore between heavy millstones, was being used
at some mines with but poor success; and heavy
stamp mills were little better. At Black Hawk a
primitive smelter had been tried out, but only small
amounts of gold were recovered by this method. What
next? repeated Daniel, and could not answer.
...
It was at the close of the third day after this dis-
covery (which had been kept from the women, since
all three dreaded to dash their hopes) that Dan
Alden threw down his pick, and cried: "What a fool
I was to promise those blessed girls that they'd swish
about in silken skirts! Better quit now, Michael, than
to break your back trying to dig into that quartz.
What good will more gold do us anyway, if the get-
ting it out makes us old men before our time? But
listen, Luther. Here's a bit of advice, and I beg you
to heed it. No matter what the temptation, don't sell
this claim for a mere song. There's gold here-deep
in these rocks. That I am sure of; and in time some-
one smarter than we are will discover a way to get
at it. Mary may yet buy silk gowns from this lode
that's got the better of us now; and in that case,
man, your grandchildren will rise up and call you
blessed!"
"God forbid that it should take a gold mine to
accomplish that," retorted Luther solemnly, whileMichael turned his head to wink at Dan. "But your
advice is sound, brother, and we'll hang onto this
claim as long as possible. Let us go home now and
face the music. It's a good thing, Michael, that our
wives didn't marry us for money."
"Here's the time I should thank my stars I've no
wife to disappoint," laughed Daniel Alden. "There
are advantages in a footloose existence, Michael; and
many's the time I've thought what a grand com-
panion you'd make on my adventures, if Mary hadn't
already cut me out. Next time I'll dig for nothing less
precious than diamonds and rubies. Don't you wish,
young fellow, that you were free to join me?"
"Have you forgotten, Dan," said Luther as he bent
to pick up a shovel, "that 'the price of a good woman
is above rubies'?"
"Score one for you, Mr. Alden!" applauded
Michael. "I wouldn't swap my Mary for all the rubies
in the world; nor would Uncle Dan, if he were
obliged to make the choice. And I'm not afraid to
carry this bad news home, disappointing though it
is. No doubt Mother Persis will say that riches would
have spoiled us!"
This was exactly what happened, for Persis Alden,
seeing how hard it was for her husband to break the
news, merely commented: "There! Didn't I tell you
we mustn't count on it? Don't look so downhearted,
Luther. Like as not great wealth would have made
us selfish as it's done many another. And besides,
we have enough laid by to keep us going a long
time, or to take us back home in the spring if yourheart's set on it. Cheer up, Daniel. I don't know
when I've seen you look so discouraged."
"I was thinking of those silk petticoats I promised
you and Mary," he confessed ruefully.
"Don't frown over that, Uncle Dan," comforted
Mary, rumpling his hair. "Give him time, and
Michael will provide all the silk I need; and if he
doesn't it will make no difference anyway. What I
mind most is not being able to help the friends at
home; but at least I had the fun of planning, and so
did Mother."
"How many million dollars did you two spend-
thrifts part with?" questioned her uncle, smiling.
"Oh, we were lavish! We gave away everything
from pianos to Shetland ponies; but it looks now as
though Cousin Hezekiah would continue to drive to
church in a Concord wagon, instead of the Goddard
buggy Mother gave him in her day dreams."
"And dear Miss Mattie will never have her gray
silk gown," said Persis with genuine regret.
Michael who had been sitting by the table, his
tired shoulders drooping a bit, arose suddenly and
gave his wife a hug that brought a blush to her
cheek, for when others were present Mary possessed
the characteristic New England reticence.
"I don't see wealth spoiling either of these women,"
he observed, "and it may yet come out of that very
claim. In the meantime, there are more mines to dis-
cover, if not here, in other parts of the mountains."
"True,” declared Daniel. "What say we run down
to Denver City for a day or two, boy, and see whatnews is coming in from California gulch? It would
be a lark."
But Michael shook his head.
"Count me out, Uncle Dan. I'll do some prospect-
ing nearer home for a while. But you're welcome to
Shamrock if you want a few days off-that is, if you'll
promise to bring her back again!"
"That I will," responded Daniel, "and if Persis
will put me up a box of grub I'll be off in the morn-
ing. Come with me, won't you, Lute? You need a
change, and home will seem all the better after you've
been away."
Luther was silent a moment, and then confessed:
"The truth is, Dan, I'm pretty tired, and don't feel
up to any sort of trip just now; though I've no doubt
we'd have a great time together," he added, pleased
that his brother should have asked for his company
on such a jaunt.
"This is what marriage does to a man," snorted
Daniel, endeavoring to look disgusted, but not suc-
ceeding very well, for his eyes softened as they met a
smile from Persis. "Perhaps I can pick up a com-
panion down the gulch. John Gardner, that lawyer
fellow, told me the other day he meant to make a trip
down before winter sets in. I'll hunt him up now;
and if he can't leave to-morrow I'll go by myself,
which I've done before this without regretting it."
"Shamrock is better company than most people
anyway," declared Mary. "She knows all you say, and
doesn't bother to answer back. Good-night, darling,"
she added as the man grasped his hat, eager to makeplans now the path was clear. "We'll be here bright
and early to see you off."
And none knew, as soon after sunrise, they stood
on the jig-saw trimmed porch and watched Dan

the last dip in the trail to wave his hat in the gay

that their much-loved soldier of fortune had set out
on the far adventure from which there is no return.You

OU don't need to break the news, John. I knew

tree below. Her greeting brought an expression of
relief to the young man's face. He had not wanted to
bring such news; and seating himself on the low rail-
ing, he answered:
"Rather, Dan's on his way to enlist, Mrs. Alden. He
left on a stage coach for the States, hoping to get
into some Massachusetts regiment. I've a letter here
which explains everything; but he begged me to
corroborate the fact that no such impulsive idea had
crossed his mind when he started out. He seemed to
be swept away by the news-the excitement-the call
to arms. I felt as I watched him that the man couldn't
have done otherwise, though he realized fully what
a blow his going would be to you all."
"A blow indeed," replied Persis, her quiet voice
concealing inward tumult. "It is no more than others
are doing for their country now; but it will hurt my
husband that his brother did not return to say good-
bye, though perhaps it's as well. He left with a cheerysmile and a wave of his hat-a happy parting to re-
member if-if he should not come back."
"Please God, he will," returned the lawyer soberly.
"He's a grand fellow, Dan Alden. We got well
acquainted on our trip down the mountains, and I
shall always recall it with pleasure, whatever hap-
pens."
The young man arose, taking some letters from a
pocket as he explained: "These had been held by
mistake at the Denver post office, so I brought them
along with your brother-in-law's note. Tell Michael
Warwick that his mare's as easy as a rocking horse,
and I'd pay her weight in gold to possess her if I had
the dust! Now I must run. I-I am truly sorry to
have brought bad news."
"How could you help it?" replied Persis with a
hard-won smile. "Come up again, John, for we'll want
to hear about Dan's trip to Denver City. And well,
good-bye."
When he had gone the woman did not move, just
turned the missives idly in her hands. As a rule,
letters from home were thrilling things and she would
have torn them open with eager fingers. Now,
curiously, they did not matter-not in the least.
One was from Julia White, she noticed almost me-
chanically. Another bore the fine, copper-plate
penmanship of Parson Gilder.
And this?
•
..
Persis stared, puzzled, at the expensive note paper-
the unfamiliar writing. Perhaps it was a long-
promised letter from Mrs. MacTavish. It was
for Mary. Lastly her fingers touched the one
•...
addressed by Dan himself. Her brave eyes
misted.
"Dearest folks-all of you," Daniel had written.
"You'll know what has happened before you read this
letter, for John Gardner will tell you. Don't think
too badly of me. I had no thought of such a proceed-
ing when I said good-bye; but I just can't help going,
and that's the truth. It's not alone the zest for a new
adventure for change and excitement. Knowing my-
self, I realize that those urges play a part in it, of
course; but it's something more a sense of duty,
perhaps, that is urging me into this righteous war
-and a sense of loyalty to the good old Common-
wealth of Massachusetts that keeps me from enlisting
in the companies recruited in Colorado. So I'm off
by the stage that's leaving in an hour, and time is
short.
"Listen to this, Luther: For God's sake, don't let
a mistaken sense of duty to your country drive you
into doing what I have done. Stick by your wife and
daughter, for Persis needs you more than the army
does; and, even with Michael to fend for her, your
girl wants her father. But I am only a footloose old
bachelor, and it's such as I who should be the first
to go; and men are badly wanted, they tell us.
"Even now, Lute, I wouldn't leave if you needed
my help at the mine; but you've taken out enough
ore to see you through even a hard winter, and to
get you back on the farm come spring, if that's what
you still want. My share of the dust is yours now,
brother. John has arranged all that. I always meantyou to have it anyway, for what use is gold to a
vagabond like me? That will fix you up pretty well
financially, you see; and when the big fight is over
I'll be with you again, whether you're East or West.
"And it's a comforting thought that we've had this
year together, isn't it? a wonderful year as I look
back upon it. May we all survive to enjoy many an-
other such year! John will tell you of the events in
Denver City, and all the stirring things that hastened
my decision. Thinking it over, I'm glad there's no
time to go back and say good-bye. I might have been
tempted to play the baby. There's a lump in my
throat now, if you want the truth, and-Oh, what's
the use in trying to say it all? I can't. But remember
I love you every one-have always loved you more,
perhaps, that you've ever guessed. ..."
When Luther Alden came down from the hills
an hour later, his wife still sat where the bearer of
this news had left her, Dan's letter clasped tensely
in her hands.I
CHAPTER XXXVII
T may be well that our eyes are blinded to the
future, for had Luther Alden foreseen the happen-
ings of the next four years, he might with good cause
have put some of his precious dust into oxen (the
beasts who had brought them across the plains hav-
ing been sold long since to a miner returning to
Illinois) and started eastward, thankful that the
Shining Mountains of Mary's dreams had yielded
sufficient gold to take them home. Perhaps though
it was long before he spoke the thought-this was
what the man really wanted, for with his brother
gone, all zest seemed to have dropped out of their
great adventure; and Luther's roots were deep in
the rocky New England soil he had left behind.
But there were others to consider. There was
Persis. Almost daily her husband marveled at the
woman's adaptability to this frontier life. Though
they had lived together for more than twenty years,
he had never suspected his wife of so much en-
thusiasm. Only at one time had she drooped. For a
few days following Daniel's unexpected departure her
face lost its brightness; and more than once Luther
found her at a window, staring down the gulch with
unseeing eyes.
"I can't realize that Dan will not come back, that'sall," she said when her husband asked what she was
watching. "I look down the trail and, somehow,
expect to see him round the curve on Shamrock,
waving his hat as he catches sight of me. There!"
(it was a contrite exclamation, as the man winced),
"I should be ashamed-making you feel worse than
you do anyway! Kiss me, my dear. I'm lonesome. I
almost believe I've a touch of homesickness and need
to be shown that you still love me."
Luther's arms went 'round her a bit shyly. He
could not remember when this wife of his had ex-
pressed any such need.
"Would you like to go home, Persis?" he ques-
tioned, his heart lifting a bit at the expectation.
But Persis shook her head.
"Not with winter so near. And besides, Michael
has had an offer from those men who've been carry-
ing on the gold brokerage business here at the mines.
They want him to represent them, for one partner
has already enlisted, and the other plans to go down
to Denver for the winter. Michael is well liked in
the gulch. Folks trust his honesty. And as the boy
says, this may be the beginning of something better.
If the town grows there's sure to be a bank estab-
lished here; or he might get a chance at Denver City.
At any rate, he can accept their offer for the present,
if it seems best. He wants us to think it over care-
fully."
"Do you mean," questioned Luther, putting one
hand to his throat as if to smooth away the rising
lump within, "do you mean, my dear, that Michaelwould consider settling in Colorado for good and
all?"
"Why not?" countered the dauntless Persis. "It's
a new country, Luther-a growing country with
countless opportunities for a young man. And
Michael's no farmer, remember. What would he do
in the village if we should all go home?"
So there was Michael to consider and Mary, who,
like her mother, took so naturally to this strange en-
vironment. Thus Luther Alden resigned himself to
another winter in the mountains, and set to work
accumulating a huge pile of firewood, ready for the
cold days to come. He did not dream that before he
left this place the Civil War would be fought and
ended that news of the conflict would, for the last
year and more, reach them daily by way of telegraph
wires spanning the miles between Julesburg and
that far mountain settlement as by a miracle. He
could not know that those years of bloodshed were to
bring both joy and sorrow to his small family-
that he was to lose a brother, and gain two grand-
children that following drouth and flood he would
see grain, fuel and provisions rise to famine prices,
and gold mount to the highest point known in the
entire history of the country.
It was the February after Daniel Alden's depar-
ture for Massachusetts, on the very day, in fact, when
the first Colorado troops set out in heavy snow to the
relief of Santa Fé and what was to go down in his-
tory as the battle of La Glorieta, that Mary's first
son was born, to be followed a year later by his littlesister. Thus in the summer of '62, when Luther's
heart turned once more toward his native state, there
was no talk of considering such a journey, for the
proud grandfather would never have risked crossing
the plains with his precious grandson, and Mary was
facing her second pregnancy.
Then too, even had the coming of those beloved
babies not frustrated Luther Alden's plans, Michael
was making real progress in the work he had under-
taken. People trusted him, and, despite his youth,
came to him for advice; and there were high hopes
that once the war was over he would be transferred
to Denver City, where living would be simpler for
them all.
During that summer of 1862, Luther made one
more effort to work his claim; but as Daniel said, it
was useless to try to drill into the solid granite. He
prospected a little-worked at wages for another mine
owner-cut and stacked firewood for neighbors
whose men were fighting for the Union; and with
true unselfishness resolutely thrust aside the longing
for home which had never been quite stilled.
By unanimous family vote Mary's first son was
called Daniel Alden, and among the letters she was
to treasure always was one from her uncle, written
on the eve of battle, and expressing profound de-
light in the honor she had done him. The little girl,
a tiny feminine edition of her father, they named
Patricia, for Michael's mother, promising Persis that
the next small daughter, if such there were, should be
her namesake.The summer of 1863 was never forgotten by even
the most intrepid pioneer, for drouth descended
upon the land, drying the streams as if they had been
soaked up by a mighty sponge which left no moisture
behind. Crops failed; and sad news came up the hills
from the dwellers on the plains below. Stock on the
ranges died from thirst and hunger; and as the
weeks passed it was impossible to maintain com-
munication with the States for supply trains were
blockaded-abandoned. Even the stages were delayed
sometimes for days, while their drivers wandered
frantically on the trackless prairies, completely be-
wildered by the fierce, wild pouderies. It was then
that provisions rose to famine prices in the moun-
tains, while at Denver City conditions were only a
little less appalling.
And to make things worse, winter was upon them
by the middle of October, a winter which buried the
gulches deep in snow; so when spring came again the
melting drifts flooded the mines to the dismay and
discouragement of those who sought to work them.
Then, as if nature were doing her best to defeat
these strangers who intruded on her solitude, rain
descended in torrents, and veritable rivers poured
down those steep slopes onto the unhappy residents,
making short work of many a mountain cabin, and
filling others with mud and débris.
Down in the valley things were no better. Ranches
were well nigh destroyed, and grazing land made
useless by a deep, deep covering of sand which turned
it into a dreary desert. And through all this hopelesstime came rumors of Indian warfare far and near;
while prices rose, and calls for troops more troops,
swept many a discouraged man into the army, not
from patriotic motives so much as in the hope of
evading what looked like possible starvation.
But to the miners in the hills one bright spot shone
amid the gloom-the price of gold. It mounted high,
higher than any of them had dreamed when they
left comfortable homes behind them and set out
across the plains to seek their fortunes; for war was
draining the Treasury of a million dollars every day.
Yet even this one gleam had its dark side. The sur-
face gold was exhausted, and mines sunk below three
hundred feet filled rapidly with water. If the work
were to continue it would be necessary to pump, and
the cost of transporting suitable machinery from
Chicago or Pittsburgh prohibited any such venture;
while primitive appliances with which most of the
men experimented, were proving useless.
This was a busy time to Michael Warwick, for the
rise in gold had brought from the East and elsewhere
an overwhelming demand for mines and mining
stock; yet there were so many fraudulent titles in
the gulch that many purchasers were unable to locate
the claims they had paid for. When these troubles
arose it was Michael, who represented the gold
brokerage interests in the vicinity, to whom they
turned for aid and advice. But so eager were many of
these companies to begin work that costly buildings
were erected before it was known whether the ore
would pay or not.Watching these unwise expenditures, Michael War-
wick shook his head.
"It can't go on," he told his father-in-law one day
when they were discussing the situation. "If these men
continue to sink money into their ventures at such
a rate, the boom will collapse some day like a pricked
balloon. Unless some miracle occurs meanwhile, I
give them six months at the most."
"And then?" asked Luther, lifting such discouraged
eyes the young man laughed and answered:
"Cheer up! Our thrifty wives have kept down ex-
penditures so cleverly, and the price of gold has
climbed so high, that we're better off now than when
Uncle Daniel left us. Besides, even if Central City is
to peter out, there are countless openings at Den-
ver."
"And there's the farm waiting for us at home, if
ever this cursed war is over," returned Luther. "I'd
like you to know the place, Michael-the fine old
house built more than a hundred years ago the
view down the valley from our west windows, and
the high pasture with its sweet fern and juniper
bushes. There is nothing in all these Rocky Moun-
tains to compare with it, boy; yet my wife and
daughter have turned their backs on all that beauty
without regrets."
"Poor man!" said Persis, stopping beside Luther's
chair to lay a gentle hand upon his shoulder. "You
think we are faithless to the old home, I suppose,
and the thought hurts you. But you're wrong, my
dear. I love the old place, too, and even think itmore beautiful than this; but there's a thrill in hav-
ing a hand in the settling of a new country, Luther.
Do you never feel it?"
"And you wouldn't be a granddad if we had
stayed on the farm," Mary reminded him with a
smile. "Aren't these babies worth more to you,
Father, than a sight of the old well sweep and a
few juniper bushes?"
"That's a mean advantage for you to take, daugh-
ter," replied Luther, smiling at her indulgently.
"Still, I fear I am one that can't be transplanted-
at my age anyway."
"Why, you talk like an old man, Luther Alden!"
exclaimed Persis with indignation. "And I never felt
younger in my life! It's the work and worry of this
last year that has upset you, to say nothing of of
having Dan go off as he did. But when times are
better and the war is ended life will look brighter
to you, my dear, even if we're still far from home
when that day comes."
"But according to Michael, worse, not better days
are on the way to Central City," Luther retorted,
dryly.
In this prophecy Michael Warwick was not far
wrong, for in April, only a few months later, the
boom collapsed. New companies ceased work; and
even some of the older, more patient inhabitants of
the gulch, fearing a future in the hills too bleak to
face, deserted their cabins or calico cottages to trek
down the mountains, hopeful of finding work at
Denver City.Many of these discouraged people went only to
meet fresh tragedy, for on a night in May, following
a series of unusual cloudbursts, a wall of water fif-
teen feet high raged down the arid Cherry Creek,
sweeping all before it: cattle, sheep, dwellings-the
Larimer Street bridge the Methodist church. The
office of the Rocky Mountain News with its flagpole
bearing the stars and stripes went too, the heavy
presses being discovered later at least two miles down
the Platte. Even the City Hall and its records, the
jail and its prisoners, were carried off in that relent-
less torrent which overwhelmed the city.
This disaster, following a fire which had swept the
place a year before, might well have shaken the
courage of the most stout-hearted; and during the
summer not only a plague of grasshoppers ravaged
the plains, but Indians, Sioux, Arapahoes, Chey-
ennes, spurred on no doubt by many a legitimate
grievance, raided and plundered, and the tale of
more than one bloody tragedy made its way to that
settlement in the hills above.
"The story is almost too horrible to repeat," said
Michael one evening when he brought the news.
"The Cheyennes attacked a lonely ranch, Mary,
killed, scalped and mutilated the entire family. The
bodies were brought to town and exposed to the pub-
lic last night, to show them what they may expect, I
suppose. Life is not so easy here in many ways, dar-
ling, but I'm thankful we didn't leave for Denver
City last April with some of our impatient neigh-
bors."Mary shuddered as she laid her sleeping baby in
his homemade crib.
"Oh, how I wish that all wars and fighting were
over, and Uncle Dan safely home!" she cried out
with passion. "It is months since we've had a word
from him, Michael, and the silence frightens me-
frightens Father and Mother, too, though they won't
admit it. I can't imagine what life would be without
my Uncle Danny. If he shouldn't come back..."
She could not finish the sentence; nor did Mary
Warwick ever know that as she spoke those words
her uncle's joyous spirit was drifting slowly, pain-
lessly, happily, into a safe harbor.
