CHAPTER XXXVIII
HE first news of Daniel Alden's death was not,
as it happened, received officially, but in a
letter addressed to Persis by his own hand, which
contained not only his last words, but a note from the
nurse who had cared for him during the days he
survived the fatal wounds received in battle.
"Captain Alden," she wrote, and it was the first
his family knew that Daniel had been promoted to a
Captaincy, "asked me to see that his last message to
you was dispatched without delay. There was no
possible hope from the first; but it seemed to me
that the Captain kept himself alive by sheer force
of will until this letter was finished, for there were
many interruptions-times when the agony was too
great for him to hold a pen. He may not have told
you that he was three times cited for bravery, and
had received two medals. He was a gallant gentle-
man and a brave soldier who faced death as cou-
rageously as, I feel sure, he had faced life. After end-
ing this letter, he fell into a deep sleep of exhaustion
from which he did not waken. It was a privilege to
have cared for him these last days, and to have eased,
even a little, the pain he bore with so much forti-
tude...."
Luther was working in the hills on the morningthis news arrived. It was Michael who brought the
letter in to Persis, and who stood by while she read
the note from Daniel's nurse. Neither spoke for a
moment, but their eyes met in a silence that meant
more than words. Then Persis said:
"I think I would like to be alone when I read his
letter, Michael. Go home now and break the news
to Mary. The dear girl will need all the help you
and the children can give her, for she has worshiped
her uncle since she was too young to speak his name."
So, understanding that need of solitude just then,
Michael kissed her like the good son he was, and
went up the hill bearing his sad message. Thus Persis
was alone when she read what proved to be the only
love letter of Daniel Alden's life-a letter curiously
like himself, touched with his own whimsy, even
though he knew that the dark shadow hovered near:
"My very dear Persis: The surgeon gives me ten
days, provided I lie still and let them feed me like
a baby! Can you see me doing it? When I said there
was a letter to be written, he called it 'suicide,' so per-
haps, after all, my name will not go down in history
as one who died for his country! But what does that
matter so long as I say the things I'd have said if I'd
had the luck to return and some that I wouldn't!
And the latter, dear Persis, being the most impor-
tant, makes me wonder if I'm not lucky to be going,
or I might never have had the happiness of this
confession.
"It is strange to think that when you read theselines I shall be away on the last of my adventures-
the adventure from which there is no returning.
Somehow I feel sure it's a happy place I'm going to,
and not so far off but that (if I behave myself!) I'll
be given occasional glimpses of my dear ones who
have been left in this beautiful world of ours to
carry on for a while longer. At any rate, it will be
fun making ready a mansion for you all in the
Heavenly City. Perhaps that very task will keep me
from missing you too much; and I'll be at the Gate
to meet each and every one of you that I will
promise!
"Does all this sound like levity to my big brother?
If it troubles him, Persis, that I should speak so in
my last hours, tell Luther from me that true to the
good old psalm our mother used to read us, I fear
no evil as I walk through the valley of the shadow
of death, and that this seeming lightness is only my
way of saying that (though I've never had the time
nor inclination to join a church) my faith in the
everlasting life is as firm as his own; only I can't see
it as a place of harps and haloes for Dan Alden!
And I'd like my brother to know, too, that he has
always stood for something very fine in my life-
something as solid and unchangeable as those New
England hills he loves so dearly-something as
dependable as a good mother, if you know what
I mean.
"And our sweet Mary! Does she realize half the
great thrill I experienced when you wrote that she
and Michael had named their son for me? It wastruly one of the most exciting days of my life-and
there've been many. Yet all that I have to bequeath
the boy is an untarnished name, and a couple of silly
medals which (I assure you!) they pinned on
me for doing nothing at all. Tell Mary also, that she
has been niece, and daughter, and youth and hope to
me all these years; and it gives me a twinge of real
sadness to think I can't hold her babies in my arms
a moment before I go. As for Michael, he is a fellow
after my own heart, as you all know. Surely there'll
come a day long hence when we two will meet again
on some 'happy hunting ground,' and go on the
mutual adventures we were denied here.
"And now, Persis-my-dear, comes the great confes-
sion! Did you ever guess that your vagabond brother
was leading a double life? Sometimes I've wondered.
..
But let's get back to the story, for my strength
is none too good at times, and I can't set out on my
journey with a light heart, dear girl, unless it is told.
"Once upon a time, long, long ago, there was a
foolish lad who ran away from a good home to seek
Life and Adventure. If I recall the boy's emotions
correctly, he had in mind a triumphant return, laden
with treasure. He saw himself strutting a bit before
his elder brother-who had stayed home like the
dutiful son he was, to look after their mother-and
cutting quite a fine figure in the village right in front
of those whom he suspected of calling him a young
fool to have gone away.
"Well, five years drifted by, and though the fel-
low found adventures a-plenty, the treasure was stilllacking when one winter's day a tremendous long-
ing swept over him to see how the folks at home
were faring after this long time. It took him a good
part of the day to reach Boston, and some hours
more to get to the place where he set out on foot.
By that time a blizzard was raging, and the lad had
to fight his way up a steep road which was buried in
drifts-the road that led to the farm where his grand-
father had been born. As he drew near he saw lights
shining out from the windows (for it was evening by
this time), and his heart pounded like a triphammer,
knowing how his mother would rejoice to see him
walk in, treasure or no treasure. Thinking, too, that
at least he'd been living real life all these years, while
his stay-at-home brother had been just existing there
on the farm, and, unless a miracle occurred (which
was most unlikely), would never possess anything
that remotely resembled a worth-while treasure of
any kind.
"So, feeling pretty well satisfied with life and him-
self, our young nomad pushed open the door with-
out a knock, and stepped inside. Gosh! but it was
good to be home! He caught up his mother and
hugged her tight; and found, rather to his own sur-
prise, that he wanted to hug his brother as well. Not
until these joyful greetings were over did he look
'round the old room, which at first glance seemed
just as he had left it five years before and at second
glance looked entirely different, and would, some-
thing told him right then, never look quite the same
to him again."For in a low chair by the hearth a girl was sit-
ting-the most beautiful girl the boy had even seen
-the most beautiful girl in the world! Her wavy
brown hair was parted in the middle as a woman's
should be, and her brown eyes under their level
brows looked up at him shyly. Her mouth (which
was, and still is, dear Persis, the loveliest mouth God
ever created) was half serious, half smiling, and he
caught a glimpse of the perfect teeth within. Her
dress was brown like her eyes, and she wore some
sort of a white collar pinned with a cameo pin. All
this the boy saw at a glance, and has never forgotten;
but as he stared at the girl, tongue-tied, bewildered
at finding her there, his brother broke into a laugh,
and, forgetting in that moment that he was a true
product of New England who must suppress his feel-
ings at all costs, slipped a possessive arm about the
girl and drew her to her feet, saying proudly: 'Don't
you remember Persis Ordway who lived with her aunt
in a house facing the common? Didn't you get the
letter telling you we were married last October? This
is Persis, Dan.'
"Persis Ordway! The prodigal thought fast. Could
this heavenly creature be that thin little girl with a
red bow on the end of her pigtail-the girl he had
pelted with spitballs one day after school? Ye gods!
What miracles the years do bring about! And she
was married to Luther!
"Then, because, after all, he was rather a self-
possessed youth for his years, and because he was
crazy to kiss her and not as a man kisses his brother'swife, either, he managed to shake hands sedately
enough and mumble something unintelligible that
did for the time being, and since he must hug
someone or die went back to his mother and
hugged her again.
"But that night, tired though he was, the boy
could not sleep. He lay in his old familiar bed under
the eaves, tossing and turning, thumping his pillow
and wondering what it was that ailed him. Not until
dawn showed pink through the frosted windowpanes
did he realize what he was trying to figure out-
that his brother Luther, who'd never been out of
New England, nor sailed a ship, nor seen the fairy
isles of the sea, had found, right in that sleepy village
in their own valley, the most beautiful, the most-to-
be-desired treasure in all the world!
"Yes, dear Persis, he thought that out before he
slept a wink. Yet not till later, when he had sat long
winter evenings spinning his yarns of foreign ports
and snow-capped mountains, while the girl sat in her
low chair looking up at him, her eyes wide with won-
der at his tales, did the youth realize all that she
meant to him, and was to mean to him through all
the years. When, as was of course inevitable, this
knowledge came, his only thought (and may God
give him a good mark for it!) was that, come what
might, he must not do anything to hurt his brother.
So, though it was like tearing the heart out of his
body, he went away-has always been going away
for the same reason, only to return as a moth returns
to singe its wings at a lighted candle. For she was•
that to him always, my Persis a light in the dark-
a glowing, undying flame in his restless life.
"Well, that's all, I believe. The story is ended.
Did you ever in all these long years guess my secret?
Sometimes I've wondered, my darling. (Forgive me,
brother!) Once I saw tears in your eyes when I came
away. And again, on a night in the gulch when we
all sat listening to the distant songs of the miners.
Do you remember?
...
"And now, since my hand's too shaky for you to
read more words, I'll say good-night-not good-bye,
my dear one, for good-bye is a sad word at best, and
I would not have you sad for a single minute. But
I'm tired, beloved so tired that now this letter is
ended I'll 'lay me down to sleep' for a little while.
And if, as the old prayer says, 'I die before I wake,'
remember that I'm not really dead (as we on earth
are prone to regard that miracle), but just away, on
the greatest adventure of all!"PERSIS
CHAPTER XXXIX
ERSIS was always thankful that she was spared
the pain of telling her husband that his brother
would not return. As it happened, Luther stopped
at Michael's cabin on his way from work that after-
noon, meaning to have a romp with the babies; but
one glance at his daughter's stricken countenance
told him the truth.
"Dan's gone?" he asked, the words almost a whis-
per.
Mary nodded, her lips unsteady as she made an
effort to wink back tears. It was Michael who came
to her rescue, saying quietly: "He left a letter, Mr.
Alden. It came more than an hour ago but we
haven't seen it, for Mother Persis wanted to read
it first alone. Will you bring it up after supper?
These youngsters must go to bed, and we can't leave
them."
He meant, of course, that it would be best for
Mary just then to busy herself about the babies; and
Luther, understanding, took the girl into his arms
for a mute moment, and then went out. Thank God
for Persis! he was thinking as he stumbled on, not
looking where he set his feet. She would give him
the strength to face this loss, as she had always given
him strength in time of trouble. Though his broth-er's going would be a blow to her, she would meet
it with courage.
Nor was Luther mistaken, for opening the door
softly, fearful, perhaps, of finding his wife in tears,
since he was home before she would expect him, he
saw her smoothing a white cloth on their small sup-
per table. The familiar sight calmed his taut nerves
almost unbelievably; then Persis turned, and coming
forward, put her arms about him.
"You know, my dear?"
Like Mary, Luther could only nod; but his wife
felt tears that she knew were not her own wetting
her face.
"Sit down," she said when the arms that held her
loosened a little. "How is Mary? I didn't go to her
because I was sure the children's needs were her best
medicine, just as" (she smiled at him bravely, though
her lovely mouth was not quite steady), "just as get-
ting your supper, Luther darling, has helped me.
Now sit down and read this letter from Daniel's
nurse; and then wash up, for our meal is almost
ready."
,
"Michael said" (Luther's voice sounded strange,
rusty, as if he had not used it for a long time)
"Michael said," he repeated, "that there's a letter
from Dan himself. Did-did he know he was-
going?"
Persis had moved a few steps off. Now she returned
to run her fingers through the man's graying hair.
"Yes, he knew. It's a beautiful letter; but I want
you to eat supper before you read it. You're tired,my dear, and Dan's death is a blow between the eyes.
You need food before too much emotion, and"
"Good God!" cried out Luther unexpectedly,
"there's emotion enough inside me now to last a
lifetime! Do you think I can eat?"
"You can, if I can," she told him quietly. "Life
must go on for all of us, you know; and there's Mary
to think of. Once I get you a cup of tea you will feel
better."
So it was not till later, when Persis was clearing
away the supper dishes, that Luther Alden read the
letter his brother had written. He sat outside on the
little porch, hearing almost subconsciously his wife's
familiar step as she went back and forth putting the
small room in order for the night. Persis was long
about her tasks that evening, purposely long. She had
been stirred to her very depths by the story of Dan-
iel's love for her; yet in the hour between her first
perusal of his letter and her husband's return, she
had tried to read the tale again through Luther's
eyes. It must not hurt him-this knowledge that
meant so much to her. Shocked he might be for a
few moments-surprised, and (being himself) a bit
bewildered by such a revelation, but not hurt if he
had a chance to think about it. Thus, she decided,
her husband must be alone when he read Dan's
"great confession." He must have time to see the
strength and beauty of his brother's character-all
his self-sacrifice. So Persis Alden set bread for to-
morrow's baking swept her kitchen floor unneces-sarily, and not until it could be put off no longer
did she join Luther where he sat alone on the nar-
row porch.
"Well?" she said quietly, and slipped a hand in
his.
The man looked up. He had read his brother's
farewell message more than once, and his heart was
torn for many reasons.
"It's a beautiful letter," said Persis, as she had
said before.
Luther cleared his throat. The words came hard.
"It-it is more of a love letter than I have ever
written you, my dear."
"It's just-Dan," she answered. "It was never hard
for him to say the things that were in his heart, you
know."
Luther was silent; then, his fingers gripping hers
too tightly: "I-I don't think he could have loved
you more than-than I have, Persis."
She smiled at that.
"Dear heart, you've loved me more than I deserve.
In all the years of our marriage I can recall few
angry words from you, and no harsh ones. Do you
suppose for a minute that Dan would have kept
silent the way he did if he hadn't seen that we were
happy together?"
"But I've been thinking. You were so young
when we married. Dan's letter has made me see
you as you were then. Hardly more than a child,
Persis. So sweet. So little. Many a time you must
have been lonely on the farm with no one butMother to speak to all day long-and I too big a
dunce to notice!"
"Too tired to notice," Persis corrected. "I could
have cried often, seeing you work so hard and only
a boy!"
Again there was silence while this wise woman
thought: "The wound won't heal till he has probed
it. Pray God he doesn't go too deep."
Then the man said: "You had no honeymoon. Dan
would have taken you on a honeymoon if he had
mortgaged the farm to do it."
"Perhaps," Persis admitted, a fugitive smile cross-
ing her face, "but it was you I married, Luther. Has
there been even once in all these more than twenty
years when you thought I regretted it?"
"But I am so stupid! I never saw how Dan felt
about you never dreamt you were more than a sis-
ter to him. Did-" He hesitated, as if he would have
held back the words if possible, then finished sud-
denly: "Did you know, Persis?"
It was the woman who was silent now for a little
time. The sunset glow had faded, and dusk was de-
scending bleakly on the mountain gulch. Dark pines
stood silhouetted against the sky-a light sprang out
in a cabin perched like a bird's nest on the steep
hillside-a laughing voice drifted up from some
home below-a dog barked once, and once again.
Then Persis answered:
...
"How can I say, Luther? Sometimes I think a
woman senses such things for no reason. Not by one
word did your brother betray himself-or you. Butthere were times when I surprised a look in his eyes
that-that troubled me. There were times, too, when
after a long absence his kiss was not the kiss of
a real brother. But what troubled me most was
something that can't be said in words a feeling each
time he left us that that it hurt him to go, and
that he went because, for some strange reason, he
dared not stay. And so I wondered."
Luther arose, walking across the porch and back
again; then said, almost as if angry at himself: "What
right have I to ask you all these questions-to pry
into your heart? One would think I had bought you
body and soul! Forgive me, Persis. But there's a
thing I must know or I'll never cease to think about
it: Would you have been happier married to Dan,
my dear? Tell me the truth. Would it have made
a difference?"
It was the question Persis had been expecting, and
her answer was ready. Yet she, too, was to learn some-
thing in that moment. For as she sprang up and met
her husband's eyes in the deepening twilight, the
subtle fears for her own loyalty which had vaguely
troubled her for the last hours, were swept away;
and she knew that whatever life with the gay Dan
Alden might have brought her, this tall, good, in-
articulate man who stood there looking down so
sadly, was now as much a part of her whole flesh
and being as were the hands she stretched out for
him to take.
Thus, though there were tears on her lashes, the
mouth that Dan Alden deemed "the loveliest God:
ever created" curved in a smile as she said gently:
"A difference? Of course it would have made a dif-
ference! Why, Luther, I love you! And have you for-
gotten, dear man, that only you and I could have
given our Mary to a world that's happier because
she's in it?"
And this wise answer lifted the shadow from
Luther's heart, as she had known it must.

CHAPTER XL
Ο bright May morning in the year of 1865,
Mary Warwick laid pen and ink on her
kitchen table, and for the first time in months
opened a leather-bound journal which had accom-
panied her across the plains. Few events had been
recorded therein since the one which Daniel Alden
had set down describing her own wedding, and now
she wrote:
"It may seem that this book has been too long neg-
lected, yet the truth is, that save for short entries I
have added about the war, and a note recording the
death of my Uncle Daniel, the happenings of these
four and a half years have been so much more per-
sonal than historical that I hesitated to use space for
them on these pages, which number too few as it is.
But now my father and mother are making plans
to return to Massachusetts by stage coach (for what
I pray will be only a visit), and wish to carry this
story of our migration to Parson Gilder, since with-
out his gift it would never have been written.
Though nearing his eighty-eighth milestone, our dear
old friend is, according to last accounts, in good
health and spirits, and we know what pleasure it
will give him to read the tale with his own eyes.
Thus I must bring it up to date both historically andpersonally, for as Father points out, in years to come
we may be regarded as Empire Builders, though it
is hard for me to see myself as anything so awe-
inspiring and important!
"First I must say that the war is ended, and-
alas! our martyred President no more. On April
9th. Lee surrendered at Appomattox Court House;
and less than a week later, while attending an en-
tertainment at Ford's Theatre in Washington, Presi-
dent Lincoln was struck down by an assassin. Thus
our rejoicing at the end of the frightful conflict was
turned to sorrow for this noble man who gave his
life for his country as truly as any who, like my
heroic uncle, received their death wounds on the
field of battle. Our mountain town went into mourn-
ing-business houses were closed; and it was a dreary
sight to see the flags drooping at half mast, with long
black streamers hanging limply in the still air. But
enough of this, for the story will be recorded more
graphically and at length in the history books my
children will study in the schoolroom, than by any
poor words of mine.
"Now for our hopes and plans. In the first place,
all things point to the fact that this settlement which
has been our happy home for nearly five years, must
ere long become rapidly deserted, since the placer
gold and surface works are exhausted; and though
there is doubtless much precious metal still left in
these hills, the situation is hopeless until some better
method of getting at it has been devised. Even now,
one after another our good friends here in thegulch are drifting away to try their luck in some
likelier vicinity; and both Father and Michael feel
sure that sooner than many expect, the town will
take on a forlorn and sad appearance, like the aban-
doned farms one sometimes sees on a New England
hillside.
"Yet whatever happens, we shall keep this cabin,
for as it was our first dear home, neither Michael
nor I can bear to part with it; and if the time comes
when Father's claim can be worked again, we may
be glad to return to its comfortable shelter for the
summer months. But already we are planning a move
to Denver City, where life will seem less lonely after
the folks depart, and where Michael has expectations
of securing a position with the same company he has
worked for here.
"It is, I fear, a disappointment to Father that we
shall not accompany them to Massachusetts; but if
we are to have a permanent home in this new Terri-
tory-if we are (as I hope) to blaze the trail for
others, and make it a safer, happier place for our
children to grow up in, we must not desert it while
it is in the making. A frontier country needs such
men as Michael; and, though it is so different from
his native England, he has grown to love its rugged
mountains, its bright sunshine and blue skies. And I
am proud to say that my husband is making a name
for himself among these pioneers, who at times have
not known where to turn for advice. Though he's
so young at heart and in years. Michael 'carries a
wise head on his shoulders,' as Grandma Aldenwould say; and people trust him instinctively. Even
the Indians, sensing, perhaps, his friendly spirit,
have shown no trace of suspicion or any outward
act of hostility in whatever dealings he has had with
them.
"It is comforting to think that my father and
mother will not be forced to endure the hardships
of the long, slow journey by ox team across the plains.
The Leavenworth and Pike's Peak stage-coach will
carry them from Denver City to Leavenworth in an
incredibly few days, which will be an adventure to
both my dear parents, for from there they go to
St. Louis for a visit with Mrs. MacTavish, who has
kept in touch with us by letter all these years, even
sending an exquisite embroidered dress for our small
Patricia!
"And while speaking of this delightful friend, I
must not neglect to set down the fact that her first
epistle gave news of the young couple whose baby
daughter we helped to bury on the plains, and whose
unknown fate caused us so many hours of anxiety.
It seems that, as we half suspected at the time, they
lost their courage after we left them, and, unable to
face the thought of crossing those great prairies with-
out their little one, had turned homeward, and were
overtaken by the MacTavish caravan, for their mules
were about done for and could make only a few miles
each day. In fact, it was an act of mercy to put the
suffering beasts out of their misery (which was done
by the kind hearted José whose voice was so decep-
tively rough), after which our generous friend pre-sented them with two fine, strong animals, and they
proceeded more happily on their journey.
"It is most interesting to pick up the threads of
these adventures which befell us on the prairie; and
a relief to know that these poor young people found
a friend to brighten the rest of their sad return. Mrs.
MacTavish declares that the lure of the plains still
tugs at her heartstrings, and that some day she hopes
to cross them again and make us a visit, which would
be a delight indeed.
"I am sad to report much Indian warfare during
the past year. The battle of Sand Creek, which took
place last November at sunrise, some miles south of
Colorado City, proved to be a veritable slaughter of
the savages, men, women and children, in which the
whites lost only ten men. This bloody fight has split
Colorado in two factions, some denouncing the Gen-
eral in command as 'bloodthirsty and treacherous,'
while others declare that he did only his duty. How-
ever one regards it, the attack was a terrible affair
which has driven the Indians to fresh outrages, and
in my heart I cannot blame them. Among other dep-
redations, they ripped up a mile or more of tele-
graph; and for months every road to the East was
blocked. Stage stations were burned. Flour rose to
the appalling price of fifty dollars a barrel; and I
have seen copies of the 'Rocky Mountain News' and
our own 'Weekly Miner's Register' printed on wrap-
ping paper!
"I confess that I dread having my parents set out
on their journey, for though times are said to bequieter along the route, I cannot help fearing for
their safety. But Father so longs for a sight of green
pastures, and brooks that are not discolored with
slag from the mines, not to mention the dear old
house where he was born, that Mother is determined
to risk the dangers for his sake; and I can only pray
that by the middle of July, the time set for their
departure, conditions will be better."
Despite this entry in Mary's journal, the Aldens
did not return to Massachusetts until another year;
for only a few days after the words were written, they
received a visit from Reverend Oldham, the preacher
from Iowa whom they met during their stop at Col-
orado City almost five years before. Since that meet-
ing this energetic parson (after making the sad dis-
covery that his brother came to a tragic death at
the hands of unfriendly Indians) had been to the
States and back again, and for three years had en-
gaged in a combination of mining and missionary
work in the vicinity of California Gulch, with con-
siderable profit to his pocketbook, and (it is to be
hoped!) to the souls who received spiritual aid at
his hands.
Having arrived at Denver on a matter of business,
and learning that his chance acquaintances, the
Aldens, were at Central City, he decided to drop in
on them for a short visit. It was this unexpected
arrival that changed their plans, for the good man
urged so strenuously that it was unwise to attempt a
journey back to the States until the Indians werequieter, that Luther was reluctantly persuaded
this was no time to go, and since he and Persis must
remain in the "calico cottage" for the present, the
young Warwicks postponed their move to Denver
for another year.
So it happened that before they turned their backs
on these mountain homes, Michael's prophecy was
to be fulfilled. Slowly, steadily, the once busy gulch
became a place of silent streets and deserted homes-
of cabins with ghostly, black, unlighted windows of
lonely stamp mills, their smokeless chimneys rising,
uselessly now, toward the blue sky. Even the "lady"
whose gay, swansdown-trimmed jacket had been a
part of Mary's wedding trousseau, lifted her bright
wings one morning, flitted away to fresher pastures,
and was seen no more.
But on a day in June of the next year, Mary War-
wick stood among a crowd of excited people assem-
bled to watch the departure of a Concord coach-
the stage that was to bear her parents on the first lap
of their journey toward the green hills of "home."
"It's only a visit. Remember that, dear child," said
Persis, holding her close. "We shall come back."
And Luther Alden, setting small Daniel Warwick
on his feet after a last hug, whispered huskily as he
kissed his daughter: "Don't cry, Mary. Nothing in all
the world is as dear to us as you and the babies. You
know that, don't you? We shall come back, unless
you come to us."
"Promise me, Father?" she asked, her sweet eyes
brimming."I promise," he told her, "and may God be with
you till we meet again."
Then quickly, too quickly, it seemed, amid a
cracking of whips, laughter, handshakes, kisses and
farewells, the coach was off-the crowd dispersing.
But Mary Warwick, unmindful of the glaring sun-
unmindful of everything save a great wave of home-
sickness, still stood in the suddenly deserted street,
a fluttering handkerchief held high above her head-
stood till the coach was lost to sight in a cloud of
dust, and Michael dropped his small son's hand to
touch her shoulder.
"Come, Mary. Come, my darling. We must go
home."L
CHAPTER XLI
OOKING back long after, Mary Warwick was
to realize that the next two years were a test
of the "pioneer spirit" which Daniel Alden had de-
clared was a part of both her mother and herself.
For following that uprising of the Indians, Denver
entered the most critical period of its whole history.
Mining in the mountains had for some time steadily
declined; and though there were farms in the river
bottoms, and in some of the high mountain parks,
agriculture had made no special strides, and in the
summer of 1867 another plague of grasshoppers
blasted the bright hopes of many a rancher.
So at this time a sense of discouragement and dis-
illusion was in the air which sapped the courage of
many less stout-hearted than the young Warwicks.
Then, too, disquieting news had come back to Mary
from the dear ones in far-off Massachusetts. The plan
had been that after a year at "home," Persis and
Luther would return to Colorado, perhaps to end
their days in that sunny land-for even love for an
old homestead and familiar ways cannot compete
with the love of children and grandchildren; and
was not their daughter's life to be lived in that
frontier country? But in the March following their
arrival in New England, Persis fell sick with pneu-monia, lying for days between life and death; and
when warm weather came again Luther wrote that
it was evident she had not regained sufficient strength
to undertake the long journey by stage-coach across
the plains.
Though the Warwicks could ill afford any extra
expense at this time, only the fact that Mary was
looking forward to her third confinement, deterred
her from going to Massachusetts, for she was torn
with anxiety for both parents, knowing full well how
helpless Luther would feel in such a crisis.
"The letters are so long in reaching us," she said
sadly to Michael. "If I could only know how they
are to-day!"
And the young husband, understanding her fears,
and sharing them, inwardly cursed the fact that the
cost of a telegram would be close onto ten dollars,
and its answer as much more, an impossible expendi-
ture just then with a new baby in prospect.
He said, after a long silence: "I've been thinking,
Mary-wondering if it's too much to expect of you-
settling out here so far away from your own people.
Even if they return, as I am sure they will when your
mother is stronger, your father will never feel really
at home away from the place where he was born.
Some of us are made that way, I suppose; and if you
want to go back-if you feel it's your duty to them,
we'll go as soon as this baby comes and you're able
to travel. You mustn't consider me for a single min-
ute, for if I've made good here I can do the same
somewhere else; and if you would be happier..."Michael hesitated. It was hard for him to make
such a proposition for he had taken root in the new
country, and his imagination was fired with the ro-
mance and beauty of helping to make it a place of
homes and safety. But Mary, loving him greatly,
understood.
"Don't you suppose I know what you dream of
doing, and how you'd choose if you hadn't me and
my people to think of?" she answered, a smile light-
ing her dark eyes. "But you're not to worry your
dear head another moment, Michael. Somehow I feel
that since we have put our shoulders to the wheel,
we must see this thing through. Too many faint-
hearted pioneers have deserted the city at the first
hint of depression; and those that are left need the
help of such men as you who have vision and
courage."
"But your father and mother"
"I know.” Mary spoke hurriedly, as if she
feared that should he press the point her defenses
might weaken. "I'm not saying it isn't hard to be so
far away from them; but if by another summer they
can't come back, Michael, we'll go home for a visit,
and Father can show you the farm he loves so dearly.
From now on we must save every penny with that
in view."
Michael Warwick was wont to say in later years,
that their son Luther, born during the next January,
brought them luck; for on the very day of his birth
a new smelting company opened for business at
Black Hawk. This method of extracting gold fromthe ore by fire (the result of patient experimenting
by Professor Hill of Rhode Island, who was to make
a fortune therefrom and pass to the United States
Senate) brought prosperity back to the paralyzed
mountain camps. Shafts opened again, and mills be-
gan to clang. And on a July afternoon of that year,
when Persis Alden, sitting in the shade of a century-
old elm tree, was writing her daughter that if all
went well they would leave for Colorado within the
month-that (though she feared it almost broke his
heart) Luther had decided definitely to part with
the old homestead, she looked up in alarm to see a
one-time schoolmate, Ed Wheeler, who did duty as
station agent and telegraph operator in the village,
driving into the yard at a gallop.
"Read this, Persis!" he shouted, jumping over the
wheel of the buggy almost before his winded mare
had come to a stop. "It's a telegram clear from Col-
orado, and such a thing ain't happened at any depot
this side o' Worcester, or maybe Springfield for all
I know. Read it, and tell me if it means that"
Persis had risen, her knees as weak as water.
"Bad news, Ed?" she questioned, for she could
imagine no other reason for such extravagance.
"Gosh, no!" ejaculated her old friend, handing out
a yellow missive. "Read what it says, Persis. Don't
stand there gapin'. I gotter get back before the up
train comes in and I want you should tell me '
But the exasperating woman appeared deaf to his
demands. With shaking hands she opened the enve-
lope to read:Rich vein discovered and being worked successfully in
the Persis-Mary. No need to sell farm. Start soon as possi-
ble and travel with every comfort. Can hardly wait to see
you. Dearest love from Michael, Mary, Dan, Patricia and
Baby Luther.
The words blurring before her eyes, Persis sank
limply down in the chair she had vacated a moment
since; while the now frantic station agent cried out:
"For gosh sake, Persis Alden, why don't you speak?
Didn't you hear me say I gotter get back to the
depot in fifty-eight and a half minutes? Be you all
rich? What'd you think I drove way up here for
instead o' sending the message by Benjy Bean when
he comes past at supper time? I never see such a
telegram-not once in the fifteen years that I been
op'rator. I ain't had time to count up what it cost;
but why in Sam Hill didn't they just say love from
all instead o' using that string o' names-at sixty-
three cents each? Come now, speak up! I wanter
spread the news down ter the village. Have you an'
Luther struck it rich in that fur-off country?"
This message from Colorado was Balm of Gilead
to Luther Alden, not because of the possible wealth
implied, but because it meant that the old home-
stead would stay in the Alden family, and that some
day he might lead his grandchildren about the farm-
show them the see-saw under the apple trees which
he had built for Mary-the brook running through
the meadow along whose banks their mother had
gathered cowslips and white violets in springtime-
the huge barn with its lofty hay rack where he him-self had played at Indians when a little boy-the big
old attic-the room where their mother, their grand-
father and great-grandfather had been born, and
where-God willing-a child of Mary's might be
born some day.
"He's like a different man," wrote Persis, finish-
ing the letter which the momentous telegram had
interrupted. "Now that he knows we can come back
to dear New England when the spirit moves us, all
sadness in our departure has vanished. He's as eager
as I am to be off, and longs to hold his precious
namesake in his arms. As for myself, dear child, I
can scarcely believe all your telegram implies. Ed
Wheeler was much shocked by its reckless extrava-
gance, I fear, and appeared enormously relieved
when your father responded in ten words! Yet even
that, I'll admit, seemed a tremendous outlay of
money to me; but we knew you would want to be
sure we had received and were rejoicing at the good
news."
It was on a bright, bright day some eight weeks
after Persis mailed this letter, when a Concord coach,
rocking gently on its heavy leather straps, came into
view far down the dusty Denver street, to meet the
usual excitement and the usual crowd. Watching that
jostling, good-natured throng, one would never sus-
pect that within the month it has been crying for
vengeance after more Indian outrages on ranches not
far from the town itself, for it is a cheerful throng
to all appearances.On one corner stands a cultured banker from
Pennsylvania in buckskin and corduroy talking with
a jolly half-breed Mexican, who, having recently ac-
quired a fortune in the mines, is attired in new
clothes from head to foot. Close by is a one-time
Kansas Border Ruffian and two squaws with black-
eyed papooses peering over their shoulders, followed
by a half dozen Indian children sans clothing of any
description! Near the doorway of the stage office
lounges a veteran of the war in shabby uniform, one
coat sleeve empty-an ex-convict from Missouri-a
Methodist parson-good Father Machebeuf, who
conducted the first Catholic service in Denver City
on Christmas night in the year of 1860, and who
smiles as he catches sight of the whole Warwick fam-
ily, for only the day before Michael had told him
that his wife's people were arriving by the stage from
far New England.
And here it is the coach for which they waited-
its once gay paint now dusty with days of travel.
Pandemonium ensues now. Shouts. Greetings. Con-
fusion.
..
"There they are, Michael!" Mary pressed closer,
the baby in her arms while Michael held Patricia on
his shoulder. "There's Grandma, Danny!"
"Hi there, old man!" broke in a voice behind her.
"I see the redskins didn't get your scalp on this
trip!"
"Stand back, won't yer? How'm I to get this door
open?"
"Hello, Jo! How'd you leave the folks?""Stand back, I say!"
"Granpa! I see my granpa!" from small Daniel.
"Hello, Jim! Hello, Sally! How's Kansas?"
Then, suddenly, the stage door flew open, and
Persis Alden, neat and comely as if she had not
traveled two thousand miles, stepped down from the
still gently rocking vehicle to be greeted by an ardent
hug from Michael and passed on to Mary-to find
(without knowing how he got there!) that she was
holding her new grandchild in her arms, while Mary
crowded past a blanketed squaw to throw her arms
about her father.
And later:
...
"How the children have grown! I'd hardly know
our Danny!"
"They've been so well, Mother. Not a cold all last
winter. Oh, Michael, isn't it just wonderful to have
them back?"
And in a moment, from Persis:
"This glorious sunshine! I'd almost forgotten it,
hadn't you, Luther?"
But Luther Alden made no reply. His namesake
was cradled in his arms, and he paid no heed to such
an unimportant question-just raised his head to
announce proudly:
"He's the handsomest of them all! Look at those
eyes, Persis-that nose! What if he was born in the
new West instead of old New England? I believe I'd
recognize this baby anywhere. He's a true Alden!"L
CHAPTER XLII
ITTLE thought the youthful Mary Alden when
on an April evening in the year of 1860 she
opened good Parson Gilder's book to set down its
first entry: "Went 22 miles to Enfield where we are
passing the night at Cousin Hezekiah White's..."
that the last pages of this chronicle were to be writ-
ten more than a quarter of a century later, and two
thousand miles from that peaceful New England
village with its white church spire.
But as it happened, coming upon the old diary one
morning and noting the blank pages still left, Mary
realized that the time was ripe to fill them realized,
too, with a sense of pleasure in the coincidence, that
it was the anniversary of that long-gone day when
with her father and mother she started west in an
ox-drawn prairie schooner! What better time to set
down the high lights of those years that had slipped
by since in the log cabin at Central City she had
recorded the death of her Uncle Daniel—the end of
the Civil War and the bloody battle of Sand Creek?
Early spring though it was, the air was warm, and
carrying the book onto her front porch, Mary forgot
her good intentions for a moment as she watched her
father puttering about his lilac bushes across the
street. A fine looking old man was Luther Alden,thought his daughter fondly. Though his hair was
white, the burden of years had not bent his sturdy
shoulders; and he was younger in heart than on that
day so long ago when they set out on the far, far
trail across the plains. Luther's grandchildren had
seen to that!
Mary had only to close her eyes to see him as he
had looked that April morning, walking solemn-
faced beside the oxen as they descended the steep
hill to the village in the valley. Her mother, too,
had felt the solemnity of the occasion; but for the
girl of twenty there had been no regrets-only the
thrill of Adventure the longing to see some of the
wondrous sights her uncle had decribed.
...
Turning away, Mary sat down and dipped pen
into the ink, then paused, for her mother had come
out of the brick house opposite and was observing
the growth of a syringa a shoot from the old bush
back on the farm. Persis was sixty-six, but looked ten
years younger. She moved briskly, with energy, and
her laugh was a happy sound drifting across the street
as she spoke to Luther. Mary Warwick loved so well
to watch these two that she was forced to turn her
back upon them before opening the book to write:
"Denver, Colorado, April 5, 1888. It seems incred-
ible that more than two decades have slipped away
since I wrote the last words on these pages. Many a
time in the intervening years I have wished to record
some happening that seemed important; but my life
has been full and busy, and the days and weeks, even
the years, have flown past too rapidly."Often, sitting here on the veranda of my com-
fortable home, I wonder what my dear Uncle Daniel
is thinking if he can look down upon us from his
'Mansion in the Skies,' as he felt so sure of doing
when on his deathbed. Does he realize that this is
almost the very spot where stood the 'shack' which
some good gentleman from Boston loaned us during
our first brief stay at Denver? That this quiet street
of green lawns and shade trees was that rough, wild
place completely overrun with prairie dogs? (It
could not have been far off that Michael let loose
his mascot, 'Victoria,' to be adopted, I hope, by some
kind prairie dog mother!) Dear Uncle Dan, if you
can indeed look down as I write this, what changes
there are for you to see!
"To go back on the arrival of my parents by
stage-coach after more than two years spent at the
old farm in Massachusetts, we all returned to Cen-
tral City, where several more years drifted by, and
where our twins, Persis and Michael, Jr. were born.
This return to the mountains was deemed wise just
then because the Persis-Mary was yielding hand-
somely; and though my husband was obliged to
spend much time in Denver, he wished to keep an
eye on things at the mine as well. I was not sorry
to go back to our log cabin, but it was so much too
small for our enlarged family that we were forced
to add more rooms, making our former bedroom
with its big fireplace into a parlor, where many a
social gathering was held as the months passed, it
being the pleasantest meeting place in the gulch."These later years at Central City are among the
happiest of my memories, for the reopening of the
mines brought many congenial families into our
mountain town. Where at first we had been obliged
to depend for recreation on neighborly calls or the
few books at hand, we now had much fine music,
games, surprise parties, informal dinners and church
activities to add to our pleasure. And though honesty
compels me to add that saloons and gambling places
were numerous as in all mining camps, I should have
been sad when the time came to leave this place and
our delightful friends, but for the fact that we could
return to the cabin for a rest and change when we
felt the need.
"This we did often during the next few years, and
were among those who attended the opening of the
Teller House, a hotel costing sixty thousand dollars.
A clipping from the 'Central City Register,' which
I find saved among others, tells us that 'The parlors
are perfect marvels of elegance. All sleeping
rooms to the number of ninety are tastefully fitted
with all essential conveniences.
The majority
•
•
..
are without transoms. Guests may therefore lie
down to peaceful slumbers undisturbed by appre-
hensions of getting their heads blown off or valuables
lifted by burglars.'
"I remember that my husband found this hectic
description most amusing; but considered it lacking
in tact, since it was doubtless intended to lure
strangers to our vicinity!
"It is a miracle, however, that all this elegance wasnot destroyed when in 1874 a terrible fire (which
curiously enough started in the house of a Chinaman
who was burning incense to placate the fire god!)
almost completely razed the town. Every man in the
place turned out to fight it, but to no avail; and
through all that night one could see knots of sor-
rowing people huddled together on the mountain
sides, gazing in despair at the ruins of their homes.
Our own cabin, being far up the gulch was one of
the few places that escaped conflagration; and it does
credit to the courage of our inhabitants that the
town was so quickly restored in more enduring stone
and brick.
"It was a proud night for those of us who could
recall the hardships of early days in the gulch, when
in the spring of 1878 the doors of our beautiful
granite Opera House swung open to admit the
throng which had assembled to be present at its
Dedication, a throng which (to quote our paper)
'filled the House from Pit to Dome.' This theatre
was built by popular subscription, for after a charm-
ing amateur presentation of The Bohemian Girl a
year before, our City was famed throughout the state
for its musical talent; and on this opening night
we listened to a ‘Grand Concert' of instrumental and
vocal music from the works of Wagner, Verdi, Strauss
and others, a delightful performance which met the
enthusiasm it deserved.
"This theatre would grace many an Eastern city;
and as we took our places that night in the com-
fortable hickory chairs that fill the pit, we looked upat a ceiling exquisitely ornamented with drifting
clouds and jewel-like medallions. To quote another
treasured clipping from the 'Daily Evening Call'
describing the event:
"As the vast audience filed into the beautiful
theatre, an audience representing the wealth, beauty,
and intelligence of the mountain towns, many were
the expressions of delight which fell from the lips
of those who for the first time viewed what may be
looked upon as Central's pride. The beautiful fresco
work brought out in bold relief by the scintillations
of one hundred gas jets, the handsome drop curtain,
and the house filled to its utmost capacity with fair
women in costly dresses, and brave men, was a sight
seldom seen, and certainly not soon to be forgotten
in these mountain regions.'
"And again in the same issue our enterprising
young editor, Mr. Laird, remarks: 'We have now
the best Opera House in the state, the best hotel,
the best musical talent, the best mines, and the best
women.
.' Superlative praise indeed!
"That night was surely one to remember, though
perhaps I should confess that vanity played a part
in my enjoyment of this occasion, for I was thrilled
by my new evening gown. It was a lustrous rose
taffeta on which the garnet jewelry that had be-
longed to Michael's mother was most effective; and
my prejudiced husband declared me the handsomest
woman in the audience, which was as absurd, when
taken literally, as it was gratifying to me! Even my
dear mother, who has never grown accustomed tothe idea that she can spend money save to help
others, was moved to the extravagance of a new
black grenadine trimmed with deep fringe, its lace
fichu caught together with an exquisite pearl brooch,
the gift of my precious Michael. (I can hear her
now, saying in pleased reproof when he presented
it: 'Oh, Michael! You shouldn't have done this!')
"Since that amateur performance the stage at Cen-
tral City has been trod by many a notable. Edwin
Booth, Patti, Jenny Lind and others equally famous
have bowed to the applause of those dwellers in the
hills. Thus it is plain that by 1878 civilization had
reached even that high mountain camp; yet I must
not give the erroneous impression that the troubles
of our pioneers were over at that time, for the Vigi-
lantes still had work to do, and Indian warfare raged
at intervals. None of us will ever forget our horror
at the Meeker massacre, when the Utes attacked the
Indian Agency on White River, killed good Nathan
Meeker and carried his wife and daughter, as well as
the wife of the agency blacksmith into captivity.
"It was unfortunate that the Utes were not all
under the direct command of their great leader,
Ouray; and that during this uprising he was living
on the Uncompahgre River. Ouray was a wise and
honorable old man-a firm believer in peace; and
when news of this outrage reached his ears he not
only gave orders to cease fighting at once, but sent
his wife, Chipeta, on a long and dangerous ride
through a wild country to save her white sisters who
had been taken captive. I am indeed fortunate tohave heard the tale of that ride from her own brave
lips, and thus can quote with feeling a stanza from
the poem with which our talented young friend,
Eugene Field, sought to honor her memory:
'But give her a page in history, too,
Though she be rotting in humble shrouds,
And write on the whitest of God's white clouds
Chipeta's name in eternal blue.'
"Yet I find as I look back on these eventful years
that the horrors grow dim, and it is the hopeful hap-
penings which stand out clearest. Never shall I for-
get our thrill in the arrival of the first passenger
train from Cheyenne to Denver. This was in June
of 1870, and we all came down from Central City
to see the last spike driven (a silver spike from the
miners at Georgetown), and the first train pull in.
And only a few months later a Pullman palace car
arrived from Chicago. It was named Comanche, and
attracted much attention, not alone because of its
luxury and comfort, but because it was the invention
of our own George Pullman, once a resident of Cen-
tral City!
"I clearly recall the first time we crossed the plains
in one of these luxurious cars, and how, as I looked
out at the flying landscape, I remembered our long,
slow journey in the covered wagon; and how my
dream that a railroad might some day span those
vast prairies, had seemed almost too fantastic to voice.
Yet only two years after that first train reached Den-
ver, we were to see the track wind its tortuous wayup the hills to Black Hawk, though not until 1878,
two years after Colorado was admitted to Statehood,
did the chugging engines reach Central City.
"There is no space on these pages to tell of all the
ups and downs to which our own family has been
subjected during the last twenty years. Suffice it to
say that the Persis-Mary was not one of the richest
lodes by any means. But, at least, it gave my father
the assurance of a comfortable old age, and the cer-
tainty that he and Mother could spend their sum-
mers at the homestead in New England, if they so
willed. This they have often done, accompanied since
his sixth birthday by our son Luther, who is his
grandfather's shadow and devoted comrade. Not in-
frequently the other youngsters have gone too, for
the farm was looked upon as a veritable fairyland
by all our children; and even now, when they have
left childhood behind, nothing pleases them better
than a visit to the old home where their grandfather
was born.
"Needless to relate, Michael and I have made many
trips to Massachusetts; and last year, accompanied by
Daniel and Patricia, we went to England for a long-
planned visit with Michael's brother, where we actu-
ally had a glimpse of Queen Victoria as she rode out
in state! I was obliged to pinch myself to make sure
I was the Mary Alden born on a farm in the New
England hills, who, as an imaginative little girl, had
dreamed of some day seeing Kings and Queens! Who
would have thought such things would come to pass?
"This record would surely lack completeness with-out brief mention of the discovery of silver at Lead-
ville, which meant so much to the state, and the few
months we spent there in '79. At this time the town's
most riotous days were over and it had taken on
something of a cosmopolitan air: dwelling houses in
place of shacks or tents, banks, business blocks, thea-
tres, and, of course, the inevitable saloons, gambling
dens and brothels which seem the accompaniment
of such a place. It was a hectic spot compared with
Denver; but three newspapers flourished, and there
was an almost innumerable number of social clubs
and 'Saturday night hops.'
"As for Denver itself, it would take more space
than this book allows to describe the changes our
city has undergone since that summer day in 1860
when my Uncle Daniel conducted us proudly about
its new-made streets. Now one sees a number of large,
comfortable homes surrounded by lawns and shade
trees, our own among them; and though to my Puri-
tanical taste some of these dwellings appear ornate,
with their jig-saw trimmings and high, narrow win-
dows, they are at least a sign of progress which is
encouraging to behold. And six years ago we held an
Industrial Exposition where were exhibited all Col-
orado's products.
"This exhibition fascinated our young people,
especially the three youngest who would have en-
joyed passing all their days on the grounds, and
who appeared to feel a genuine pride in the accom-
plishments of our pioneers. I am not sure but that
their father enjoyed it as much as they, though (Isuspect!) for different reasons. Medals were awarded
the best displays, and special prizes given, some of
which were so incongruous (such as a plush piano
stool for the best exhibition of silk worms, and an
accordion for the most lifelike portrait in crayons)
as to make my irrepressible husband collapse with
mirth. Indeed, my mother, who accompanied him on
his rounds during the first day, said afterward that
she was obliged to hurry him through the 'Art Gal-
lery' for fear he would disgrace them both, since the
place was haunted by the artists themselves, who
would have been deeply offended had they known
how my English husband regarded most of their
handiwork.
"In fact, though so highly respected and looked up
to in the community, Michael refuses to grow sedate
as the years pass, and can find only amusement in the
moral reform movements which spring up at inter-
vals. One of these is the Citizens' Protective Associa-
tion, its chief object, so far as we can discover, being
to prevent ladies from 'killing themselves with tight
lacing.' Other well-meaning agitators declare that
'cigarette smoking is fast becoming a national curse;'
and though out of respect for my father (who regards
tobacco in any form as the first step on the dark path
to ruin) Michael would never dream of lighting a
cigarette in his presence, I will admit that he is not
above enjoying a quiet smoke in our own back yard,
especially when the boys are not on hand to witness
his depravity!
"In outward appearance our city is quite metro-politan, with the Tabor Block on Larimer Street, the
Windsor Hotel (where one can indulge in a Turk-
ish bath if desired!); and last but far from least,
the Tabor Opera House, a gift from one of Lead-
ville's 'Carbonate Kings,' a five-story building which,
greatly to Michael's delight, Eugene Field describes
as 'modified Egyptian Moresque,' and which opened
with Emma Abbott in Lucia di Lammermoor. This
is a truly gorgeous theatre if one admires that sort
of thing, the Tabor box being lined with white
satin! But though all Denver's best were present on
the opening night, the wife of the donor, that kind
and estimable woman, Augusta Tabor, was notice-
ably absent, a fact much commented upon by her
friends until her husband's infatuation for 'Baby
Doe' became common gossip.
"This fine Opera House has attracted such artists
as Modjeska, Joe Jefferson, the charming young
actress, Minnie Maddern, and many another; so, al-
though we regret much in Horace Tabor's private
life, we may well feel grateful for his generous gift
to the city, which has brought such talent within
our reach.
"This has been especially appreciated by those of
us who have growing young people; and I recall with
amusement the astonished expression on the face of
Dan's Harvard roommate (with us for a visit, and
expecting an uncivilized community, I dare say) on
being told the day of his arrival that we had tickets
to see the English actor, Henry Irving, at the 'Tabor
Grand' that evening!"As I think back to the days at Central City, it
seems strange indeed that the penniless young couple
who started life together in a mountain cabin,
should, when the time arrived, be able to send their
boys to Harvard College. Surely we have much for
which to be thankful; and since our oldest son is
almost fanatical in his love for Colorado and all
things Western, it was Michael's wish that he spend
those formative years in an older part of the coun-
try, and would, not unnaturally, have liked the boy
to receive his higher education in England, had not
such a proceeding seemed unwise to me.
"Our son, Luther, however, has inherited his
grandfather's devotion to New England, and when
ready for college was so eager to follow in Dan's foot-
steps that we could not deny him, though the Uni-
versity of Denver is now a splendid institution, from
which our inseparable twins expect to graduate.
"As for Patricia (said jokingly to be my favorite
because she resembles her father so much more than
do any of the others), I am happy to relate that she
was married a year ago to a splendid young doctor,
and has a home of her own a few blocks off; while
if we can judge from where Daniel (now in business
with his father) spends most evenings, it will not be
long before we are asked to admit a new daughter
into the family circle, and one whom we shall be
glad to welcome.
"So the years drift by, and I come with reluctance
to my last page, at the end of which, though so much
is yet untold, I must write 'Finis.' Glancing at thefirst part of this record, I realize that the very maps
in the geographies have changed since we traveled
across the Kansas plains in our prairie schooner; and
there are moments when I wonder if we might not
have accomplished bigger things, had our family life
not taken so much time and energy. At this thought
a sense of sadness creeps over me; yet deep in my
heart I know it is a foolish sadness for after all,
what bigger thing could we do for this new country
than to leave behind us young lives to carry on? And
something tells me that the story of these eventful
years which I have written here, is only a prelude to
a greater story which our children will live.
"We, who went before them, have blazed the trail.
May they tread it worthily!"
It was with the regret of one who finishes a con-
genial task that Mary Warwick closed that ancient,
leather-bound book; but, lost to the present, she did
not hear approaching footsteps until Michael, smil-
ing down on her, said softly:
"Day dreaming, mavourneen?"
And at his voice Mary sprang up, her face alight,
one arm thrown out in a gesture that embraced not
only the city which they had helped to build, but
plains, and mountains, and gently flowing river.
"No, Michael," she answered, "not day dreaming.
Just thinking of all the dreams that we have seen
come true!"
For in those enchanted moments she had been a
little girl again, gazing far off beyond a green, greenvalley to a blue horizon-feeling the warm spring
sunshine against her face-watching high treetops
stirring in the west wind, and seeing visions-visions
of "rivers, and prairies, and shining mountains...."UNIVERSITY OF MICHIGAN
